🏗️ Category 1: Architecture20% · 40 Questions

1.1 Kubernetes Networking Fundamentals (Q1-Q10)

Architecture Q1 of 200
What are the four fundamental Kubernetes networking rules that every CNI plugin must satisfy?
A) Each Pod gets its own IP. Pods can communicate across nodes without NAT. Node agents can communicate with all pods on that node. Pods communicate without NAT between each other.
B) All pods share the node IP. Pods communicate through NAT. Services use iptables. DNS is optional.
C) Pods use host networking by default. CNI creates overlay networks only. Containers share pod IP.
D) Each container gets a unique IP. Pods on different nodes always need NAT. Services replace DNS.
✅ Correct Answer: A

The four fundamental rules: (1) Every pod gets its own unique IP address, (2) Pods on a node can communicate with all pods on all nodes without NAT, (3) Agents on a node (like kubelet) can communicate with all pods on that node, (4) Pods can communicate with each other without Network Address Translation.

📝 Detailed Explanation
The 4 Fundamental K8s Networking Rules
  RULE 1                    RULE 2                    RULE 3                    RULE 4
  ┌─────────────────┐      ┌─────────────────┐      ┌─────────────────┐      ┌─────────────────┐
  │ Each Pod gets   │      │ Pods can talk   │      │ Node agents     │      │ No NAT between  │
  │ its OWN IP      │      │ across nodes    │      │ can reach all   │      │ pods            │
  │                 │      │ WITHOUT NAT     │      │ pods on node    │      │                 │
  │ Pod A: 10.0.1.5│      │ 10.0.1.5 ───►  │      │ kubelet ───►   │      │ 10.0.1.5 ───►  │
  │ Pod B: 10.0.1.6│      │   10.0.2.9     │      │   10.0.1.5     │      │   10.0.2.9     │
  │ Pod C: 10.0.2.9│      │ (same IP seen) │      │ (direct access)│      │ (source IP     │
  └─────────────────┘      └─────────────────┘      └─────────────────┘      │  preserved)    │
                                                                             └─────────────────┘
  ✅ Cilium: eBPF O(1) hash maps    ❌ Legacy: iptables O(n) rule walking

These rules are the foundation of Kubernetes networking. Rule 1 means each pod has a routable IP — unlike Docker where containers share the host IP. Rule 2 is WHY CNI plugins exist: they must set up routes so pod IPs are reachable cross-node. Rule 3 enables health checks and readiness probes. Rule 4 preserves source IPs for NetworkPolicy enforcement. Cilium implements all four using eBPF instead of iptables/overlays, providing O(1) performance at scale.

Architecture Q2 of 200
What is the primary role of a CNI (Container Network Interface) plugin in Kubernetes?
A) Managing container storage volumes and persistent volume claims
B) Allocating IP addresses to pods and setting up network routes so pods can communicate across nodes
C) Providing DNS resolution for Kubernetes services and external domains
D) Managing authentication and RBAC for pod-to-pod communication
✅ Correct Answer: B

The CNI plugin's core job is IPAM (IP Address Management) — assigning each pod a unique IP — and establishing network routes so those IPs are reachable from any node in the cluster.

📝 Detailed Explanation

CNI is called by the kubelet at pod creation time (CMD_ADD) and deletion time (CMD_DEL). The CNI spec defines a simple JSON config file in /etc/cni/net.d/. When a pod is created, kubelet calls the CNI binary which: (1) allocates an IP from the pod CIDR range, (2) creates a veth pair connecting the pod namespace to the host, (3) configures routes so the pod IP is reachable. Cilium's CNI does this via eBPF — allocating IPs through its IPAM and creating eBPF-based routes instead of iptables rules.

Architecture Q3 of 200
How does a Kubernetes Service provide stable networking for pods that are constantly being created and destroyed?
A) Services create static IP addresses for each pod that never change
B) Services use DNS round-robin that updates instantly when pods change
C) Services provide a stable virtual IP (ClusterIP) and use label selectors to automatically discover matching pods, with kube-proxy or Cilium handling the traffic routing
D) Services create a dedicated proxy container for each application to handle routing
✅ Correct Answer: C

Services get a stable virtual IP (ClusterIP) that never changes, even as pods come and go. Label selectors match pods to the service, and kube-proxy (or Cilium's eBPF) handles the actual traffic routing to backend pods.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kubernetes Service — Stable VIP with Dynamic Backends
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Service: anihpj-api (ClusterIP: 10.96.100.50, Port: 8001)               │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  Label Selector: app=anihpj, tier=api                            │    │
  │  │                                                                  │    │
  │  │  ┌──────────────────┐  ┌──────────────────┐  ┌──────────────────┐│    │
  │  │  │ Backend Pod 1    │  │ Backend Pod 2    │  │ Backend Pod 3    ││    │
  │  │  │ IP: 10.0.1.10    │  │ IP: 10.0.2.15    │  │ IP: 10.0.3.20    ││    │
  │  │  │ Ready: ✅        │  │ Ready: ✅        │  │ Ready: ✅        ││    │
  │  │  └──────────────────┘  └──────────────────┘  └──────────────────┘│    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                          │
  │  Client (anihpj-web): curl http://anihpj-api:8001/api/jobs/              │
  │  DNS resolves anihpj-api → 10.96.100.50 (ClusterIP — NEVER changes!)     │
  │                                                                          │
  │  kube-proxy/Cilium KPR intercepts packet to 10.96.100.50:8001            │
  │  → Maglev hash(5-tuple) selects Backend Pod 2 (10.0.2.15)                │
  │  → DNAT: 10.96.100.50 → 10.0.2.15                                       │
  │  → Pod 2 processes request, responds directly                            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  
  ✅ Add/remove backends = just update label selectors, VIP stays the same!

A Service's ClusterIP is allocated from the service CIDR range and never changes. The service uses label selectors to dynamically discover backend pods. With Cilium KPR (kube-proxy replacement), eBPF programs intercept connect() syscalls and redirect to backend pods using Maglev consistent hashing — achieving O(1) lookup vs iptables' O(n) rule walking.

Architecture Q4 of 200
What is the difference between overlay networking and native routing in Kubernetes?
A) Overlay is for cloud only; native routing is for bare metal only
B) Overlay encapsulates pod traffic inside another protocol (VXLAN/Geneve) so the physical network doesn't need pod routes; native routing adds pod CIDR routes directly to the network
C) Overlay is faster than native routing; native routing is more secure than overlay
D) Overlay uses eBPF; native routing uses iptables. They cannot be used together.
✅ Correct Answer: B

Overlay encapsulates pod traffic inside VXLAN or Geneve tunnels, hiding pod IPs from the physical network. Native routing adds actual pod CIDR routes to the network's routing table so routers can forward pod traffic directly.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Overlay (VXLAN/Geneve): Pod packet is wrapped in another IP+UDP header. The outer header uses node IPs that the physical network understands. Advantage: works on any network without reconfiguration. Disadvantage: ~50 bytes overhead per packet, slight CPU cost for encap/decap. Native routing: Pod CIDR routes are added to the network (via BGP or static routes). Physical routers forward pod traffic directly. Advantage: no encapsulation overhead, better performance. Disadvantage: requires the network to know about pod IPs — usually needs BGP. Cilium supports both modes: tunnel mode (VXLAN/Geneve) and native routing mode (with or without BGP).

Architecture Q5 of 200
In a Kubernetes cluster with Cilium, how does a pod on node-1 (IP 10.0.1.5) send a packet to a pod on node-2 (IP 10.0.2.9) using VXLAN tunnel mode?
A) Pod-1 sends to node-2 IP directly. Node-2 NATs to pod-2 IP.
B) Pod-1 sends to 10.0.2.9. Cilium eBPF on node-1 encapsulates the packet in VXLAN with outer dst=node-2 IP. Node-2 Cilium decapsulates and delivers to pod-2.
C) Pod-1 sends to kube-proxy which creates an iptables DNAT rule to pod-2 IP.
D) Pod-1 sends to the service ClusterIP which then routes to pod-2 through the API server.
✅ Correct Answer: B

In VXLAN tunnel mode, Cilium's eBPF programs on the source node encapsulate the original packet inside a VXLAN header with the destination node's IP. The destination node decapsulates and delivers to the target pod.

📝 Detailed Explanation
VXLAN Tunnel — Step-by-Step Packet Walk
  STEP 1: Pod-1 sends packet           STEP 2: Cilium eBPF on node-1
  ┌────────────────────────┐           ┌──────────────────────────────┐
  │ anihpj-web pod         │           │ TC ingress on veth (host)    │
  │ eth0: 10.0.1.5         │           │ • Lookup dst 10.0.2.9        │
  │                        │           │ • Found: pod-2 on node-2     │
  │ Src: 10.0.1.5          │           │ • Node-2 IP: 192.168.1.2     │
  │ Dst: 10.0.2.9 ─────────┼──►        │ • Encapsulate in VXLAN!      │
  └────────────────────────┘           └──────────────┬───────────────┘
                                                      │
  STEP 3: Physical network routes     STEP 4: Cilium eBPF on node-2   STEP 5: Deliver
  ┌──────────────────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐
  │ VXLAN packet:                │    │ VXLAN interface (cilium_vxlan)│ │ anihpj-api   │
  │ Outer: 192.168.1.1→.1.2      │──► │ • Decapsulate VXLAN          │ │ pod          │
  │ Inner: 10.0.1.5→10.0.2.9     │    │ • Extract inner packet       │►│ eth0:10.0.2.9│
  │ UDP: 8472 (VXLAN port)       │    │ • Deliver to pod-2 veth      │ │ ✅ Received! │
  └──────────────────────────────┘    └──────────────────────────────┘ └──────────────┘
  
  ⚡ All in kernel via eBPF — no userspace proxying! < 1ms overhead.

The packet flow: (1) Pod-1 sends packet with src=10.0.1.5, dst=10.0.2.9 via its veth. (2) Cilium's eBPF TC program on the veth sees the packet and looks up the destination. (3) It finds pod-2 is on node-2 and encapsulates in VXLAN. (4) The physical network routes this to node-2. (5) Node-2 decapsulates and delivers to pod-2.

Architecture Q6 of 200
What is the pause container in a Kubernetes pod and why does it exist?
A) It pauses all containers in the pod when the pod is not in use to save resources
B) It's a minimal container that holds the network namespace (and PID namespace) for the pod, allowing all other containers in the pod to share the same network stack
C) It's a debugging container that pauses pod execution for troubleshooting
D) It's a temporary container created during pod initialization and removed once the pod is running
✅ Correct Answer: B

The pause container is the first container created in every pod. Its sole purpose is to hold the network namespace (and optionally PID namespace) that all other containers in the pod share.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Pause Container — Holding the Pod's Network Namespace
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                          POD: anihpj-web                          │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ┌────────────────┐  ┌────────────────┐  ┌────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ PAUSE CONTAINER│  │ DJANGO CONTAINER│  │ SIDECAR CONTAINER │  │
  │  │ (Created FIRST)│  │ (app code)     │  │ (logs/metrics)    │  │
  │  │                │  │                │  │                   │  │
  │  │ Holds:         │  │ Joins pause's: │  │ Joins pause's:    │  │
  │  │ ✅ Network NS  │  │ ✅ Network NS  │  │ ✅ Network NS     │  │
  │  │ ✅ PID NS      │  │ ✅ PID NS      │  │ ✅ PID NS         │  │
  │  │ IP: 10.0.1.5   │  │ Shares IP!     │  │ Shares IP!        │  │
  │  │ Just sleeps... │  │ localhost:8000 │  │ localhost:9090    │  │
  │  └────────────────┘  └────────────────┘  └────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                  │
  │  All containers share: same IP (10.0.1.5), same port space,      │
  │  same localhost, same network interfaces                         │
  │                                                                  │
  │  ⚠️ If pause container dies → entire pod loses network!          │
  │  📊 Cilium sees this pod as ONE endpoint regardless of container  │
  │     count (they all share the same network namespace)            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

In Kubernetes, all containers in a pod share the same network namespace. The pause container is created first and the CNI plugin sets up the network for this container. Then all other containers join its network namespace. In Cilium, each pod appears as ONE endpoint in `cilium endpoint list` regardless of how many containers it has.

Architecture Q7 of 200
How does CoreDNS provide service discovery in Kubernetes?
A) CoreDNS watches pod IPs and creates /etc/hosts entries in every pod
B) CoreDNS watches the Kubernetes API for Services and Pods, and responds to DNS queries with ClusterIPs for services and pod IPs for headless services
C) CoreDNS is an external DNS service that requires manual configuration for each service
D) CoreDNS replaces kube-proxy and handles all service traffic routing
✅ Correct Answer: B

CoreDNS watches the Kubernetes API server for Services and Pods, automatically creating DNS records. A service `anihpj-api` in namespace `anihpj` gets the FQDN `anihpj-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local` resolving to its ClusterIP.

📝 Detailed Explanation

CoreDNS runs as a Deployment in kube-system and uses the `kubernetes` plugin to watch the API server. For every Service, it creates: (1) An A/AAAA record for the service name → ClusterIP, (2) SRV records for named ports. For headless services (clusterIP: None), it returns pod IPs directly. DNS is critical — if CoreDNS is blocked by a NetworkPolicy, pods cannot resolve service names. Cilium can enforce DNS-based policies (toFQDNs) that integrate with CoreDNS responses for identity-aware filtering of external domains.

Architecture Q8 of 200
What is the pod CIDR range and how does it relate to pod IP assignment?
A) The pod CIDR is the range of IPs reserved for Kubernetes Services (ClusterIPs)
B) The pod CIDR is the IP range from which each node allocates IPs to pods running on that node. Each node gets a subset (/24 or /25) of the cluster's pod CIDR
C) The pod CIDR is a single /32 IP assigned to each pod by the kubelet at creation time
D) The pod CIDR only applies when using overlay networking; native routing doesn't use pod CIDRs
✅ Correct Answer: B

The cluster's pod CIDR (e.g., 10.0.0.0/16) is divided into smaller ranges — each node gets a /24 (10.0.1.0/24, 10.0.2.0/24, etc.). Pods on that node get IPs from that node's range.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Pod CIDR Allocation — Cluster → Node → Pod
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CLUSTER POD CIDR: 10.0.0.0/16 (65,536 IPs)                              │
  │                                                                          │
  │  ┌───────────────────────┐ ┌───────────────────────┐ ┌──────────────────┐│
  │  │ NODE-1                │ │ NODE-2                │ │ NODE-3           ││
  │  │ Allocated: 10.0.1.0/24│ │ Allocated: 10.0.2.0/24│ │ Allocated:       ││
  │  │ (254 usable IPs)      │ │ (254 usable IPs)      │ │ 10.0.3.0/24      ││
  │  │                       │ │                       │ │                  ││
  │  │ Pod A: 10.0.1.5       │ │ Pod D: 10.0.2.9       │ │ Pod G: 10.0.3.5  ││
  │  │ Pod B: 10.0.1.6       │ │ Pod E: 10.0.2.10      │ │ Pod H: 10.0.3.6  ││
  │  │ Pod C: 10.0.1.7       │ │ Pod F: 10.0.2.11      │ │ Pod I: 10.0.3.7  ││
  │  └───────────────────────┘ └───────────────────────┘ └──────────────────┘│
  │                                                                          │
  │  ⚠️ Pod CIDR (10.0.0.0/16) MUST NOT overlap with:                        │
  │     • Service CIDR (e.g., 10.96.0.0/12 for ClusterIPs)                   │
  │     • Node IP range (e.g., 192.168.1.0/24 for host network)              │
  │                                                                          │
  │  In Cilium: clusterPoolIPv4PodCIDRList: ["10.0.0.0/16"]                  │
  │  Operator allocates /24 per node, Agent assigns individual IPs to pods   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

In Cilium, the pod CIDR is configured via `clusterPoolIPv4PodCIDRList` (default: 10.0.0.0/8). Each node gets a smaller subnet. Cilium's IPAM assigns individual IPs to pods from the node's subnet. The pod CIDR must NOT overlap with the service CIDR or node IP range.

Architecture Q9 of 200
Why is iptables-based kube-proxy considered problematic at scale (5000+ services)?
A) iptables cannot handle UDP traffic, only TCP
B) iptables rules are evaluated sequentially (O(n) complexity) — with 5000 services, every packet may traverse 5000+ rules. Rule updates take 30+ seconds and connection tracking tables overflow
C) iptables requires a kernel module that is deprecated since Linux 5.0
D) iptables can only handle 100 services maximum before crashing the kube-proxy process
✅ Correct Answer: B

iptables walks rules sequentially — every packet must traverse all matching rules. With thousands of services, this becomes extremely slow (O(n) vs Cilium's O(1)). Rule updates are also expensive and cause connection tracking table pressure.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Aspectiptables kube-proxyCilium eBPF KPR
LookupO(n) — walks ALL rules sequentiallyO(1) — single hash map lookup
10k ServicesEvery packet traverses 10,000+ rulesSame single lookup regardless of count
Rule Updates30+ seconds to update iptablesInstant — atomic BPF map swap
Connection Trackingconntrack table overflow at scaleNo conntrack — identities handle state
Interception PointNetfilter (after TCP/IP stack)Socket layer (before TCP handshake)
PerformanceBaselineUp to 587x faster

Cilium's KPR solves all three problems: (1) eBPF hash maps provide O(1) backend lookup regardless of service count, (2) eBPF programs at the socket layer intercept connect() before the TCP stack — no iptables traversal, (3) Maglev consistent hashing ensures even backend distribution.

Architecture Q10 of 200
What is a veth (virtual ethernet) pair and how is it used in Kubernetes pod networking?
A) A veth pair is a virtual switch that connects multiple pods on the same node
B) A veth pair is a pair of connected virtual network interfaces — one end goes into the pod's network namespace (as eth0), the other stays in the host namespace. It acts like a virtual cable connecting the pod to the host
C) A veth pair is a virtual ethernet bridge used exclusively by the Docker runtime
D) A veth pair is a Kubernetes resource that defines virtual host-to-pod routing rules
✅ Correct Answer: B

A veth pair is like a virtual Ethernet cable — one end in the pod (`eth0`), the other end in the host namespace (named something like `lxcXXXX`). Packets sent through one end appear on the other.

📝 Detailed Explanation
veth Pair — Virtual Cable Between Pod and Host
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                         POD NAMESPACE                               │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  anihpj-web container                                       │    │
  │  │  App: curl http://anihpj-api:8001/api/jobs/                │    │
  │  │         │                                                   │    │
  │  │         ▼                                                   │    │
  │  │  ┌──────────┐     veth pair (virtual cable)                 │    │
  │  │  │  eth0    │════════════════════════════════╗              │    │
  │  │  │10.0.1.5  │                               ║              │    │
  │  │  └──────────┘                               ║              │    │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────╫──────────────┘    │
  └────────────────────────────────────────────────╫───────────────────┘
                                                    ║
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────╫──────────────────┐
  │                      HOST NAMESPACE              ║                  │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────╨─────────────┐   │
  │  │  lxc12345 (host end of veth)                                │   │
  │  │  • eBPF TC ingress: inspect every packet FROM pod           │   │
  │  │  • eBPF TC egress: inspect every packet TO pod              │   │
  │  │  • Policy enforcement happens HERE                          │   │
  │  │  • Hubble flow data captured HERE                           │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  
  View with: ip link show | grep lxc
  Cilium: cilium endpoint list (shows the veth-to-identity mapping)

When a pod is created, the CNI plugin: (1) Creates a veth pair, (2) Moves one end into the pod's network namespace as eth0, (3) Assigns the pod IP, (4) The host end gets a name like lxc12345. Cilium attaches eBPF programs to the host end of the veth for policy enforcement, load balancing, and observability.

1.2 Cilium Architecture & Components (Q11-Q20)

Architecture Q11 of 200
What are the two main components of Cilium and their roles?
A) Cilium Controller (manages nodes) and Cilium Proxy (handles L7 traffic)
B) Cilium Agent (DaemonSet on every node — manages eBPF programs, endpoints, policies) and Cilium Operator (Deployment — manages cluster-wide tasks like IPAM, identity GC, CES)
C) Cilium Scheduler (assigns pods to nodes) and Cilium Router (handles inter-node routing)
D) Cilium API Server (processes CRDs) and Cilium Kubelet (manages container lifecycle)
✅ Correct Answer: B

Cilium Agent runs as a DaemonSet on every node and manages eBPF programs, local endpoints, and policy enforcement. Cilium Operator is a cluster-wide Deployment handling IPAM, identity garbage collection, and CiliumEndpointSlice management.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Cilium Agent vs Operator — Responsibilities
  ┌──────────────────────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────────────────────┐
  │     CILIUM AGENT (DaemonSet)     │    │   CILIUM OPERATOR (Deployment)   │
  │     One pod per node             │    │   1-2 replicas, cluster-wide      │
  ├──────────────────────────────────┤    ├──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ ✅ Load eBPF programs            │    │ ✅ IPAM: allocate /24 to each     │
  │ ✅ Manage local CiliumEndpoints  │    │    node from cluster pod CIDR    │
  │ ✅ Enforce NetworkPolicy (CNP)   │    │ ✅ Identity GC: remove unused     │
  │ ✅ KPR service load balancing    │    │    CiliumIdentity CRDs           │
  │ ✅ Export Hubble flow events     │    │ ✅ CES: batch CEPs into slices    │
  │ ✅ WireGuard key management      │    │ ✅ Sync with external KV store    │
  │ ✅ Health checking               │    │ ✅ Node initialization            │
  ├──────────────────────────────────┤    ├──────────────────────────────────┤
  │ IF AGENT DIES:                   │    │ IF OPERATOR DIES:                │
  │ ⚠️ Node loses eBPF networking   │    │ ⚠️ New nodes can't get IPs       │
  │ ⚠️ Policies stop on that node   │    │ ⚠️ Identities accumulate (no GC) │
  │ ⚠️ Hubble data stops            │    │ ✅ Existing nodes work fine       │
  │ ✅ eBPF persists (bpffs pinned) │    │ ✅ Policies still enforced        │
  └──────────────────────────────────┘    └──────────────────────────────────┘

Agent (DaemonSet): One pod per node. Loads eBPF programs, manages CiliumEndpoints, enforces policies, handles service load balancing. Operator (Deployment): Handles cluster-wide tasks: IPAM allocation, identity garbage collection, CES management. If you kill the Operator, individual nodes still work but IPAM and identity cleanup stop. If you kill an Agent, that node loses eBPF-based networking.

Architecture Q12 of 200
How does Cilium's identity-based security model differ from traditional IP-based firewalling?
A) Cilium uses MAC addresses instead of IP addresses for policy enforcement
B) Cilium doesn't enforce security; it only provides observability
C) Cilium assigns each pod a numeric security identity based on its labels. Policies reference identities, not IPs — so if a pod restarts with a new IP, policies still apply correctly
D) Cilium uses the pod's UID from Kubernetes for all security decisions
✅ Correct Answer: C

Cilium assigns numeric identities (e.g., 12345) based on pod labels. Network policies reference these identities instead of IPs. If a pod restarts and gets a new IP, it keeps the same identity, and all policies continue to apply.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Identity-Based vs IP-Based Security
  IP-BASED (Traditional Firewall):              IDENTITY-BASED (Cilium):
  ┌─────────────────────────────────┐          ┌─────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Rule: allow 10.0.1.5 →         │          │ Rule: allow ID:12345(web) →      │
  │       10.0.2.9:5432            │          │       ID:56789(api):5432         │
  │                                 │          │                                 │
  │ Pod web restarts:               │          │ Pod web restarts:               │
  │ ❌ IP changes to 10.0.1.99     │          │ ✅ Same labels → same identity   │
  │ ❌ RULE BROKEN!                 │          │ ✅ RULE STILL WORKS!             │
  │ ❌ Must update firewall rules   │          │ ✅ No changes needed             │
  └─────────────────────────────────┘          └─────────────────────────────────┘
  
  Cilium Identity Mapping:
  Labels: {app: anihpj, tier: web}  →  Hash  →  Identity 12345 (numeric)
  Labels: {app: anihpj, tier: api}  →  Hash  →  Identity 56789 (numeric)
  
  Policies use ID numbers, not IPs. Kernel eBPF maps store ID→ID rules for O(1) lookup.

Traditional firewalls use IP-based rules. When a pod restarts, its IP changes, breaking the rules. Cilium's identity model: labels map to numeric identities. Policies reference identities, not IPs — if a pod restarts with a new IP, it keeps the same identity, and all policies continue to apply. This is the key architectural difference.

Architecture Q13 of 200
What CRDs (Custom Resource Definitions) does Cilium create when installed?
A) CiliumPod, CiliumService, CiliumNode, CiliumConfig
B) CiliumEndpoint (CEP), CiliumNode, CiliumIdentity, CiliumNetworkPolicy (CNP), CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy (CCNP), CiliumBGPPeeringPolicy, and more
C) CiliumDeployment, CiliumIngress, CiliumConfigMap, CiliumSecret
D) CiliumContainer, CiliumNetwork, CiliumFirewall, CiliumRoute
✅ Correct Answer: B

Cilium installs multiple CRDs including CiliumEndpoint (per-pod state), CiliumNode (per-node state), CiliumIdentity (security identities), CiliumNetworkPolicy and CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy (policy rules), and CiliumBGPPeeringPolicy (BGP configuration).

📝 Detailed Explanation

Key CRDs to know for CCA: (1) CiliumEndpoint (CEP) — represents a pod managed by Cilium. Contains networking state, identity, policy enforcement status. One CEP per pod. (2) CiliumNode — represents a node, contains IPAM allocation, encryption keys, tunnel endpoint. (3) CiliumIdentity — maps label sets to numeric identities. (4) CiliumNetworkPolicy (CNP) — namespaced policy CRD. (5) CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy (CCNP) — cluster-scoped policy for nodes and cluster-wide rules. Run `kubectl get crd | grep cilium` to see all installed CRDs.

Architecture Q14 of 200
What is the Cilium datapath and what are its main stages?
A) The datapath is the physical network cable connecting nodes
B) The datapath is the path a packet takes through Cilium's eBPF programs: ingress from veth → policy enforcement → identity lookup → forwarding decision → egress to destination
C) The datapath is a Kubernetes API path used by Cilium to read pod specs
D) The datapath is a YAML configuration file defining how Cilium routes traffic
✅ Correct Answer: B

The Cilium datapath refers to the sequence of eBPF programs a packet traverses: pre-filtering → policy enforcement at L3/L4 → identity resolution → L7 proxy (if needed) → forwarding → encapsulation (if tunneling) → physical NIC.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Cilium eBPF Datapath — Life of a Packet (anihpj-web → anihpj-api)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  POD (anihpj-web)                 HOST (Node-1)                   NETWORK     │
  │  ┌──────────┐     ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐            │
  │  │ App: curl│     │  ① TC INGRESS (veth lxc12345)               │            │
  │  │ → API   │     │  • Pre-filter: drop martians, invalid        │            │
  │  │         │     │  • Mark packet with pod identity (ID: 12345) │            │
  │  │  eth0   │═╗   │                                              │            │
  │  └──────────┘ ║   │  ② POLICY ENFORCEMENT                       │            │
  │               ║   │  • Check BPF map: ID 12345→ID 56789:8001   │            │
  │               ╠══►│  • Verdict: ALLOW ✓                         │            │
  │               ║   │                                              │            │
  │               ║   │  ③ L7 PROXY? (if HTTP CNP)                  │            │
  │               ║   │  • Redirect TCP to Envoy for HTTP inspect   │            │
  │               ║   │  • Envoy: GET /api/jobs → ALLOW             │            │
  │               ║   │                                              │            │
  │               ║   │  ④ FORWARDING DECISION                       │            │
  │               ║   │  • Lookup dst 10.0.2.9 → Node-2             │            │
  │               ║   │  • Encapsulate in VXLAN (if tunnel mode)    │            │
  │               ║   │                                              │            │
  │               ║   │  ⑤ TC EGRESS (eth0)                         │            │
  │               ║   │  • Egress policy check                      │            │
  │               ║   │  • WireGuard encrypt (if enabled) ──────────┼──► Internet │
  │               ║   └─────────────────────────────────────────────┘            │
  └───────────────╫──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                  ║
         Hubble captures flow at ② & ⑤ — before encryption!

The Cilium datapath stages: (1) Packet arrives at host veth from pod, (2) TC ingress eBPF program pre-filters and stamps identity, (3) Policy enforcement checks BPF maps, (4) L7 proxy redirection if HTTP/DNS policies exist, (5) Forwarding decision — local delivery or VXLAN encapsulation, (6) TC egress on physical NIC with egress policies and encryption. This all happens in kernel via eBPF — no userspace proxying except L7.

Architecture Q15 of 200
What happens when a Cilium agent restarts? Do existing connections survive?
A) All connections drop immediately and pods must restart
B) Existing connections survive because eBPF programs remain loaded in the kernel during agent restart. The agent reconciles state on restart — new policy changes are delayed until reconciliation completes
C) Connections survive but all network policies are temporarily disabled
D) The node must be cordoned and drained before the agent can restart
✅ Correct Answer: B

eBPF programs are pinned to the BPF filesystem (bpffs) and persist in the kernel even when the agent process restarts. Existing connections continue working. The agent reconciles state when it comes back up.

📝 Detailed Explanation

This is a critical design advantage of eBPF. When cilium-agent starts, it loads eBPF programs and pins them to /sys/fs/bpf/. These programs stay in the kernel until explicitly removed or the node reboots. During an agent restart: (1) eBPF programs keep running — existing connections are unaffected, (2) New policies won't be enforced until the agent reconnects to the API server, (3) New pods may not get endpoints until the agent is ready, (4) The agent reconciles all CiliumEndpoints, identities, and policies from the API server. This means rolling restarts of the Cilium DaemonSet are safe for production workloads.

Architecture Q16 of 200
What is the role of the Cilium CLI (`cilium` command)?
A) It's a replacement for kubectl that only works with Cilium-managed clusters
B) It provides installation, status checking, connectivity testing, and cluster management commands. It wraps Helm for installation and provides diagnostic commands like `cilium status`, `cilium connectivity test`, `cilium clustermesh`
C) It's the command run inside every Cilium agent pod to load eBPF programs
D) It's a debugging tool that only works when Cilium is in debug mode
✅ Correct Answer: B

The `cilium` CLI is a multi-purpose tool: install/upgrade/uninstall Cilium, check cluster status, run connectivity tests, manage Hubble, and configure Cluster Mesh. It uses Helm under the hood for installations.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Key CLI commands for CCA: `cilium install` (deploy Cilium), `cilium status` (health check), `cilium connectivity test` (end-to-end validation), `cilium upgrade` (update version), `cilium hubble enable/ui` (Hubble management), `cilium clustermesh enable/connect` (multi-cluster), `cilium config` (view ConfigMap). Don't confuse `cilium` (management CLI) with `cilium-dbg` (agent debugging command run inside the agent pod via kubectl exec).

Architecture Q17 of 200
How does Cilium Agent discover other nodes and their pod CIDRs?
A) Through a gossip protocol between agents on port 7946
B) Through the Kubernetes API — the agent watches CiliumNode CRDs which contain each node's pod CIDR, tunnel endpoint address, and health information
C) Through BGP route advertisements between nodes
D) Through etcd directly — each agent writes its information to a shared etcd key
✅ Correct Answer: B

Cilium uses the Kubernetes API as its source of truth. Each node's CiliumNode CRD contains its pod CIDR allocation, tunnel endpoint IP, encryption keys, and health status. All agents watch CiliumNode objects to learn about other nodes.

📝 Detailed Explanation

When a node joins the cluster, the Cilium Operator allocates a pod CIDR range to that node and creates/updates its CiliumNode CRD. Other agents watch for CiliumNode changes and automatically set up: (1) Routes to that node's pod CIDR, (2) VXLAN/Geneve tunnel to the node's tunnel endpoint, (3) Encryption keys for WireGuard/IPSec. This is why Cilium clusters converge so quickly — all state is in the API server, and agents react to CRD changes via watches.

Architecture Q18 of 200
What is Envoy's role in Cilium's architecture?
A) Envoy replaces the Cilium Agent for all networking functions
B) Cilium uses Envoy as an L7 proxy — when an L7 network policy (HTTP, DNS, Kafka, gRPC) is applied, Cilium's eBPF redirects matching traffic to Envoy for deep packet inspection, then Envoy either forwards or drops the traffic
C) Envoy is used exclusively for Hubble metrics aggregation
D) Envoy is an alternative to Cilium that runs as a sidecar proxy like Istio
✅ Correct Answer: B

Cilium embeds Envoy for L7 policy enforcement. When you create an HTTP-based CNP (e.g., "allow GET /api but deny POST /admin"), Cilium's eBPF redirects TCP connections to Envoy, which inspects the HTTP content and enforces the L7 rules.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Cilium + Envoy L7 — Sidecar-Free Deep Packet Inspection
  WITHOUT L7 POLICY (eBPF only):               WITH L7 POLICY (eBPF → Envoy):
  ┌─────────────────────────────┐             ┌─────────────────────────────────┐
  │ TCP: 10.0.1.5 → 10.0.2.9   │             │ TCP: 10.0.1.5 → Envoy (proxy)   │
  │ Port: 8001                  │             │                                 │
  │                             │             │ Envoy inspects:                 │
  │ eBPF: Allow (L3/L4 check)   │             │ ┌─────────────────────────────┐ │
  │ ✅ Forwarded                │             │ │ GET /api/jobs  → ALLOW ✓    │ │
  └─────────────────────────────┘             │ │ POST /admin    → DENY  ✗    │ │
                                               │ │ DELETE /api/.* → DENY  ✗   │ │
  KEY DIFFERENCE:                              │ └─────────────────────────────┘ │
  eBPF = L3/L4 only (IP + Port)                │                                 │
  Envoy = L7 (HTTP method, path, headers)      │ Envoy → 10.0.2.9:8001 (if OK)  │
                                               │ or RST (if denied)              │
  ⚡ Per-node Envoy (NOT per-pod sidecar!)     └─────────────────────────────────┘

eBPF operates at L3/L4 efficiently but cannot parse HTTP headers or DNS queries. For L7 policies, Cilium redirects traffic to a per-node Envoy instance (sidecar-free). Envoy terminates the TCP connection, inspects the application protocol, and either proxies (if allowed) or resets (if denied).

Architecture Q19 of 200
What is Hubble and how does it integrate with Cilium's architecture?
A) Hubble is a separate CNI plugin that runs alongside Cilium
B) Hubble is Cilium's observability layer. Each Cilium Agent exports eBPF flow events to Hubble Relay, which aggregates them cluster-wide. Hubble CLI and UI connect to Relay for real-time flow visibility
C) Hubble is a Kubernetes operator that replaces Prometheus for Cilium metrics
D) Hubble is the name of Cilium's eBPF verifier that validates programs before loading
✅ Correct Answer: B

Hubble is built into Cilium — each agent exports flow events from the eBPF ring buffer. Hubble Relay aggregates flows from all agents. CLI and UI connect to Relay for cluster-wide observability.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Hubble Architecture — Cluster-Wide Flow Visibility
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                         HUBBLE ARCHITECTURE                           │
  │                                                                      │
  │  NODE-1                     NODE-2                     NODE-3         │
  │  ┌──────────────────┐      ┌──────────────────┐      ┌──────────────┐│
  │  │ Cilium Agent      │      │ Cilium Agent      │      │ Cilium Agent  ││
  │  │ ┌──────────────┐  │      │ ┌──────────────┐  │      │ ┌──────────┐  ││
  │  │ │ eBPF Programs │  │      │ │ eBPF Programs │  │      │ │ eBPF     │  ││
  │  │ │ → Ring Buffer │  │      │ │ → Ring Buffer │  │      │ │ → Ring   │  ││
  │  │ └──────┬───────┘  │      │ └──────┬───────┘  │      │ └────┬─────┘  ││
  │  │        │          │      │        │          │      │      │       ││
  │  │ ┌──────▼───────┐  │      │ ┌──────▼───────┐  │      │ ┌────▼─────┐  ││
  │  │ │Hubble Server │  │      │ │Hubble Server │  │      │ │Hubble Svr│  ││
  │  │ │  (gRPC)      │──┼──────┼─│  (gRPC)      │──┼──────┼─│ (gRPC)   │  ││
  │  │ └──────────────┘  │      │ └──────────────┘  │      │ └──────────┘  ││
  │  └──────────────────┘      └──────────────────┘      └──────────────┘│
  │           │                         │                       │        │
  │           └─────────────────────────┼───────────────────────┘        │
  │                                     │                                │
  │                           ┌─────────▼─────────┐                      │
  │                           │   HUBBLE RELAY    │ ← Aggregates ALL     │
  │                           │   (Deployment)    │   flows cluster-wide │
  │                           └────┬─────────┬────┘                      │
  │                                │         │                           │
  │                        ┌───────▼──┐ ┌────▼───────┐                   │
  │                        │Hubble CLI│ │ Hubble UI  │                   │
  │                        │(observe) │ │ (browser)  │                   │
  │                        └──────────┘ └────────────┘                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Hubble is built into Cilium — each agent exports flow events from the eBPF ring buffer. Hubble Relay aggregates flows from all agents. CLI and UI connect to Relay for cluster-wide observability showing pod names, namespaces, identities, policy verdicts, and L7 details.

Architecture Q20 of 200
What is the purpose of Cilium's BPF Host Routing feature?
A) It routes traffic between BGP peers using BPF programs
B) It bypasses the host's network stack (iptables, netfilter) for pod-to-pod traffic on the same node, using an eBPF program to forward packets directly between veth interfaces — reducing latency and CPU overhead
C) It creates BPF programs that replace the Linux kernel's TCP/IP stack entirely
D) It routes host-level traffic to external networks through BPF-based NAT
✅ Correct Answer: B

BPF Host Routing uses an eBPF program to forward packets between pods on the same node, bypassing the Linux network stack (iptables, netfilter, routing tables). This significantly reduces latency.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Without BPF Host Routing: pod1 → veth → host namespace → iptables → routing decision → netfilter → veth → pod2 (many kernel subsystems involved). With BPF Host Routing: pod1 → veth → eBPF forward → veth → pod2 (single eBPF program does the forwarding). This eliminates the overhead of traversing the host's iptables rules, conntrack, and routing lookup. BPF Host Routing is required for BBR congestion control to work for pods — without it, packet socket association is lost in the host stack. Enable with: `--set bpf.hostRouting=true`.

1.3 Identity & Endpoint Management (Q21-Q30)

Architecture Q21 of 200
How are Cilium security identities assigned to pods?
A) Identities are random UUIDs generated by the Cilium Agent at pod startup
B) Identities are derived from the set of Kubernetes labels on the pod. Pods with identical label sets share the same numeric identity. The identity is stored in a CiliumIdentity CRD
C) Identities are manually assigned by cluster administrators using `cilium identity create`
D) Identities are IP-based — each unique pod IP gets a unique identity number
✅ Correct Answer: B

Cilium hashes the pod's Kubernetes labels to generate a numeric identity. All pods with the same labels (e.g., `app=anihpj, tier=web`) share the same identity. The identity is persisted as a CiliumIdentity CRD.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Identity allocation: (1) Pod starts with labels `{app: anihpj, tier: web}`, (2) Cilium Agent computes a hash of these labels, (3) Agent checks if a CiliumIdentity CRD with this hash exists, (4) If not, Agent creates one and gets back a numeric ID (e.g., 56789), (5) This identity is stored in the CiliumEndpoint and used for all policy decisions. Reserved identities: 1=host, 2=world, 3=cluster, 4=health, etc. Identity GC: Cilium Operator periodically removes unused identities that have no active endpoints.

Architecture Q22 of 200
What is a CiliumEndpoint (CEP) and what information does it contain?
A) A CEP is a Kubernetes Service endpoint used for load balancing
B) A CEP is a CRD representing a pod managed by Cilium. It contains the pod's networking state: IP addresses, security identity, policy enforcement status, and eBPF program state
C) A CEP is a configuration file for setting up Cilium endpoints on a new node
D) A CEP is the API endpoint used by Hubble to query flow data
✅ Correct Answer: B

A CiliumEndpoint (CEP) is created for every pod managed by Cilium. It stores the pod's IP (IPv4 + IPv6), security identity ID, labels, policy enforcement status, and networking health.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Key CEP fields: `status.networking.addressing` (pod IPs), `status.identity.id` (numeric security identity), `status.identity.labels` (pod labels), `status.policy.ingress/enforcing` (whether ingress policies are enforced), `status.state` (ready/not-ready). CEPs are created by the Cilium Agent on the pod's node. Other agents watch CEPs to learn about remote endpoints. In large clusters, CiliumEndpointSlice (CES) batches CEPs into slices for better scalability. Check CEPs with: `kubectl get cep -n anihpj`.

Architecture Q23 of 200
What is CiliumEndpointSlice (CES) and why is it used?
A) CES is a Kubernetes EndpointSlice that Cilium manages for Service load balancing
B) CES is a CRD that batches multiple CiliumEndpoints into a single object to reduce API server load in large clusters (1000+ nodes). The Operator creates CES objects; agents watch CES instead of individual CEPs
C) CES is a CLI tool that slices Cilium log files for easier debugging
D) CES is the Cilium encryption slice that manages WireGuard key distribution
✅ Correct Answer: B

CES (CiliumEndpointSlice) is a beta feature that groups CEPs into slices. Instead of every agent watching 100k+ individual CEPs, they watch ~1k CES objects. This dramatically reduces API server load in large clusters.

📝 Detailed Explanation

CES is NOT related to Kubernetes EndpointSlice — they solve different problems. CES batches CiliumEndpoint objects for policy/routing decisions. K8s EndpointSlice batches Service backend endpoints for load balancing. CES migration: enable on Operator first, let it create all CES objects, then enable on agents. Incompatible with Egress Gateway. Key config: `--enable-cilium-endpoint-slice=true`. For priority namespaces: annotate with `cilium.io/ces-namespace=priority`.

Architecture Q24 of 200
What are Cilium's reserved identities and what are the most important ones?
A) Reserved identities are manually created by administrators for special workloads
B) Reserved identities are fixed numeric IDs for special entities: 1=host, 2=world, 3=cluster, 4=health, 5=init, 6=unmanaged. They are used in policies to reference these entities without knowing their IPs
C) Reserved identities are IP ranges reserved for Cilium internal services
D) Reserved identities are temporary IDs used during pod startup before the real identity is assigned
✅ Correct Answer: B

Cilium has predefined reserved identities: 1=host (the node itself), 2=world (anything outside the cluster), 3=cluster (all cluster endpoints), 4=health (health checking), 5=init, 6=unmanaged.

📝 Detailed Explanation

In policies, you use `fromEntities: [host, world, cluster]` instead of IP addresses. `host` (1) refers to the local node — used in Host Firewall policies. `world` (2) is everything outside the cluster — used for egress rules. `cluster` (3) is all pods/nodes within the cluster. `health` (4) is for Cilium health checks. These identities never change and are the same on every node. This is why you can write a policy saying "allow from cluster" without knowing any pod IPs.

Architecture Q25 of 200
How does Cilium handle endpoint lifecycle — from pod creation to pod deletion?
A) Cilium does not manage pod lifecycles; it only reacts to Kubernetes events
B) Pod created → kubelet calls Cilium CNI (CMD_ADD) → Cilium allocates IP, creates veth, loads eBPF programs, creates CEP → Pod running → Pod deleted → kubelet calls Cilium CNI (CMD_DEL) → Cilium removes eBPF programs, releases IP, deletes CEP
C) Cilium creates endpoints before pods are scheduled and destroys them after pod deletion
D) Endpoint lifecycle is managed entirely by the Cilium Operator, not the agent
✅ Correct Answer: B

Cilium hooks into the CNI lifecycle: CMD_ADD on pod creation (allocate IP, setup veth, load eBPF, create CEP), CMD_DEL on pod deletion (remove eBPF, release IP, delete CEP).

📝 Detailed Explanation

Complete lifecycle: (1) kubelet creates pod sandbox (pause container), (2) kubelet calls CNI CMD_ADD with pod info, (3) Cilium CNI allocates IP from node's pod CIDR, (4) Creates veth pair, moves one end to pod namespace as eth0, assigns IP, (5) Loads eBPF programs on the host veth end (TC ingress/egress), (6) Creates CiliumEndpoint CRD with identity and networking state, (7) Other agents detect new CEP and update their BPF maps for routing/policy, (8) On CMD_DEL: removes eBPF programs, releases IP, deletes CEP, (9) Identity may be GC'd later if no other endpoints use it.

Architecture Q26 of 200
What happens to security identities when all pods with those labels are deleted?
A) Identities are immediately deleted when the last pod with those labels is removed
B) Identities are garbage collected by the Cilium Operator after a grace period. The CiliumIdentity CRD remains until the Operator verifies no endpoints reference it
C) Identities are permanent and never deleted — they accumulate over time
D) Identities are deleted by the Cilium Agent on the node where the last pod ran
✅ Correct Answer: B

The Cilium Operator periodically scans for unused identities (no CEPs referencing them). After confirming they're unused, it deletes the CiliumIdentity CRD. This prevents premature deletion while new pods with the same labels are starting.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Identity GC process: (1) Operator lists all CiliumIdentity CRDs, (2) Checks if any CiliumEndpoint references each identity, (3) If no endpoints reference an identity for a configurable period (default: 15 minutes), (4) Operator deletes the CiliumIdentity CRD, (5) All agents remove the identity from their local BPF maps. This is important: if you have a Deployment scaled to 0, the identity stays alive for the GC period. If you scale back up within that window, new pods reuse the same identity — avoiding unnecessary churn.

Architecture Q27 of 200
How does Cilium ensure that a pod cannot spoof another pod's identity?
A) Cilium uses TLS certificates to authenticate pod identities
B) Cilium's eBPF programs on the host veth track which identity is assigned to each veth interface. When a packet arrives from a pod, the eBPF program looks up the source veth and assigns the identity — the pod cannot control this
C) Cilium uses port-based authentication — each identity uses a unique port range
D) Cilium relies on Kubernetes RBAC to prevent identity spoofing
✅ Correct Answer: B

Identity is enforced at the kernel level by eBPF. When a packet arrives from a pod's veth interface, the eBPF program knows exactly which endpoint (and thus which identity) that interface belongs to. The pod cannot fake its source identity — it's assigned by the kernel based on the veth.

📝 Detailed Explanation

This is one of Cilium's strongest security properties. The eBPF program attached to the host end of each pod's veth maintains a mapping: veth interface → endpoint ID → security identity. When a packet enters the host namespace from that veth, the eBPF TC ingress program stamps it with the pod's identity. The pod cannot modify this — it doesn't have access to the host veth or the eBPF programs. Even if a compromised pod crafts packets with fake source IPs, the identity is always correct because it comes from the veth-to-identity mapping, not from the packet headers. Only a root-equivalent host attacker can bypass this.

Architecture Q28 of 200
What is the CiliumNode CRD and what key information does it contain?
A) CiliumNode is a Kubernetes Node object with Cilium annotations
B) CiliumNode is a CRD representing a node managed by Cilium. It contains: allocated pod CIDRs, tunnel endpoint address, encryption keys (WireGuard public key), health check IPs, and IPAM state
C) CiliumNode is a YAML configuration file applied to each node at boot time
D) CiliumNode is a CLI command that shows Cilium's node-level statistics
✅ Correct Answer: B

CiliumNode stores per-node Cilium state: pod CIDRs allocated to the node, VXLAN/Geneve tunnel endpoint IP, WireGuard public key, IPAM pool status, and node health information.

📝 Detailed Explanation

One CiliumNode CRD exists per Kubernetes node. Key fields: `spec.ipam` (pod CIDRs allocated), `spec.encryption.key` (WireGuard public key), `spec.health` (node health IP), `status` (IPAM usage statistics). When you enable WireGuard, each node's public key is stored in its CiliumNode. Other agents read this to establish WireGuard tunnels. View with: `kubectl get ciliumnodes`. This CRD is the primary mechanism for node-to-node information sharing in Cilium.

Architecture Q29 of 200
How does Cilium's IPAM (IP Address Management) work in cluster-pool mode?
A) Each pod requests an IP from a central DHCP server managed by Cilium
B) Cilium Operator allocates a /24 subnet to each node from the cluster's pod CIDR. The Cilium Agent on each node then allocates individual IPs to pods from its node-local pool
C) Cilium uses the Kubernetes Node's podCIDR field and delegates IPAM to kube-controller-manager
D) Cilium assigns IPs sequentially from a flat pool without any per-node subdivision
✅ Correct Answer: B

In cluster-pool mode (default), the Operator allocates a /24 to each node. Each agent gets its own pool segment and assigns individual IPs to pods locally — no cross-node coordination needed for each pod creation.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Cilium supports multiple IPAM modes: (1) cluster-pool: Operator divides the cluster CIDR into per-node /24s. Simple, works anywhere. (2) kubernetes: Uses k8s Node's podCIDR field. (3) eni (AWS): Pods get VPC-native IPs from AWS ENIs. (4) azure: Pods get VNet IPs from Azure IPAM. (5) multi-pool: Multiple CIDR pools for different purposes. For CCA, know that cluster-pool is the default and creates a CiliumNode CRD per node with the allocated pool.

Architecture Q30 of 200
What is the purpose of the Cilium Identity CRD?
A) It stores user authentication credentials for accessing Cilium-managed services
B) It maps a set of Kubernetes labels to a numeric security identity. Multiple pods with the same labels share the same CiliumIdentity, enabling label-based policy enforcement
C) It stores TLS certificates for mTLS between Cilium agents
D) It identifies which Cilium version is running on each node
✅ Correct Answer: B

CiliumIdentity is the central CRD for identity-based security. It maps a label set (e.g., `{app: anihpj, tier: api}`) to a numeric ID (e.g., 56789). All pods with those labels share this identity.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Each unique label combination in the cluster gets one CiliumIdentity. View with: `kubectl get ciliumidentities`. The identity number is used in BPF maps for policy lookups — BPF maps use the numeric ID, not label strings, for O(1) performance. When you create a CNP that selects `app: anihpj`, Cilium resolves this to the numeric identity and programs the BPF policy maps with that number. If a new pod starts with the same labels, it gets the same identity and all existing policies apply immediately.

1.4 Encryption & Security Architecture (Q31-Q40)

Architecture Q31 of 200
What encryption protocols does Cilium support for transparent node-to-node encryption?
A) TLS and mTLS only
B) WireGuard (recommended, kernel 5.6+) and IPSec (widely compatible, hardware offload). Both are transparent — no application code changes needed
C) SSL/TLS and SSH tunneling
D) AES-NI encryption at the application layer using Envoy
✅ Correct Answer: B

Cilium supports WireGuard (modern, simple, ChaCha20-Poly1305) and IPSec (older, hardware offload support). Both encrypt all node-to-node traffic transparently.

📝 Detailed Explanation
FeatureWireGuardIPSec
Code Size~4,000 lines~400,000+ lines
EncryptionChaCha20-Poly1305AES-GCM (various)
Kernel5.6+ (built-in)Any (external module)
Overhead~3-5% throughput~5-15% (HW offload helps)
HW OffloadLimitedWide NIC support
Key MgmtAuto via CiliumNode CRDManual or external IKE daemon
Recommendation✅ Preferred for new deploysLegacy/hardware offload needs

Both are transparent — Cilium's eBPF programs intercept connections and encrypt before they leave the node. Applications connect normally with no changes. Enable with: `--set encryption.enabled=true --set encryption.type=wireguard`. Verify: `cilium encrypt status`.

Architecture Q32 of 200
How does Cilium manage WireGuard encryption keys across nodes?
A) Keys are manually distributed by the administrator to each node
B) Each node auto-generates a WireGuard key pair. The public key is stored in the node's CiliumNode CRD. All other agents read this CRD and automatically establish tunnels. Key rotation is done via `cilium encrypt flush`
C) Keys are stored in etcd and distributed via the Cilium Operator
D) Cilium uses a single cluster-wide pre-shared key stored in a Kubernetes Secret
✅ Correct Answer: B

Each node generates its own WireGuard key pair at startup. The public key is written to the node's CiliumNode CRD. All other agents watch CiliumNodes, extract public keys, and automatically configure WireGuard tunnels to every other node.

📝 Detailed Explanation

The key distribution flow: (1) Node-1 agent starts, generates key pair, writes public key to CiliumNode for node-1, (2) Node-2 agent watches CiliumNode, sees node-1's public key, (3) Node-2 agent automatically configures WireGuard with: `wg set cilium peer endpoint :51871 allowed-ips `, (4) Same process in reverse. Key rotation: `cilium encrypt flush` regenerates all keys and re-establishes tunnels. Existing connections may briefly pause but don't drop.

Architecture Q33 of 200
What is Cilium's Host Firewall and how does it differ from pod-level network policies?
A) Host Firewall is the same as pod policies but applied to hostNetwork pods
B) Host Firewall protects the Kubernetes NODE itself (SSH, kubelet, API server ports) using CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy with nodeSelector. Pod policies protect pod-to-pod traffic. Host Firewall uses the reserved:host identity (identity 1)
C) Host Firewall replaces iptables on the node; pod policies replace kube-proxy
D) Host Firewall is a separate Cilium component that runs as a DaemonSet alongside the agent
✅ Correct Answer: B

Host Firewall uses CCNP with nodeSelector to protect the node itself — restricting SSH (port 22), kubelet API (10250), and other node-level services. Pod policies protect pod-to-pod communication. The host endpoint always has identity 1 (reserved:host).

📝 Detailed Explanation

Key differences: (1) Host policies use CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy (CCNP), pod policies can use CNP or CCNP. (2) Host policies use `nodeSelector`, pod policies use `endpointSelector`. (3) Host endpoint always has identity `1` and label `reserved:host`. (4) Host policies apply to host-networking pods too. (5) Always use audit mode first for host policies — a bad policy can lock you out of the node. Emergency recovery: access node via SSH/console, `crictl exec` into cilium-agent, enable audit mode, delete bad CCNP.

Architecture Q34 of 200
What is Cilium's Threat Model and what methodology does it use?
A) Cilium uses OWASP Top 10 methodology to assess web application threats
B) Cilium uses STRIDE methodology (Spoofing, Tampering, Repudiation, Information Disclosure, Denial of Service, Elevation of Privilege) to analyze 8 attacker types placed at different parts of the deployment stack
C) Cilium uses MITRE ATT&CK framework to map Kubernetes threat actors
D) Cilium uses NIST Cybersecurity Framework to categorize security controls
✅ Correct Answer: B

Cilium's formal threat model uses STRIDE to analyze 8 attacker profiles: K8s Workload Attacker, Limited-privilege Host Attacker, Root-equivalent Host Attacker, MITM Attacker, Network Attacker, API Server Attacker, KV Store Attacker, Hubble Data Attacker.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Key findings: (1) Compromised pod without host access: Cilium fully constrains it via NetworkPolicy. (2) Root on node: Cilium CANNOT protect — attacker can disable eBPF. (3) MITM between nodes: without encryption, traffic is visible. Mitigation: WireGuard/IPSec. (4) API server access: attacker can modify CRDs, exec into Cilium pods → root on node. Mitigation: strict K8s RBAC. The Threat Model also provides 9 production hardening recommendations.

Architecture Q35 of 200
What happens to Hubble observability when WireGuard encryption is enabled?
A) Hubble can only see encrypted blobs and loses all L7 visibility
B) Hubble sees flows BEFORE encryption — all L7 visibility (HTTP methods, DNS queries, paths) is preserved. Encryption happens after Cilium's eBPF programs process the packet
C) Hubble must be configured with WireGuard keys to decrypt flows
D) Hubble is disabled when encryption is enabled to prevent information leakage
✅ Correct Answer: B

Hubble observes flows at the eBPF level, which is BEFORE encryption. When WireGuard encrypts the packet, Hubble has already captured all the L3/L4/L7 metadata. You get full visibility AND encryption simultaneously.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Hubble + Encryption — Visibility BEFORE Encryption
  PACKET PROCESSING ORDER (inside kernel):
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                     │
  │  ① Pod sends packet (plaintext HTTP GET /api/jobs)                  │
  │         │                                                           │
  │         ▼                                                           │
  │  ② eBPF POLICY ENFORCEMENT  ← Hubble captures: FORWARDED/DROPPED   │
  │     • Check identity, port, policy maps                             │
  │     • L7 inspection (if CNP with HTTP rules)                        │
  │         │                                                           │
  │         ▼                                                           │
  │  ③ eBPF FORWARDING DECISION ← Hubble captures: src/dst pod, IPs    │
  │     • Local or remote? Tunnel or native?                            │
  │     • Hubble flow event EXPORTED HERE (with ALL L7 metadata!)       │
  │         │                                                           │
  │         ▼                                                           │
  │  ④ WIREGUARD ENCRYPTION      ← AFTER Hubble has already seen it!   │
  │     • Encrypts the packet                                            │
  │     • Now it's an encrypted blob on the wire                        │
  │         │                                                           │
  │         ▼                                                           │
  │  ⑤ PHYSICAL NIC — encrypted packet leaves the node                 │
  │                                                                     │
  │  ✅ Result: Hubble sees real app traffic + WireGuard encrypts it    │
  │  ✅ tcpdump would only see encrypted blobs — Hubble sees the real   │
  │     HTTP methods, DNS queries, and pod identities                   │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

The eBPF processing order: packet arrives → policy enforcement → forwarding decision → encryption → physical NIC. Hubble taps into stages BEFORE encryption. This means Hubble shows the actual HTTP methods, DNS queries, and pod identities even when all traffic is WireGuard-encrypted on the wire.

Architecture Q36 of 200
What is the difference between CiliumNetworkPolicy (CNP) and CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy (CCNP)?
A) CNP is for L3 policies, CCNP is for L7 policies
B) CNP is namespaced (applies within a namespace), CCNP is cluster-scoped (applies across all namespaces or to nodes). CCNP is required for Host Firewall policies and cluster-wide rules
C) CNP is for IPv4, CCNP is for IPv6
D) CNP is the old API version; CCNP is the new replacement for all policies
✅ Correct Answer: B

CNP is namespaced — it only affects pods within its namespace. CCNP is cluster-scoped — it can apply across all namespaces or target nodes via nodeSelector. Host Firewall MUST use CCNP because nodes are cluster resources, not namespaced.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Key differences: CNP uses `endpointSelector` to match pods in the same namespace. CCNP can use `endpointSelector` across namespaces OR `nodeSelector` for nodes. CNP cannot have a `nodeSelector`. Use cases: CNP for per-namespace micro-segmentation (e.g., anihpj namespace policies). CCNP for: Host Firewall, cluster-wide security rules, policies spanning multiple namespaces. Both support the same rule types (L3, L4, L7, DNS, toFQDNs).

Architecture Q37 of 200
How does Cilium's Cluster Mesh achieve cross-cluster pod-to-pod connectivity?
A) Cluster Mesh creates a VPN tunnel between cluster API servers
B) Cluster Mesh establishes direct tunnels (VXLAN or native routing) between nodes in different clusters. Global identities ensure pods in one cluster can be identified and authorized in another cluster. etcd synchronizes identity and state between clusters
C) Cluster Mesh uses BGP to advertise pod routes between clusters over the internet
D) Cluster Mesh creates a shared Kubernetes Service that spans both clusters through the cloud provider
✅ Correct Answer: B

Cluster Mesh creates direct tunnels between nodes across clusters. A shared etcd (or KV store) synchronizes identities and state. Global identities allow pods to be recognized across clusters, and policies can reference pods in other clusters.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Cluster Mesh — Cross-Cluster Pod-to-Pod Connectivity
  ┌─────────────────────────────────┐       ┌─────────────────────────────────┐
  │     CLUSTER 1 (US-East)         │       │     CLUSTER 2 (EU-West)         │
  │     ID: 1, Name: us-east        │       │     ID: 2, Name: eu-west        │
  │                                 │       │                                 │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────┐  │       │  ┌───────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ anihpj-web (10.0.1.5)     │  │       │  │ anihpj-api (10.1.2.9)     │  │
  │  │ Identity: 12345@1         │  │       │  │ Identity: 56789@2         │  │
  │  │ (globally unique!)        │──┼───────┼──│ (globally unique!)        │  │
  │  └───────────────────────────┘  │       │  └───────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                 │       │                                 │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────┐  │       │  ┌───────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │ Cilium Agent              │  │       │  │ Cilium Agent              │  │
  │  │ → Establishes VXLAN       │══╪═══════╪══│ → Establishes VXLAN       │  │
  │  │   tunnel to node in       │  │       │  │   tunnel to node in       │  │
  │  │   Cluster 2               │  │       │  │   Cluster 1               │  │
  │  └───────────────────────────┘  │       │  └───────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                 │       │                                 │
  │  Shared etcd: synchronizes      │       │  Shared etcd: synchronizes      │
  │  identities, CiliumNodes,       │       │  identities, CiliumNodes,       │
  │  and global services            │       │  and global services            │
  └─────────────────────────────────┘       └─────────────────────────────────┘
  
  ✅ Pod IPs routed directly across clusters (no NAT, no gateways!)
  ✅ NetworkPolicies can reference identities from other clusters
  ✅ Global services: same VIP available in both clusters

Cluster Mesh creates direct tunnels between nodes across clusters. A shared etcd synchronizes identities and state. Global identities include cluster ID prefix to prevent collisions. A pod in cluster-1 can send traffic to a pod in cluster-2 using its pod IP directly — no NAT, no gateways.

Architecture Q38 of 200
What is Cilium's Egress Gateway and what problem does it solve?
A) It's an alternative to kube-proxy for handling egress service traffic
B) It ensures that traffic from specific pods exits the cluster through designated gateway nodes with a predictable source IP. This is essential for integrating with external firewalls, licensing servers, and legacy systems that require IP whitelisting
C) It's Cilium's implementation of Kubernetes Gateway API for HTTP routing
D) It routes all cluster egress through a single NAT gateway for cost optimization
✅ Correct Answer: B

Egress Gateway routes traffic from selected pods through specific gateway nodes with a fixed source IP — essential for external systems that use IP-based allowlisting (firewalls, payment processors, licensing).

📝 Detailed Explanation

Without Egress Gateway, pod egress traffic exits from whichever node the pod runs on, using that node's IP — which changes if the pod moves. With Egress Gateway: (1) You define a CiliumEgressGatewayPolicy selecting pods, (2) Specify gateway node(s) and egress IP, (3) Cilium tunnels selected pods' egress traffic to the gateway node, (4) Gateway node SNATs it to the configured egress IP, (5) External system sees a consistent IP. Limitation: Egress Gateway is incompatible with CiliumEndpointSlice (CES).

Architecture Q39 of 200
What kernel version is required for full Cilium functionality including WireGuard and BBR?
A) Kernel 4.15 or higher
B) Kernel 5.10+ for basic Cilium eBPF support, 5.6+ for WireGuard, and 5.18+ for BBR congestion control for pods
C) Kernel 6.1 or higher is required for all Cilium features
D) Any kernel version works; Cilium auto-detects and uses compatible features
✅ Correct Answer: B

The minimum kernel is 5.10 for basic Cilium functionality. WireGuard needs 5.6+. BBR congestion control for pods requires 5.18+ (older kernels lose packet timestamps when switching network namespaces).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Cilium Kernel Version Requirements
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    KERNEL VERSION REQUIREMENTS                        │
  │                                                                      │
  │  Kernel 4.x    │ Kernel 5.4     │ Kernel 5.6     │ Kernel 5.10+     │
  │  ──────────────┼────────────────┼────────────────┼────────────────── │
  │  ⚠️ Limited    │ ✅ CO-RE       │ ✅ WireGuard   │ ✅ Full Cilium   │
  │  ⚠️ No CO-RE   │   (with BTF)   │   (native)     │   eBPF support   │
  │  ⚠️ Old eBPF   │                │                │                  │
  │                │                │                │                  │
  │                                                        │           │
  │                                                Kernel 5.18+         │
  │                                                ───────────          │
  │                                                ✅ BBR for pods      │
  │                                                (timestamp fix)      │
  │                                                                      │
  │  RECOMMENDED: Kernel 5.15+ or 6.1+ for production                    │
  │  CHECK: uname -r | ls /sys/kernel/btf/vmlinux (BTF support)         │
  │                                                                      │
  │  CCA MEMORY AID:                                                     │
  │  • 5.10 = Minimum for Cilium                                         │
  │  • 5.6  = WireGuard native                                           │
  │  • 5.18 = BBR congestion control for pods                            │
  │  • 5.4  = CO-RE with BTF (Compile Once, Run Everywhere)             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Kernel requirements: 5.10+ for core eBPF features. 5.6+ for WireGuard. 5.18+ for BBR to work for pods (timestamp retention fix). CO-RE requires 5.4+ with BTF. For CCA, remember: 5.10 = minimum, 5.18 = BBR for pods, 5.6 = WireGuard.

Architecture Q40 of 200
What happens during a Cilium connectivity test and what does it validate?
A) It only tests if the Cilium agent pods can reach the API server
B) `cilium connectivity test` deploys test pods and validates: pod-to-pod (same node + cross-node), ClusterIP services, NodePort services, DNS resolution, NetworkPolicy enforcement (allow + deny + L7), Hubble (relay + UI + CLI), encryption (WireGuard), and performance (bandwidth + latency)
C) It runs a vulnerability scan against all Cilium components
D) It tests only the BGP peering and external networking functionality
✅ Correct Answer: B

`cilium connectivity test` is a comprehensive end-to-end test that validates every aspect of Cilium: pod-to-pod, Services, DNS, NetworkPolicy, Hubble, and encryption. It's the first thing to run after installation.

📝 Detailed Explanation

The connectivity test deploys several test pods and runs scenarios: (1) client-to-service: verifies ClusterIP routing, (2) pod-to-pod: same-node and cross-node, (3) pod-to-service: via ClusterIP, (4) pod-to-external: egress to internet, (5) DNS: resolution via CoreDNS, (6) networkpolicy: creates allow/deny rules and verifies enforcement, (7) L7 policy: HTTP path-based rules, (8) encryption: verifies WireGuard/IPSec if enabled, (9) Hubble: tests Relay and UI connectivity, (10) bandwidth: measures throughput. Run with `cilium connectivity test --flow-validation=disabled` if Hubble is not enabled.

🛡️ Category 2: Network Policy18% · 36 Questions

2.1 Kubernetes NetworkPolicy (Q41-Q48)

Network Policy Q41 of 200
What happens when NO NetworkPolicy exists in a namespace?
A) All traffic is blocked — pods cannot communicate at all
B) Only ingress traffic is blocked; egress is always allowed
C) All traffic is ALLOWED — pods communicate freely. NetworkPolicies are opt-in: you must create a policy to restrict traffic
D) Only traffic between namespaces is blocked; intra-namespace traffic allowed
✅ Correct Answer: C

By default, Kubernetes allows all pod-to-pod traffic. NetworkPolicies are opt-in — pods are unrestricted until you create a policy that selects them. Once selected, only traffic matching the policy rules is allowed (default-deny on selection).

📝 Detailed Explanation

This is critical: without any NetworkPolicy, every pod can reach every other pod on any port. Creating a NetworkPolicy that selects a pod IMMEDIATELY isolates it — the pod can only receive traffic allowed by the policy's ingress rules. For egress: once a policy selects a pod with policyTypes including Egress, the pod can only send traffic allowed by the egress rules. This is called "default-deny on selection" and is the foundation of Kubernetes network security.

Network Policy Q42 of 200
What three selectors can a Kubernetes NetworkPolicy use to match traffic sources/destinations?
A) podSelector only — you can only select pods by labels
B) podSelector (match pods by labels), namespaceSelector (match entire namespaces), and ipBlock (match CIDR ranges). These can be combined across separate from/to entries
C) podSelector, namespaceSelector, ipBlock, and nodeSelector — all four are standard
D) Only ipBlock — NetworkPolicy is purely IP-based
✅ Correct Answer: B

K8s NetworkPolicy supports podSelector (label match), namespaceSelector (namespace match), and ipBlock (CIDR match). These can be combined with AND logic within a rule.

📝 Detailed Explanation
NetworkPolicy Selector Combinations
  ingress:
  - from:
    - podSelector:        ← Matches pods by label in the SAME namespace
        matchLabels:
          app: web
    - namespaceSelector:  ← Matches ALL pods in matching namespaces
        matchLabels:
          environment: staging
    - podSelector: {...}  ← Combines: pods with label "app:web"
      namespaceSelector:   ← in namespaces with label "env:prod"
        matchLabels:
          env: prod
    - ipBlock:            ← Matches external IP ranges (standalone)
        cidr: 10.0.0.0/8
        except: ["10.0.1.0/24"]  ← Exclude specific subnet

Important: podSelector + namespaceSelector can be COMBINED (AND), but ipBlock is always standalone — you cannot mix ipBlock with podSelector in the same `from` entry.

Network Policy Q43 of 200
What happens when MULTIPLE NetworkPolicies select the same pod?
A) Only the most recently created policy applies (last-write-wins)
B) Only the most restrictive policy applies (intersection)
C) The policies conflict and none apply — an error is reported
D) Policies are UNIONED (combined additively) — a connection is allowed if ANY policy allows it. This means policies ADD permissions, they don't restrict each other
✅ Correct Answer: D

NetworkPolicies are unioned (additive). If Policy-A allows port 80 and Policy-B allows port 443, the pod can receive traffic on BOTH ports — not just the intersection.

📝 Detailed Explanation

This is a common exam trap. Unlike traditional firewalls (first-match-wins), Kubernetes NetworkPolicies are COMBINED. The effective policy for a pod = UNION of all policies selecting it. This means: (1) You can split policies by concern (database policy, monitoring policy, app policy), (2) Policies never "override" each other — they only add, (3) To achieve "most restrictive wins" you'd create a single policy with all rules. CiliumNetworkPolicy follows the same additive behavior.

Network Policy Q44 of 200
What does a "default-deny all ingress" policy look like?
A) A NetworkPolicy with podSelector: {} (matches ALL pods), policyTypes: [Ingress], and ZERO ingress rules. This blocks ALL inbound traffic to all pods in the namespace
B) A NetworkPolicy with podSelector: {} and an ingress rule with empty from field
C) Delete all NetworkPolicies — no policies = default-deny
D) Set namespace annotation networking.k8s.io/default-deny: true
✅ Correct Answer: A

A policy with empty podSelector `{}` and policyTypes including Ingress/Engress but NO rules = default-deny for that direction. Empty rules ≠ allow all; empty rules = block all.

📝 Detailed Explanation
YAML — Default Deny All Ingress
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata:
  name: default-deny-ingress
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  podSelector: {}      # Selects ALL pods in namespace
  policyTypes:
  - Ingress            # Only ingress is restricted
  # NO ingress rules → ALL inbound traffic blocked

After applying: no pod in anihpj namespace can receive ANY external connection. Then add specific allow policies for web→api, monitoring, etc. This is the "zero-trust" approach — deny everything, then explicitly allow what's needed.

Network Policy Q45 of 200
What is the #1 most common mistake when creating egress NetworkPolicies?
A) Using the wrong API version for the NetworkPolicy resource
B) Forgetting to allow DNS (UDP 53 to CoreDNS/kube-dns). When you restrict egress, pods can no longer resolve service names — breaking ALL service communication even if the service ports are allowed
C) Creating the policy in the wrong namespace
D) Using ipBlock instead of podSelector for pod-to-pod rules
✅ Correct Answer: B

DNS is the #1 forgotten rule. When you restrict egress, the pod can't resolve ANY service name — even services you've explicitly allowed — because DNS resolution fails first.

📝 Detailed Explanation
The DNS Trap — Why Services Break with Egress Policies
  ❌ BROKEN EGRESS POLICY:             ✅ FIXED EGRESS POLICY:
  egress:                              egress:
  - to:                                - to:          ← DNS FIRST!
    - podSelector:                         - namespaceSelector:
        matchLabels:                           matchLabels:
          app: api                               name: kube-system
    ports:                                  - podSelector:
    - port: 8001                                matchLabels:
      protocol: TCP                               k8s-app: kube-dns
                                              ports:
  Result: pod CANNOT resolve                 - port: 53
  "anihpj-api" because DNS (UDP 53)            protocol: UDP
  to kube-dns is BLOCKED!                   - to:          ← THEN app
                                              - podSelector:
  Even though port 8001 is allowed,              matchLabels:
  the pod doesn't know the IP to                  app: api
  connect to!                                 ports:
                                              - port: 8001
                                                protocol: TCP

Always add DNS rules FIRST. Debug: `hubble observe --verdict DROPPED --to-port 53` to confirm DNS is being blocked.
