Network Policy Q46 of 200
How does ipBlock work in NetworkPolicy and what is its key limitation?
A) ipBlock matches pod IPs only — it cannot match external IPs
B) ipBlock is namespace-scoped and only matches IPs within the same namespace
C) ipBlock matches CIDR ranges for external traffic. It CANNOT be combined with podSelector/namespaceSelector in the same `from` entry — it's standalone. Traffic via ipBlock is NOT subjected to pod identity checks
D) ipBlock requires CiliumNetworkPolicy — standard K8s NetworkPolicy doesn't support it
✅ Correct Answer: C

ipBlock is standalone — you can use ipBlock OR podSelector+namespaceSelector in a `from` entry, but never mix ipBlock with pod selectors. ipBlock traffic skips identity checks.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Structure rule: a single `from` entry contains EITHER `{podSelector, namespaceSelector}` OR `{ipBlock}`, never both. To allow both pod and IP-based sources, use TWO separate `from` entries. Cilium extends this with `fromCIDRSet` which adds `cidrGroupRef` for CiliumCIDRGroup integration and `except` for CIDR exclusions — enabling more sophisticated external traffic policies.

Network Policy Q47 of 200
When you create a NetworkPolicy allowing egress from pod-A to pod-B, what else is needed?
A) Pod-B must also have an INGRESS rule allowing traffic from pod-A. NetworkPolicy is DIRECTIONAL — egress from A does NOT automatically create ingress to B
B) Nothing — egress rules automatically create matching ingress rules (stateful)
C) A Service must exist between pod-A and pod-B for the policy to work
D) Both pods must be in the same namespace
✅ Correct Answer: A

NetworkPolicy is directional. Egress from A to B controls A's OUTBOUND. B's INBOUND is controlled by B's ingress policies. Both sides need rules for the connection to succeed.

📝 Detailed Explanation

However: once the TCP connection is established (SYN → SYN-ACK), return packets are tracked by conntrack and allowed through. The policy check happens at connection INITIATION time. For anihpj: if web needs to reach api, the simplest approach is to create an INGRESS policy on api allowing from web. Since web's egress is open by default (no egress policies selecting it), only api's ingress rule is needed. But if you add egress policies to web, you must explicitly allow egress to api.

Network Policy Q48 of 200
What is the difference between ingress and egress in NetworkPolicy?
A) Ingress controls pod-to-pod; egress controls pod-to-internet only
B) Ingress is for TCP; egress is for UDP traffic
C) Ingress is for north-south (external) traffic; egress is for east-west (internal)
D) Ingress controls traffic ENTERING the selected pods. Egress controls traffic LEAVING the selected pods. Both are independently configured via policyTypes. If not specified, that direction is unrestricted
✅ Correct Answer: D

Ingress = traffic coming INTO selected pods. Egress = traffic going OUT of selected pods. Both are optional — if omitted from policyTypes, that direction is fully open.

📝 Detailed Explanation

policyTypes: [Ingress] → only inbound restricted, outbound fully open. policyTypes: [Egress] → only outbound restricted, inbound fully open. policyTypes: [Ingress, Egress] → both restricted. For anihpj: a common pattern is ingress-only policies on api/db pods (control who can reach them) with no egress restrictions. If you add egress policies, always remember DNS first (UDP 53 to kube-dns)!

2.2 CiliumNetworkPolicy & CCNP (Q49-Q58)

Network Policy Q49 of 200
What is the key difference between CiliumNetworkPolicy (CNP) and CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy (CCNP)?
A) CNP is for L3 rules; CCNP is for L7 rules
B) CNP is the older API; CCNP is the replacement for all policies
C) CNP is NAMESPACED (applies within one namespace). CCNP is CLUSTER-SCOPED (applies across ALL namespaces and can target NODES via nodeSelector). Host Firewall MUST use CCNP
D) CNP is for IPv4; CCNP is for IPv6 and dual-stack
✅ Correct Answer: C

CNP = namespaced, CCNP = cluster-scoped. CCNP is the ONLY policy type that supports nodeSelector (required for Host Firewall). Both support L3/L4/L7 rules.

📝 Detailed Explanation
FeatureCiliumNetworkPolicyCiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy
ScopeSingle namespaceAll namespaces
nodeSelector❌ NOT supported✅ Supported
endpointSelectorWithin same namespaceAcross all namespaces
L7 Rules✅ HTTP, DNS, Kafka✅ Same L7 support
Best Useanihpj namespace policiesHost Firewall, cluster rules

Use CNP for per-namespace micro-segmentation. Use CCNP when you need cross-namespace policies OR node-level Host Firewall protection. Both support the same rich rule types.

Network Policy Q50 of 200
What are fromEntities/toEntities in CiliumNetworkPolicy?
A) They reference Kubernetes ServiceAccounts for identity-based access control
B) They reference Cilium's RESERVED IDENTITIES: host (1), world (2), cluster (3), health (4), etc. Allows policies to reference these special entities without knowing any IPs
C) They reference external cloud resources like AWS security groups by entity ID
D) They are a Cilium-specific replacement for podSelector (more efficient)
✅ Correct Answer: B

fromEntities/toEntities reference Cilium's reserved identities. `world` = everything outside the cluster, `cluster` = all cluster endpoints, `host` = the local node.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Use cases: `fromEntities: [world]` with toPorts allows external traffic to a specific port. `fromEntities: [cluster]` allows all in-cluster traffic without specifying individual pod selectors. `fromEntities: [host]` is used in Host Firewall CCNPs to allow traffic from the node itself. These are powerful abstractions — you don't need to know IPs. Reserved identities: 1=host, 2=world, 3=cluster, 4=health, 5=init, 6=unmanaged.

Network Policy Q51 of 200
How do you write a CNP allowing anihpj-web pods to reach anihpj-api pods on port 8001?
A) Create a CNP with endpointSelector matching api pods, ingress from endpoints matching web pods, toPorts: [{ports: [{port: "8001", protocol: TCP}]}]
B) Create a CNP with endpointSelector matching web pods, egress to api pods — ingress-only doesn't work
C) Two CNPs required: one for web egress AND one for api ingress
D) Use CCNP instead — CNP cannot do cross-tier communication
✅ Correct Answer: A

Create an INGRESS policy on the api pods. Since web pods have unrestricted egress by default, only the api side needs an ingress allow rule.

📝 Detailed Explanation
YAML — anihpj API Ingress CNP
apiVersion: cilium.io/v2
kind: CiliumNetworkPolicy
metadata:
  name: allow-web-to-api
  namespace: anihpj
spec:
  endpointSelector:
    matchLabels:
      app: anihpj
      tier: api
  ingress:
  - fromEndpoints:
    - matchLabels:
        app: anihpj
        tier: web
    toPorts:
    - ports:
      - port: "8001"
        protocol: TCP

This is the "destination-centric" approach — control access AT the resource being protected. web→api works because web has no egress restrictions and api allows ingress from web.

Network Policy Q52 of 200
What is CiliumCIDRGroup and how is it referenced in policies?
A) A CLI command grouping CIDRs for Hubble filtering
B) A replacement for ipBlock — same functionality, different syntax
C) A Cilium agent configuration for grouping node IPs
D) A CRD defining a NAMED GROUP of external CIDRs. Referenced via cidrGroupRef (by name) or cidrGroupSelector (by label). One identity per group = scalability + single update point
✅ Correct Answer: D

CCG centralizes CIDR management. Define once, reference everywhere via name or label. Change the CCG → all referencing policies update automatically. One identity per group saves BPF map space.

📝 Detailed Explanation
YAML — CIDRGroup Definition + Usage
# Define the group
apiVersion: cilium.io/v2alpha1
kind: CiliumCIDRGroup
metadata:
  name: office-vpn
spec:
  externalCIDRs: ["10.48.0.0/24", "10.16.0.0/24"]
---
# Reference in CNP
apiVersion: cilium.io/v2
kind: CiliumNetworkPolicy
spec:
  ingress:
  - fromCIDRSet:
    - cidrGroupRef: office-vpn     # By name
    # OR:
    - cidrGroupSelector:           # By label
        matchLabels:
          role: vpn

For anihpj: define office VPN ranges once as a CCG, reference in all policies that need VPN access. Add a new office subnet → update one CCG → all policies reflect it.

Network Policy Q53 of 200
What is Cilium's policy audit mode and when is it essential?
A) Audit mode doubles enforcement — applies rules twice for verification
B) Audit mode LOGS what WOULD be blocked without actually blocking. Shows "action audit" instead of "action deny." ESSENTIAL for Host Firewall — a bad host policy can lock you out of the node entirely
C) Audit mode automatically generates policy recommendations from observed traffic
D) Audit mode is a Kubernetes feature — Cilium doesn't have its own audit mode
✅ Correct Answer: B

Audit mode = log-only (no blocking). Mandatory for Host Firewall testing — a mistake can block the Cilium agent from the API server, preventing you from deleting the bad policy.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Enable: `cilium-dbg endpoint config PolicyAuditMode=Enabled` or in CNP YAML: `enableAudit: true`. Monitor: `hubble observe --verdict AUDIT` shows what WOULD be blocked. For Host Firewall: find host endpoint (identity 1), enable audit, verify all required traffic shows "allow," then disable audit. Emergency recovery from lockout: SSH to node → `crictl exec` into cilium-agent → enable audit → delete bad CCNP → restart.

Network Policy Q54 of 200
How does fromCIDRSet differ from standard ipBlock?
A) They are identical — just Cilium's naming convention
B) fromCIDRSet only works for egress; ipBlock only for ingress
C) fromCIDRSet adds cidrGroupRef/cidrGroupSelector (CiliumCIDRGroup integration) and supports the except field for excluding subnets. Standard ipBlock only supports cidr + basic except
D) fromCIDRSet uses eBPF enforcement; ipBlock uses iptables enforcement
✅ Correct Answer: C

fromCIDRSet is Cilium's enhanced CIDR selector with CCG integration. Key additions: cidrGroupRef (by name), cidrGroupSelector (by label), and except for exclusions.

📝 Detailed Explanation

fromCIDRSet supports: (1) Direct CIDR: `{cidr: "10.0.0.0/8"}`, (2) With except: `{cidr: "10.0.0.0/8", except: ["10.0.1.0/24"]}`, (3) CCG by name: `{cidrGroupRef: "vpn-ranges"}`, (4) CCG by label: `{cidrGroupSelector: {matchLabels: {role: vpn}}}`. Standard ipBlock only supports (1) and basic (2). This is a Cilium-specific extension not available in standard K8s NetworkPolicy.

Network Policy Q55 of 200
What happens when a CNP has a label selector that matches ZERO pods?
A) The CNP is created successfully but has NO EFFECT — it matches zero endpoints. Cilium logs a warning. Always verify selectors with kubectl get pods -l
B) Kubernetes rejects the CNP with a validation error
C) Cilium applies the policy to ALL pods as a safety measure
D) The CNP is automatically deleted after a timeout
✅ Correct Answer: A

The CNP exists but applies to nothing. Kubernetes accepts it (valid YAML), but Cilium can't enforce it on zero endpoints. Always verify: `kubectl get pods -l -n `.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Common selector mistakes: (1) Typo in label key (`tier` vs `teir`), (2) Missing namespace (CNP only applies within its namespace), (3) Using nodeSelector in CNP (only CCNP supports it), (4) Labels don't exist on pods but were expected. Use `cilium policy get -n ` to see which policies are active and `cilium endpoint list` to verify endpoint labels.

Network Policy Q56 of 200
How do you create a policy that applies to ALL pods in the anihpj namespace?
A) Use endpointSelector with matchLabels: {namespace: anihpj}
B) Use endpointSelector: {} — the empty selector matches ALL pods in the CNP's namespace. Same pattern as standard K8s NetworkPolicy
C) Use a CCNP with namespaceSelector — CNP cannot select all pods
D) Create individual CNPs for each pod in the namespace
✅ Correct Answer: B

`endpointSelector: {}` in a namespaced CNP = matches ALL pods in that namespace. This is the standard pattern for default-deny or baseline policies.

📝 Detailed Explanation

An empty selector `{}` means "match everything." In a CNP in namespace `anihpj` → matches all pods in anihpj. In a CCNP → matches all pods cluster-wide. Common pattern: create a baseline CNP with `endpointSelector: {}` and basic rules (allow DNS, allow monitoring), then add specific CNPs for app-level rules (web→api, api→db). Remember: policies are additive — the effective policy is the union.

Network Policy Q57 of 200
What is toServices in CiliumNetworkPolicy?
A) toServices is the same as toPorts — just a different name
B) toServices references external cloud services by ARN or URL
C) toServices references a Kubernetes Service by NAME and NAMESPACE. Cilium resolves the service to its ports automatically. More maintainable than hardcoding port numbers
D) toServices is deprecated — use toPorts instead
✅ Correct Answer: C

toServices lets you reference services semantically: `{k8sService: {namespace: anihpj, serviceName: anihpj-api}}`. Cilium auto-resolves ports, so if the service port changes, the policy still works.

📝 Detailed Explanation

This is more maintainable than hardcoding ports. If someone changes anihpj-api from port 8001 to 8002, toPorts-based policies break, but toServices-based policies adapt automatically. Requirement: the service must be managed by Cilium (KPR). This is a Cilium-specific extension to NetworkPolicy — standard K8s NetworkPolicy doesn't have this concept.

Network Policy Q58 of 200
How can CiliumNetworkPolicies be migrated between clusters (dev → staging → prod)?
A) Policies cannot be migrated — they're cluster-specific
B) Use cilium policy migrate --source dev --target prod
C) Copy-paste the YAML — but namespace names must match exactly
D) Export: `cilium policy get -n anihpj -o yaml > policies.yaml`. Clean up cluster-specific fields (uid, resourceVersion). Import: `kubectl apply -f policies.yaml` or `cilium policy import policies.yaml`
✅ Correct Answer: D

`cilium policy get -o yaml` exports policies. Remove uid/resourceVersion, then `kubectl apply` or `cilium policy import`. For CIDRGroups, also export/import those CRDs.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Best practice for GitOps: store CNP YAML in git, apply via CI/CD. This ensures dev/staging/prod have identical policies. The `cilium policy import` command validates policies before applying. If using CiliumCIDRGroup, remember those are separate CRDs that must also be migrated. Pro tip: use `--dry-run` with import to validate before applying.

2.3 Layer 7 Policies (Q59-Q68)

Network Policy Q59 of 200
What can an L7 HTTP policy filter that an L3/L4 policy cannot?
A) Source and destination IP addresses
B) HTTP method (GET/POST/DELETE), URL path (/api/*, /admin), headers (User-Agent, Authorization), and host — inspecting actual application-layer content
C) Bandwidth usage per connection
D) TCP flags (SYN, ACK, RST) and sequence numbers
✅ Correct Answer: B

L7 policies inspect HTTP method, path, headers, and host — application-layer content that L3/L4 cannot see. L3/L4 only sees IPs and ports.

📝 Detailed Explanation
L3/L4 vs L7 Policy Capabilities
  L3/L4 (eBPF kernel):              L7 (eBPF → Envoy proxy):
  ✅ Source/Dest IP                 ✅ Everything in L3/L4 PLUS:
  ✅ Source/Dest Port               ✅ HTTP Method (GET, POST, PUT, DELETE)
  ✅ Protocol (TCP/UDP)             ✅ HTTP Path (/api/jobs, /admin)
  ✅ Identity (label-based)         ✅ HTTP Headers (Authorization, User-Agent)
  ❌ HTTP content                   ✅ HTTP Host (virtual hosting)
  ❌ DNS queries                    ✅ DNS query names (matchName, matchPattern)
  ─────────────────                 ✅ Kafka topics, gRPC methods
  ⚡ Kernel-level, very fast         ⚠️ Userspace Envoy proxy, more CPU

For anihpj: "allow GET /api/jobs but deny POST /admin" requires L7 — both use port 8001, so L3/L4 can't distinguish them.

Network Policy Q60 of 200
How does Cilium technically enforce L7 HTTP policies?
A) eBPF redirects matching TCP connections to a per-NODE Envoy proxy (not per-pod sidecar). Envoy terminates TCP, inspects HTTP content, and either proxies or resets
B) eBPF programs parse HTTP headers directly in the kernel (eBPF can do L7)
C) Cilium injects an Envoy sidecar into each pod with L7 policies
D) L7 traffic is sent to an external WAF appliance for inspection
✅ Correct Answer: A

Cilium uses per-NODE Envoy (sidecar-free). eBPF cannot parse HTTP — it redirects TCP to Envoy which does the L7 inspection. No per-pod sidecar injection needed.

📝 Detailed Explanation

This is sidecar-free L7 enforcement — a key Cilium differentiator from Istio. Per-node Envoy: one Envoy process per node handles L7 inspection for ALL pods on that node. When an L7 policy is applied: (1) eBPF updates BPF maps to redirect matching traffic to Envoy, (2) Envoy terminates TCP and inspects the application protocol, (3) If allowed: Envoy proxies to the original destination, (4) If denied: Envoy resets the connection. Limitation: L7 policies are incompatible with Bandwidth Manager.

Network Policy Q61 of 200
How does toFQDNs work in Cilium and what is its security consideration?
A) toFQDNs blocks DNS queries for specific domains
B) toFQDNs watches DNS responses and dynamically allows the resolved IPs. Security: Cilium TRUSTS the DNS server — if DNS is compromised, an attacker can redirect allowed IPs. Always constrain the DNS trust boundary
C) toFQDNs statically resolves domain names once at policy creation time
D) toFQDNs requires manual IP updates whenever DNS changes
✅ Correct Answer: B

toFQDNs intercepts DNS responses and programs allowed IPs dynamically. Trust boundary: Cilium assumes DNS is honest. A malicious DNS server can poison policy decisions.

📝 Detailed Explanation

How it works: (1) CNP specifies `toFQDNs: [{matchName: "api.github.com"}]`, (2) Pod makes DNS query for api.github.com, (3) CoreDNS responds with 140.82.121.5, (4) Cilium's Envoy intercepts the DNS response, (5) Cilium adds 140.82.121.5 to the allowed egress IPs for that pod, (6) Pod can now reach the GitHub API. To secure: restrict DNS traffic to only cluster DNS service, use DNS L7 policies to control which domains pods can query.

Network Policy Q62 of 200
Which L7 protocols does Cilium support for deep packet inspection?
A) HTTP only — other protocols are handled at L4
B) HTTP and HTTPS — Cilium can decrypt TLS inline
C) HTTP, DNS, and ICMP
D) HTTP, DNS, Kafka, gRPC, and Elasticsearch via Envoy proxy. TLS visibility is also supported for inspecting encrypted connections
✅ Correct Answer: D

Cilium's Envoy proxy inspects HTTP (method/path/headers), DNS (matchName/matchPattern), Kafka (topic/API key), gRPC (method), and Elasticsearch at L7.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Each protocol has specific CNP rules: HTTP — `rules: {http: [{method: GET, path: "/api/.*"}]}`, DNS — `rules: {dns: [{matchName: "*.svc.cluster.local"}]}`, Kafka — `rules: {kafka: [{topic: "orders", apiKey: "produce"}]}`, gRPC — uses HTTP/2 rules. TLS visibility: Envoy terminates TLS, inspects the decrypted content, and re-encrypts to the backend — enabling L7 inspection of HTTPS traffic.

Network Policy Q63 of 200
Write a CNP that allows GET /api/jobs but denies POST/DELETE on the same port.
A) CNP on api pods with HTTP rules: [{method: GET, path: "/api/jobs.*"}]. Methods not listed (POST, DELETE, PUT) are automatically denied
B) Create two CNPs: one allowing GET, one denying POST — policies are combined
C) Cilium cannot filter by HTTP method — this requires a WAF
D) Use an egress policy on web pods blocking POST/DELETE methods
✅ Correct Answer: A

L7 HTTP rules are EXPLICIT — only listed methods/paths are allowed. If you only list GET, then POST/PUT/DELETE are all blocked by default.

📝 Detailed Explanation
YAML
ingress:
- fromEndpoints:
  - matchLabels: {app: anihpj, tier: web}
  toPorts:
  - ports: [{port: "8001", protocol: TCP}]
    rules:
      http:
      - method: GET
        path: "/api/jobs.*"
# POST, DELETE, PUT to /api/jobs → DENIED
# GET /api/jobs/123 → ALLOWED
# Any method to /admin → DENIED (no matching rule)

This provides API security without application changes. The Django app doesn't need to implement method-level authorization — Cilium's Envoy handles it at the network layer.

Network Policy Q64 of 200
What happens to L7 enforcement if the Envoy proxy on a node crashes?
A) All traffic is blocked — safety first
B) L7 rules are NOT enforced — traffic falls through to L3/L4 rules only. If only L7 rules exist (no L3/L4), connections fail. Cilium Agent restarts Envoy automatically
C) Cilium Agent takes over L7 inspection temporarily
D) Kubernetes restarts Envoy instantly with zero downtime
✅ Correct Answer: B

If Envoy crashes, L7 inspection stops. Traffic passes through L3/L4 only. Defense-in-depth: always have L3/L4 rules as baseline + L7 as additional layer.

📝 Detailed Explanation

If both L3 and L7 rules exist: traffic passes L3 check but L7 enforcement is missing → traffic may reach the backend without L7 filtering. If only L7 rules exist: proxy redirection fails → connection dropped. Best practice: layer L3 port restrictions + L7 HTTP filtering for defense-in-depth. Monitor Envoy health via Cilium metrics.

Network Policy Q65 of 200
What HTTP headers can be filtered in a Cilium L7 policy?
A) ANY HTTP header — User-Agent, Authorization, Content-Type, X-Custom-*, etc. Matched with exact, prefix, suffix, or regex patterns
B) Only standard headers: Host, User-Agent, and Accept
C) Only the Authorization header for JWT/bearer token validation
D) Headers cannot be filtered — only method and path are supported
✅ Correct Answer: A

Cilium supports matching ANY HTTP header with exact, prefix, suffix, or regex patterns. Common uses: User-Agent whitelisting, Authorization token validation, Content-Type restrictions.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Header matching: exact match (`X-Version: v1`), prefix (`X-Env: prod-*`), suffix (`*@company.com`), regex (`X-Token: [a-f0-9]{32}`). Example: `http: [{headers: ["Authorization: Bearer .*"], method: GET}]` — requires valid auth token for GET requests. This enables API security at the network layer without modifying application code.

Network Policy Q66 of 200
Does same-node traffic bypass L7 policies?
A) Yes — same-node traffic is short-circuited for performance
B) Only cross-node traffic goes through L7 inspection
C) NO — same-node traffic goes through the SAME L7 enforcement path: pod-A → eBPF redirect → Envoy proxy → pod-B. Network topology doesn't affect policy enforcement
D) Same-node requires a separate Envoy instance per pod pair
✅ Correct Answer: C

L7 enforcement is independent of network topology. Same-node or cross-node — the same eBPF redirect + Envoy inspection applies identically.

📝 Detailed Explanation

This is by design. Whether pod-A and pod-B are on the same node or different nodes, the enforcement path is identical: eBPF on the source veth → identity check → L7 redirect to Envoy → Envoy inspects → proxy to destination. This ensures consistent policy enforcement regardless of pod placement. Some CNIs have issues with same-node traffic bypassing policies — Cilium does not.

Network Policy Q67 of 200
What is the relationship between L7 policies and Bandwidth Manager?
A) They work together — L7 policies and bandwidth limits are complementary
B) They are INCOMPATIBLE — if an L7 policy selects a pod at egress, bandwidth enforcement is DISABLED for that pod. The Envoy proxy path bypasses EDT pacing
C) L7 policies can enforce bandwidth limits through HTTP rate limiting
D) Bandwidth Manager only works if L7 policies are also enabled
✅ Correct Answer: B

L7 policies and Bandwidth Manager are mutually exclusive. Proxy redirection to Envoy bypasses the EDT pacing mechanism, so bandwidth limits don't apply.

📝 Detailed Explanation

This is a critical CCA exam point. If you need both L7 filtering and bandwidth limits: choose one per pod. Options: (1) Use L3/L4 policies + Bandwidth Manager, (2) Use L7 policies but accept no bandwidth enforcement, (3) Split workloads — some pods get L7 protection, others get bandwidth limits. The incompatibility is architectural: EDT operates at the physical NIC, but Envoy proxy redirects traffic through userspace, bypassing the NIC-level pacing.

Network Policy Q68 of 200
What is the recommended debugging sequence when a CNP doesn't seem to be working?
A) 1) Verify selector: kubectl get pods -l 2) Hubble: hubble observe --verdict DROPPED 3) Policy trace: cilium policy trace --src-label/--dst-label 4) Endpoint inspect: cilium endpoint inspect
B) Delete and recreate the CNP — this fixes most selector issues
C) Restart all pods in the namespace — policies only apply to new connections
D) Check Cilium agent logs first, then restart the agent if needed
✅ Correct Answer: A

The systematic 4-step approach: verify selectors → check Hubble for drops → simulate with policy trace → inspect endpoint enforcement. Don't restart things randomly.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CNP Debugging Flow
  START: CNP "not working"
  │
  ├─ ① SELECTOR CHECK: kubectl get pods -l  -n 
  │   ├─ No pods → selector typo or wrong namespace → FIX
  │   └─ Pods found → Continue
  │
  ├─ ② HUBBLE: hubble observe -n  --verdict DROPPED
  │   ├─ DROPPED flows shown → Policy IS blocking → check which rule
  │   └─ No DROPPED → Issue is connectivity, not policy → check DNS, routes
  │
  ├─ ③ POLICY TRACE: cilium policy trace --src-label ... --dst-label ... --dport X
  │   └─ Shows expected verdict without sending real traffic
  │
  └─ ④ ENDPOINT INSPECT: cilium endpoint inspect 
      └─ Shows all enforced policies on that endpoint

Never restart pods as first step — that destroys evidence. Always diagnose with Hubble + policy trace.

2.4 Policy Debugging & Troubleshooting (Q69-Q76)

Network Policy Q69 of 200
What does `cilium policy trace` do?
A) Simulates traffic between endpoints and shows allowed/denied verdict WITHOUT sending packets. Uses labels or identities
B) Traces network path packets take through routers
C) Shows which policies were recently modified and by whom
D) Creates a tcpdump trace of policy-related events
✅ Correct Answer: A

Dry-run simulator: `cilium policy trace -n anihpj --src-label app=web --dst-label app=api --dport 8001` shows verdict without real traffic.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Policy trace uses labels or identity numbers to simulate traffic. Output: "Policy verdict: allowed" or "denied" with matching rule. Essential for pre-deployment policy validation — test before applying. Also useful when policies aren't behaving as expected.

Network Policy Q70 of 200
How do you see enforced policies on a specific endpoint?
A) kubectl describe pod — policies are in annotations
B) hubble observe --verdict FORWARDED
C) cilium endpoint inspect <id> — shows all enforced policies, identities, and L3/L4/L7 rules
D) cilium policy get --endpoint <pod>
✅ Correct Answer: C

`cilium endpoint inspect ` is the definitive way. Find endpoint ID with `cilium endpoint list`, then inspect for enforced policy details.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Shows: identity + labels, ingress/egress enforcement status (Enabled/Disabled/Audit), all L3/L4 rules, all L7 rules, and the union of all matching policies. This is more reliable than reading CNP YAMLs which might have selector mismatches.

Network Policy Q71 of 200
What command shows real-time policy verdicts with identity information?
A) kubectl logs -n kube-system ds/cilium | grep policy
B) cilium-dbg monitor --type policy-verdict — shows every policy decision with identities, ports, and verdict (allow/deny/audit) in real-time
C) hubble observe --type policy
D) cilium policy watch
✅ Correct Answer: B

Per-node kernel-level debugging. Output: `Policy verdict log: local EP ID 567, remote ID 890, action deny, 10.0.1.5:45678 -> 10.0.2.9:5432 tcp SYN`.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Use `cilium-dbg monitor` when Hubble Relay is down or for deep kernel-level investigation. Compare with `hubble observe --verdict DROPPED` for cluster-wide view with pod names.

Network Policy Q72 of 200
anihpj-web cannot reach anihpj-api. Correct debugging sequence?
A) Restart both pods immediately
B) Delete all policies and reapply one by one
C) Check kubectl logs first, then restart Cilium agent
D) DNS → Hubble DROPPED → policy trace → endpoint inspect. Diagnose BEFORE restarting anything
✅ Correct Answer: D

1) nslookup name, 2) hubble observe --verdict DROPPED, 3) cilium policy trace, 4) cilium endpoint inspect. Never restart first — destroys evidence.

📝 Detailed Explanation

The systematic approach: DNS first (most common issue), Hubble to see drops (identifies which policy), policy trace to simulate, endpoint inspect to verify enforcement. Restarting pods masks the root cause.

Network Policy Q73 of 200
hubble observe vs cilium-dbg monitor for policy debugging?
A) Hubble = cluster-wide, pod names, via Relay. Monitor = per-node, identity numbers, raw eBPF. Hubble for ops; monitor for kernel debug
B) They show identical data — Hubble is just a GUI
C) Monitor is deprecated; Hubble replaces it entirely
D) Hubble only shows FORWARDED; monitor only shows DROPPED
✅ Correct Answer: A

Hubble: cluster-wide via Relay, pod names, L7 details. Monitor: per-node, identity numbers, raw events. Different tools for different debugging depths.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Aspecthubble observecilium-dbg monitor
ScopeCluster-wideSingle node
Names✅ Pod names❌ Identity numbers
L7✅ HTTP, DNSLimited
Best ForOps debuggingKernel-level debug
Network Policy Q74 of 200
CNP selects a pod with NO ingress rules and NO egress rules — what happens?
A) Full connectivity — empty rules = allow all
B) If Ingress specified with no rules → ALL inbound blocked. If Egress specified with no rules → ALL outbound blocked. Empty rules = default deny
C) CNP rejected by Kubernetes validation
D) CNP ignored until rules are added
✅ Correct Answer: B

Empty rules in a policyType direction = default deny for that direction. This is the "select-and-isolate" pattern for zero-trust security.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Create a CNP with `endpointSelector: {matchLabels: {app: db}}`, `ingress: []` (empty), `policyTypes: [Ingress]`. Result: DB pod can reach OUT but nothing can reach IN. Then add specific allow rules. Always add DNS if restricting egress.

Network Policy Q75 of 200
How does Host Firewall policy debugging differ from pod policy debugging?
A) Identical — same tools and process
B) Host policies can only be debugged via node console
C) Host endpoint has identity 1 (reserved:host). Find it with endpoint list, inspect normally. BUT: ALWAYS use audit mode — a lockout requires console access to recover
D) Host policies don't appear in endpoint list
✅ Correct Answer: C

Host endpoint = identity 1, label reserved:host. Debug similarly but AUDIT MODE IS MANDATORY — bad host policy → lockout → need SSH/console recovery.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Find host EP: `cilium-dbg endpoint list -o jsonpath='{[?(@.status.identity.id==1)].id}'`. Enable audit: `cilium-dbg endpoint config PolicyAuditMode=Enabled`. Emergency: SSH → crictl exec cilium-agent → enable audit → delete bad CCNP.

Network Policy Q76 of 200
CNP not appearing in `cilium policy get` — possible causes?
A) Wrong namespace (CNP is namespaced), selector matches zero pods, created CCNP but checking CNP, or CRD validation error
B) cilium policy get only shows policies from last 24 hours
C) CNPs take 5-10 minutes to appear
D) Policy auto-deleted because it had no effect
✅ Correct Answer: A

Check: `kubectl get cnp -A`, `kubectl get ccnp`, `kubectl describe cnp -n `, verify selectors match pods. Policy exists but may apply to nothing.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Checklist: `kubectl get cnp -A` (all namespaces), `kubectl get ccnp` (cluster-wide), `kubectl describe cnp -n ` (status errors), `kubectl get pods -l -n ` (verify match). Empty endpointSelector `{}` matches all pods in namespace.

🔄 Category 3: Service Mesh16% · 32 Questions

3.1 KPR & Maglev (Q77-Q86)

Service Mesh Q77 of 200
Two modes of Cilium KPR?
A) Local and Remote
B) Partial (ClusterIP only, any kernel) and Strict (ALL services, kernel 5.10+)
C) Ingress and Egress
D) Tunnel and Native
✅ Correct Answer: B

Partial = ClusterIP only via eBPF. Strict = ALL service types (NodePort, LoadBalancer, ExternalIPs) via eBPF. Enable: `--set kubeProxyReplacement=true`.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Strict requires: kernel 5.10+, native routing or tunnel with DSR, all node IPs known. Verify: `cilium status | grep KubeProxyReplacement`. Strict mode replaces iptables kube-proxy entirely.

Service Mesh Q78 of 200
What is Maglev hashing?
A) Magnetic levitation for data center cooling
B) Simple round-robin distribution
C) Google's consistent hashing: even distribution, connection affinity, only ~1/N connections disrupted when backends change
D) Kernel module replacing iptables NAT
✅ Correct Answer: C

Maglev = consistent hashing. Same 5-tuple → same backend (affinity). Backend removed → only connections hashed to that backend move (~1/N).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Maglev — Minimal Disruption on Backend Change
  BACKENDS [A,B,C]:              BACKEND B REMOVED:
  Hash(conn-1)→A   Hash(conn-4)→A    Hash(conn-1)→A ✓  Hash(conn-4)→A ✓
  Hash(conn-2)→B   Hash(conn-5)→B    Hash(conn-2)→C ✗  Hash(conn-5)→C ✗
  Hash(conn-3)→C   Hash(conn-6)→C    Hash(conn-3)→C ✓  Hash(conn-6)→C ✓
                                      Only 2/6 connections disrupted!
Service Mesh Q79 of 200
At what layer does Cilium KPR intercept service traffic?
A) SOCKET layer — eBPF cgroup/connect hooks intercept connect() syscall BEFORE TCP handshake
B) iptables layer — eBPF-based iptables rules
C) Network interface — TC hooks on eth0
D) Application layer — modifies destination in app code
✅ Correct Answer: A

Socket-level interception: pod calls `connect("10.96.100.50", 8001)`, eBPF redirects to real backend IP BEFORE TCP handshake. No iptables, no netfilter, no conntrack.

📝 Detailed Explanation

This is fundamentally different from kube-proxy's iptables DNAT. Socket LB: O(1) Maglev lookup at syscall time, TCP handshake starts directly with backend pod. NodePort (Strict mode): also uses TC hooks on physical devices.

Service Mesh Q80 of 200
What is DSR (Direct Server Return)?
A) Encrypts return path for security
B) Routes return traffic through central gateway
C) Compresses return traffic to save bandwidth
D) Backend responds DIRECTLY to client — bypasses LB on return. Cilium rewrites MAC. Backend sees real client IP. Higher throughput
✅ Correct Answer: D

DSR: Client → LB (MAC rewrite) → Backend → Client (direct!). Backend sees real client IP. No LB bottleneck on return path.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Without DSR: Client → LB (SNAT/DNAT) → Backend → LB (reverse) → Client. With DSR: return traffic bypasses LB entirely. Requires same L2 domain for MAC rewrite.

Service Mesh Q81 of 200
NodePort handling with KPR Strict mode?
A) Not supported — only ClusterIP works
B) eBPF TC programs on physical NIC intercept NodePort traffic, Maglev selects backend. No iptables
C) Goes through kube-proxy even with KPR
D) Uses userspace proxy per port
✅ Correct Answer: B

External packet → node eth0 → eBPF TC intercepts → recognizes NodePort → Maglev → DNAT to backend pod. All in kernel via eBPF.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Requires devices configured: `--set devices='{eth0}'`. externalTrafficPolicy: Local = only local pods, Cluster = any pod (extra hop).

Service Mesh Q82 of 200
How to verify KPR is managing services?
A) Check if kube-proxy pods are absent
B) kubectl describe service — KPR annotations
C) cilium service list — shows all services in eBPF LB with frontend IPs and backend endpoints
D) Check /var/log/cilium-kpr.log
✅ Correct Answer: C

`cilium service list` shows all Cilium-managed services: ID, Frontend IP, Service Type, Backend endpoints. Also: `cilium status | grep KubeProxyReplacement`.

📝 Detailed Explanation

If services appear here, they're managed by eBPF (not iptables). Shows: ClusterIP, NodePort, LoadBalancer services with all backend IPs and ports.

Service Mesh Q83 of 200
Cilium KPR performance vs iptables kube-proxy at scale?
A) ~587x faster — O(1) hash lookup vs O(n) sequential rule walking. 10k services: eBPF = 1 lookup; iptables = 10k+ rule checks per packet
B) Roughly equivalent — both use hash tables
C) ~2x faster due to kernel bypass
D) iptables is faster for small service counts
✅ Correct Answer: A

O(1) vs O(n). eBPF: single hash map lookup. iptables: sequential rule walk. Plus: socket-level interception avoids netfilter + conntrack overhead.

📝 Detailed Explanation

iptrules: 10k services = every packet traverses 10k+ rules. Rule updates take 30+ seconds. Conntrack table overflows. eBPF: atomic BPF map swaps, no conntrack. Maglev preserves connection affinity.

Service Mesh Q84 of 200
Flag to enable kube-proxy replacement?
A) --set kube-proxy.enabled=false
B) --set kubeProxyReplacement=true
C) --set loadBalancer.mode=ebpf
D) --set networking.kpr=true
✅ Correct Answer: B

`--set kubeProxyReplacement=true` during Helm install. For Strict mode: also `--set kubeProxyReplacementHealthzBindAddr="0.0.0.0:10256"`.

📝 Detailed Explanation

After enabling, kube-proxy is removed automatically. Not to be confused with `kube-proxy.enabled` (different flag). The correct current Helm value is `kubeProxyReplacement`.

Service Mesh Q85 of 200
Backend pod removed — what happens to connections with Maglev?
A) All connections dropped
B) All redistributed randomly
C) Only connections hashed to removed backend affected (~1/N). Others continue to original backends uninterrupted
D) Connections migrated without disruption
✅ Correct Answer: C

Consistent hashing = minimal disruption. Removed backend → only its connections reconnect. All others keep their affinity.

📝 Detailed Explanation

This is why Maglev is superior to random hashing (which redistributes ALL connections). Critical for dynamic environments where pods scale frequently.

Service Mesh Q86 of 200
Devices required for KPR Strict NodePort handling?
A) Only loopback (lo)
B) All veth interfaces
C) VXLAN tunnel interface
D) Physical network devices (eth0, ens192). Specify with --set devices='{eth0,eth1}'. Cilium auto-detects default route device
✅ Correct Answer: D

Cilium needs to know which devices face external traffic to attach NodePort TC programs. Auto-detection works for simple setups; specify explicitly for multi-homed nodes.

📝 Detailed Explanation

If devices are wrong: NodePort traffic arrives but Cilium doesn't intercept → kube-proxy handles it. Verify: `cilium status | grep Devices`.

3.2 Ingress & Gateway API (Q87-Q96)

Service Mesh Q87 of 200
What is Cilium Ingress?
A) Same as nginx-ingress
B) Built-in Envoy-based Ingress controller. Supports Ingress + Gateway API. No separate deployment. Integrates with CNP for L7 security
C) Only supports HTTP; not other protocols
D) CLI tool, not a Kubernetes resource
✅ Correct Answer: B

Enable: `--set ingressController.enabled=true`. Uses same per-node Envoy as L7 policies. Supports TLS termination, path/host routing, canary via Gateway API.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Unlike nginx-ingress (separate Helm chart), Cilium Ingress is built-in. Benefits: no extra deployment, integrates with CNP, supports Gateway API natively.

Service Mesh Q88 of 200
TLS modes supported by Cilium Ingress?
A) Edge only
B) Passthrough only
C) Edge (terminate at ingress), Passthrough (SNI routing, backend decrypts), Mutual (mTLS — both sides authenticate)
D) Cilium Ingress does not support TLS
✅ Correct Answer: C

Edge: TLS terminates at Ingress → backend gets HTTP. Passthrough: SNI-based routing without decrypting. Mutual: mTLS with client certificate authentication.

📝 Detailed Explanation

For anihpj: Edge mode with cert stored in K8s Secret. Passthrough for services that need end-to-end encryption. Mutual for service-to-service mTLS within mesh.

Service Mesh Q89 of 200
Canary deployments with Cilium?
A) Gateway API HTTPRoute with weighted backendRefs: 90% → v1, 10% → v2. Envoy handles weighted L7 distribution
B) Kubernetes Deployment rollingUpdate strategy
C) CiliumTrafficSplit CRD
D) Requires Istio
✅ Correct Answer: A

HTTPRoute with weighted backends. Deploy v2, send 10% traffic, monitor metrics, increase to 100%. All via Cilium's Envoy.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Progressive delivery without separate service mesh. Combined with Hubble HTTP metrics for canary analysis: compare v1 vs v2 latency/error rates.

Service Mesh Q90 of 200
Cilium Ingress Envoy crashes — what happens?
A) Permanent traffic loss
B) K8s restarts Envoy pod. Brief interruption. Other replicas continue. Pod-to-pod traffic unaffected (different data path)
C) Cilium Agent takes over
D) Auto-routed to another node's Envoy
✅ Correct Answer: B

Ingress Envoy is a Deployment (not DaemonSet). Multiple replicas for HA. Pod-to-pod traffic continues via eBPF — only external ingress is affected.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Production: 2+ ingress replicas, pod anti-affinity across nodes, health checks. Ingress is separate from Cilium's eBPF datapath.

Service Mesh Q91 of 200
Sidecar-free service mesh — how does Cilium implement it?
A) No proxies at all — only eBPF
B) One shared sidecar per namespace
C) Per-NODE Envoy (not per-pod). eBPF redirects traffic to local Envoy when L7 policies apply. No sidecar injection, no pod restarts
D) Application implements mesh logic itself
✅ Correct Answer: C

Key Cilium differentiator vs Istio. Per-node Envoy + eBPF redirect = L7 policies and mTLS without injecting sidecars into every pod.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Benefits: no pod restarts on mesh join, lower resource usage (1 Envoy per node, not per pod), simpler operations. L7 policies and mTLS work transparently.

Service Mesh Q92 of 200
externalTrafficPolicy: Local vs Cluster?
A) Controls encryption (TLS/plain)
B) IPv4 vs IPv6 selection
C) Cilium-specific BGP routing policy
D) Cluster: any node accepts, may need 2nd hop (loses client IP). Local: only nodes with ready pod accept (preserves client IP). Local incompatible with L2 Announcements
✅ Correct Answer: D

Cluster = better distribution, loses client IP. Local = preserves client IP, but traffic dropped if no local pod. Local + L2 Announcements = incompatible.

📝 Detailed Explanation

For anihpj: use Cluster for web (stateless), Local for api (if client IP needed for rate limiting). L2 Announcements requires Cluster.

Service Mesh Q93 of 200
mTLS in Cilium's sidecar-free mesh?
A) Per-node Envoy handles TLS termination + re-encryption. Apps communicate plaintext locally; Envoy encrypts on wire. Transparent to applications
B) Each pod needs its own cert and app-level TLS
C) Not supported without sidecars
D) Uses WireGuard instead of TLS certs
✅ Correct Answer: A

Transparent mTLS via Envoy. Apps send plaintext → Envoy encrypts → wire → remote Envoy decrypts → plaintext to backend. No app changes.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Different from WireGuard (L3 blanket encryption). mTLS provides per-service identity authentication with certificates. Both can coexist.

Service Mesh Q94 of 200
What is Gateway API?
A) Cilium-specific Ingress replacement

C) Cloud-only feature
D) Requires separate controller
✅ Correct Answer: B

Successor to Ingress. Role-based: GatewayClass (infra), Gateway (cluster admin), HTTPRoute (developer). Enable: `--set gatewayAPI.enabled=true`.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Cilium supports: weighted routing, header-based routing, request/response modification, traffic mirroring, cross-namespace routing via ReferenceGrant.

Service Mesh Q95 of 200
L7 rate limiting in Cilium?
A) Only through Hubble metrics — no enforcement

D) Via CiliumNetworkPolicy rateLimit rules

Per-route rate limiting through Envoy. Different from Bandwidth Manager (L3/L4 bits/sec). Example: 10 POST/minute to /api/jobs to prevent spam.

Service Mesh Q96 of 200

3.3 Bandwidth Manager & BBR (Q97-Q102) + 3.4 Sidecar-Free Mesh (Q103-Q108)

Service Mesh Q97 of 200
Bandwidth Manager uses what mechanism?
A) Token Bucket Filter (TBF)
B) EDT (Earliest Departure Time) with MQ/FQ scheduling. Precise per-packet pacing, no burstiness
C) iptables rate limiting
D) TCP window manipulation
✅ Correct Answer: B

EDT calculates exact departure time per packet. MQ/FQ ensures fairness. TBF causes burst-then-silence (jitter); EDT is smooth.

📝 Detailed Explanation

EDT: "1500-byte packet at 10 Mbps = 1.2ms after last one." Enable: `--set bandwidthManager.enabled=true`. Requires BPF Host Routing for best results.

Service Mesh Q98 of 200
How to set pod bandwidth limits?
A) CiliumBandwidthPolicy CRD
B) cilium bandwidth set --pod X --limit 10M
C) Standard K8s annotations: kubernetes.io/egress-bandwidth: "10M" and kubernetes.io/ingress-bandwidth: "20M"
D) Helm values per namespace
✅ Correct Answer: C

Same annotations as legacy bandwidth CNI. Verify: `cilium-dbg bpf bandwidth list`. Each replica gets full limit independently.

📝 Detailed Explanation

For anihpj: `kubernetes.io/egress-bandwidth: "50M"` on web pods. Use `"1G"` for gigabit. Limits apply per-pod, not per-deployment.

Service Mesh Q99 of 200
BBR congestion control — what and kernel requirement?
A) Google's algorithm: models bandwidth+RTT instead of reacting to loss. 2,700x throughput vs CUBIC on lossy links. Kernel 5.18+ for pods
B) Cisco proprietary; kernel 6.0+
C) Built into all kernels since 4.15
D) Cilium-specific using eBPF instead of kernel TCP
✅ Correct Answer: A

BBR models the pipe, not packet loss. Kernel 5.18+ required for pods (timestamp retention across network namespaces). Must also have BPF Host Routing.

📝 Detailed Explanation

CUBIC: "packet lost → congestion → cut rate." BBR: "what's the real bottleneck bandwidth? Send at that rate." Much better on WiFi/cellular/internet. Enable: `--set bandwidthManager.bbr=true`.

Service Mesh Q100 of 200
Bandwidth Manager WITHOUT BPF Host Routing?
A) Works normally — BPF Host Routing optional
B) Fails to start
C) Only egress works
D) Causes HIGH LATENCY — legacy routing adds unpredictable delays after EDT scheduling. Strongly recommended together. BBR REQUIRES BPF Host Routing
✅ Correct Answer: D

EDT schedules packet at time T, then legacy stack adds iptables/conntrack/routing delays → jitter. BPF Host Routing = direct veth forwarding, minimal delay.

📝 Detailed Explanation

BBR is even stricter: REQUIRES BPF Host Routing (not just recommended). Without it, socket association is lost in host stack, breaking BBR pacing entirely.

Service Mesh Q101 of 200
Hubble HTTP metrics — what do they provide?
A) Only status codes
B) Request rate (RPS), latency (P50/P95/P99), status codes, method, path. Prometheus metrics exported for Grafana dashboards
C) Only in Hubble UI, not Prometheus
D) Requires app code changes
✅ Correct Answer: B

Enable: `--set hubble.metrics.enabled="{http:query;path;method;statusCode}"`. For anihpj: GET /api/jobs latency, POST /admin errors — no Django changes.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Metrics: `hubble_http_requests_total` (counter), `hubble_http_request_duration_seconds` (histogram). Auto-scraped by Prometheus for Grafana visualization.

Service Mesh Q102 of 200
WireGuard encryption vs service mesh mTLS?
A) WireGuard = L3 blanket encryption between nodes. mTLS = L7 per-service encryption with identity auth. Complementary — can use together
B) Same thing — different names
C) mTLS replaces WireGuard
D) WireGuard for external; mTLS for internal
✅ Correct Answer: A

WireGuard: encrypts ALL node-to-node traffic at L3. mTLS: per-service authentication at L7 with certs. Use both for defense-in-depth.

📝 Detailed Explanation

WireGuard provides baseline encryption. mTLS adds identity verification (Service A proves "I am anihpj-web" with certificate). They complement, not compete.

Service Mesh Q103 of 200
CiliumEnvoyConfig CRD?
A) Auto-generated by Cilium
B) Creates sidecar proxies
C) ADVANCED: custom Envoy config for specific services — custom filters, clusters, listeners beyond CNP capabilities. Most users never need it
D) Deprecated
✅ Correct Answer: C

For edge cases: custom auth filters, rate limiting algorithms, external authz integration. Direct Envoy config injection while Cilium manages networking.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Most deployments never need this. Cilium auto-generates Envoy config from CNP, Ingress, and Gateway API. CiliumEnvoyConfig is the escape hatch for advanced Envoy features.

Service Mesh Q104 of 200
Session affinity (sticky sessions) in Cilium?

C) Requires Redis for session storage
D) Only works with L7 policies

Maglev consistent hashing naturally provides connection affinity. sessionAffinity: ClientIP extends across connections from same IP within timeout. O(1) eBPF lookup.

Service Mesh Q105 of 200

Service Mesh Q107 of 200

📝 Detailed Explanation

Gateway API is the successor to Ingress. Cilium's implementation uses the same Envoy as Ingress — consistent data plane across both APIs.

Service Mesh Q108 of 200

🔭 Category 4: Network Observability10% · 20 Questions

4.1 Hubble CLI & Flows (Q109-Q116)

Observability Q109 of 200
Show all real-time flows in anihpj namespace?
A) cilium monitor -n anihpj
B) hubble observe -n anihpj — pod names, identities, ports, verdicts, L7 details
C) kubectl logs -n anihpj --all-containers
D) cilium flow list --namespace anihpj
✅ Correct Answer: B

Primary command for flow visibility. Key flags: --from-pod, --to-pod, --verdict, --protocol, --http-method, --http-path, --to-port, --since, -o json.

📝 Detailed Explanation

Combine: `hubble observe -n anihpj --verdict DROPPED --to-port 5432` = only blocked DB traffic. `--since 5m` = last 5 minutes. `-o json` = scriptable output.

Observability Q116 of 200

Installation Q131 of 200
Migrate from Calico to Cilium?
A) Drain node → remove Calico CNI config → install Cilium → uncordon. One node at a time for zero downtime
B) Install Cilium alongside Calico — automatic migration
C) Delete all pods — they recreate with Cilium
D) Just run cilium install — overwrites Calico
✅ Correct Answer: A

Can't run two CNIs simultaneously — IP conflicts. Drain one node at a time, remove old CNI config from /etc/cni/net.d/, install Cilium.

Installation Q132 of 200

B) Running two CNIs simultaneously

✅ Correct Answer: D

Primary CNI: IPAM + veth creation. Cilium: attaches eBPF to veth for policy/visibility/encryption. Key: `cni.chainingMode`, `cni.exclusive=false`.

Deploys test pods, runs scenarios. First thing after install. Use `--flow-validation=disabled` if Hubble is off.

What is Cilium Cluster Mesh?

✅ Correct Answer: B

✅ Correct Answer: C

Each cluster needs unique `cluster.id` and `cluster.name`. Shared etcd for identity sync. Nodes must have IP reachability. Pod CIDRs must not overlap.

D) Via Kubernetes Federation API
✅ Correct Answer: A

Two-step: enable on each cluster, then connect. Verify: `cilium clustermesh status`. Shows connected clusters and their status.

Cluster Mesh Q152 of 200
How do global identities work in Cluster Mesh?
A) All clusters share same identity space
B) Identities are cluster-local only
C) Random UUIDs across clusters
D) Identity prefixed with cluster ID: identity 12345 in cluster-1 becomes 12345@1. Globally unique. Policies reference identities across clusters
✅ Correct Answer: D

Cluster ID prefix prevents collisions. Same labels in different clusters → different global identities. Cross-cluster policies reference global identities.

Cluster Mesh Q153 of 200

A) Faster internet access
B) Predictable source IP for outbound traffic. Pod egress exits through designated gateway nodes with fixed IP. Essential for external firewall whitelisting
C) Load balancing egress traffic
D) Encrypting outbound traffic
✅ Correct Answer: B

Without Egress GW: pod's outbound traffic exits from random node IP (changes if pod moves). With Egress GW: fixed, predictable source IP.

Global services span clusters. Affinity preferences: prefer local cluster backends, fail over to remote. Enables multi-cluster HA.

⚡ Category 7: eBPF10% · 20 Questions

Q169-Q188

eBPF Q169 of 200
eBPF program lifecycle?
A) Compile → Run
B) Load → Verify (no loops, bounded, known pointers) → JIT compile → Attach to hook point → Run in kernel. Pinned to bpffs for persistence
C) Write → Execute — no verification
D) Interpret at runtime — no compilation
✅ Correct Answer: B
eBPF Lifecycle: Load → Verify → JIT → Attach → Run
  ① COMPILE: clang -target bpf → program.o (BPF bytecode)
  ② LOAD:    bpf(BPF_PROG_LOAD) → copies bytecode into kernel
  ③ VERIFY:  Kernel verifier checks: no loops, known pointers, memory safe, max 1M instructions
  ④ JIT:     Translate BPF bytecode → native x86/ARM for speed
  ⑤ ATTACH:  Hook to event: XDP (NIC), TC (veth), cgroup/connect (socket), tracepoint
  ⑥ RUN:     Executes on every matching event in kernel context
  PERSIST:   Pinned to /sys/fs/bpf/ → survives cilium-agent restart!

Verifier ensures safety (no crashes, no infinite loops, memory safety). JIT for native performance. Pinned programs survive agent restarts.

eBPF Q170 of 200
eBPF hook points used by Cilium?
A) Only TC
B) Only XDP
C) XDP (early drop), TC (ingress/egress on veth), cgroup/connect (socket LB), socket (L7 redirect), tracepoint (observability)
D) Application-level hooks only
✅ Correct Answer: C
eBPF Hook Points — Where Cilium Attaches
  ① XDP (NIC driver)         ② TC Ingress/Egress (veth eth0)
     • Earliest processing       • Policy enforcement
     • DDoS mitigation           • Encapsulation/NodePort
     • Before sk_buff alloc      • Hubble flow capture

  ③ cgroup/connect (socket)   ④ Socket Operations
     • Intercepts connect()      • Redirect to Envoy (L7)
     • Maglev hash lookup        • HTTP/DNS/Kafka inspect
     • KPR backend selection     • Transparent to app

XDP: earliest packet processing (DDoS). TC: policy enforcement on veth. cgroup/connect: KPR socket LB. socket: L7 redirect to Envoy. tracepoint: runtime monitoring.

eBPF Q171 of 200
eBPF map types used by Cilium?
A) BPF_MAP_TYPE_HASH (policy lookup), LRU_HASH (conntrack), ARRAY (config), PERCPU_HASH (per-CPU counters), LRU (endpoint cache)
B) Only HASH
C) Only ARRAY
D) Custom Cilium maps
✅ Correct Answer: A

HASH: O(1) policy lookup. LRU_HASH: connection tracking with auto-eviction. ARRAY: fixed config. PERCPU: lockless per-CPU stats.

eBPF Q172 of 200
eBPF verifier rules?
A) No rules — any code runs
B) Only size limit
C) Only instruction count limit
D) No unbounded loops, all pointers must be known/checked, no unreachable instructions, max 1M instructions (5.1+), no kernel memory access outside allowed regions
✅ Correct Answer: D

Verifier ensures safety: program won't crash kernel, won't leak memory, won't run forever. This is why eBPF is safe for production kernel use.

eBPF Q173 of 200
What is CO-RE (Compile Once, Run Everywhere)?
A) Cross-platform eBPF runtime
B) Compile eBPF once, run on ANY kernel version (5.4+ with BTF). Uses BTF for type information, relocations for kernel struct differences
C) Container runtime for eBPF
D) eBPF compiler optimization
✅ Correct Answer: B

Without CO-RE: compile per kernel version. With CO-RE: one eBPF binary runs on any 5.4+ kernel. Cilium uses CO-RE for portability.

eBPF Q174 of 200
eBPF in Cilium — how used?
A) Policy enforcement (BPF maps), load balancing (Maglev in BPF), bandwidth (EDT), encryption (WireGuard), observability (ring buffer), Host Routing (veth forwarding)
B) Only policy enforcement
C) Only observability
D) Only load balancing
✅ Correct Answer: A

Cilium uses eBPF for the ENTIRE data plane — not just one function. This is why it can replace kube-proxy, iptables, and provide observability simultaneously.

eBPF Q175 of 200
bpftool — inspect Cilium's eBPF?
A) bpftool cilium list
B) Cannot inspect — Cilium hides eBPF
C) bpftool prog list (loaded programs), bpftool map dump pinned /sys/fs/bpf/tc/globals/cilium_* (policy maps, conntrack, LB)
D) Only through Cilium CLI
✅ Correct Answer: C

Use bpftool for kernel-level eBPF inspection. Cilium's maps are pinned to /sys/fs/bpf/. `bpftool map dump` shows policy rules, endpoints, conntrack.

eBPF Q176 of 200
eBPF Host Routing vs legacy routing?
A) Same — different name
B) eBPF Host Routing: direct veth→veth forwarding via BPF, bypasses iptables/netfilter/route lookup → lower latency, preserves socket association. Legacy: goes through full host stack
C) Legacy is faster
D) eBPF only for cross-node
✅ Correct Answer: B

Without BPF Host Routing: pod1 → veth → iptables → routing → netfilter → veth → pod2. With it: pod1 → veth → eBPF forward → veth → pod2.

🌐 Category 8: BGP & External Networking6% · 12 Questions

Q189-Q200

BGP & External Q189 of 200
BGP in Cilium — what CRD?
A) CiliumBGPPolicy
B) CiliumBGPConfig
C) CiliumBGPPeeringPolicy — defines local ASN, neighbor router IP/ASN, which Service IPs to advertise. nodeSelector picks BGP speaker nodes
D) CiliumBGPNeighbor
✅ Correct Answer: C

apiVersion: cilium.io/v2alpha1. Defines peering with upstream routers. nodeSelector limits which nodes participate.

BGP & External Q190 of 200
LB IPAM in Cilium?
A) CiliumLoadBalancerIPPool CRD defines IP ranges. Cilium assigns IPs to LoadBalancer Services automatically — no cloud provider needed. Combine with BGP to announce IPs
B) Only cloud LB integration
C) Manual IP assignment per service
D) Uses DHCP for LB IP allocation
✅ Correct Answer: A

Enable: `--set lbIPAM.enabled=true`. Define pool: `spec: {cidrs: ["10.100.0.0/24"]}`. Cilium auto-assigns from pool + BGP announces.

BGP & External Q191 of 200
L2 Announcements — what are they?
A) Replacement for BGP
B) Beta feature for bare metal WITHOUT BGP routers. Responds to ARP/NDP for Service IPs. One node (leader via K8s Lease) answers ARP. Same L2 domain only
C) VLAN announcement protocol
D) L2 encryption protocol
✅ Correct Answer: B

For office/campus LANs without BGP. CRD: CiliumL2AnnouncementPolicy. Leader election via K8s Leases. Failover: 10-20s default.

BGP & External Q192 of 200
L2 Announcements limitations?
A) No limitations
B) Only 10 services
C) Only works with BGP
D) Single L2 domain only, one leader per service (bottleneck), incompatible with externalTrafficPolicy:Local, not a security feature, needs client rate limit sizing
✅ Correct Answer: D

Key: ARP maps one IP→one MAC. All external traffic for a service goes through ONE leader node. Watch API rate: QPS = #services × (1/renewDeadline).

BGP & External Q193 of 200
BGP vs L2 Announcements — when to use each?
A) BGP: routed networks, large DC, internet-facing. Requires BGP router. L2: office/campus LAN, no BGP router, same broadcast domain. L2 simpler but less scalable
B) Always BGP — L2 deprecated
C) Always L2 — BGP too complex
D) Both simultaneously
✅ Correct Answer: A

BGP = L3, ECMP possible, multi-path, entire network. L2 = ARP, single leader, same broadcast domain, simpler setup but less scalable.

BGP & External Q194 of 200
BGP graceful restart in Cilium?
A) Not supported
B) Automatic — no configuration
C) Supported — Cilium can preserve forwarding state during BGP session restart. Reduces traffic disruption during Cilium agent restarts
D) Only for eBGP, not iBGP
✅ Correct Answer: C

Graceful restart preserves routes during session flap. Combined with eBPF program persistence, minimizes traffic disruption during maintenance.

🔧 Part 2: 100 Troubleshooting Issues

Complete Cilium Troubleshooting Reference — 100 real issues you'll face with Cilium in Kubernetes. Each with 15 categorized causes, solution steps, and personal advice. Plus 15 decision tree checklists.

100Issues
15Decision Trees
500+Causes Cataloged
8Categories

🏗️ Category 1: ArchitectureA1-A20

20 troubleshooting issues covering pod-to-pod communication, services, identity, agent crashes, and endpoint failures — the foundation of Cilium networking.

📦 A1 — A5: Pod-to-Pod Communication Failures

🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. Veth pair disconnected or missing: Check ip link show lxc_<endpoint-id>. The virtual ethernet to the pod may not exist or be DOWN. Run cilium endpoint list.
  2. BPF datapath not attached: Run cilium-dbg endpoint config <ep-id>. Look for "Conntrack: Enabled" and "Policy: Enforced". If the BPF program is detached, packets drop.
  3. NetworkPolicy denial: A default-deny CiliumNetworkPolicy may be silently dropping same-node traffic. Run hubble observe --from-pod ns/pod-a --to-pod ns/pod-b --verdict DROPPED.
  4. Pod CIDR conflict: Two pods on the same node may have duplicate IPs. Check kubectl get pod -o wide for duplicate PodIP entries.
  5. Cilium agent endpoint sync stalled: The agent may not have registered the pod endpoint. Run cilium endpoint get <ep-id> and check state is "ready".
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. Kernel BPF verifier rejecting program on load
  2. eBPF map (cilium_ipcache) corruption causing wrong identity lookup
  3. Conntrack table full — net.netfilter.nf_conntrack_max reached
  4. iptables rule left over from previous CNI intercepting traffic
  5. ARP/NDP cache poisoning from a misconfigured neighbor entry
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. Cilium not fully installed: Agent pod may still be in CrashLoopBackOff. Check kubectl get pods -n kube-system -l k8s-app=cilium.
  2. Wrong CNI conflist: /etc/cni/net.d/05-cilium.conflist may point to wrong socket. Verify with cat /etc/cni/net.d/05-cilium.conflist.
  3. Container runtime not using Cilium: containerd/cri-o config may still reference old CNI. Check containerd config.toml for plugins.cni.
  4. Kubelet CNI args mismatch: --network-plugin=cni --cni-conf-dir=/etc/cni/net.d may be wrong. Check kubelet flags.
  5. Cilium ConfigMap not propagated: Run kubectl get cm -n kube-system cilium-config -o yaml and verify values match expected.
🔍 What to Look At / Take Note Of: cilium endpoint list output -- state must be "ready". Check ip link show lxc_* for veth pairs. cilium-dbg bpf policy get <id> shows if BPF programs are attached. Note: same-node failures are the rarest Cilium issue -- usually a recent CNI change.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. cilium endpoint list — find the endpoint ID for both pods
2. cilium-dbg endpoint config <id> — verify BPF programs are attached
3. cilium-dbg bpf policy get <id> — check if policies are denying
4. ip link show lxc_<id> — verify veth is UP
5. If all good, restart Cilium agent: kubectl -n kube-system delete pod -l k8s-app=cilium --field-selector spec.nodeName=node-1

💡 Personal Advice: Same-node failures are rare with Cilium — the BPF datapath is very reliable. When they happen, it is usually a recent CNI config change or a policy change. Always run cilium connectivity test first — it catches same-node issues immediately. For anihpj: keep a simple allow-all CNP in your dev namespace so you can isolate whether it is a policy vs. datapath issue.
A2
🟢 CATEGORY 1: ARCHITECTURE — Issue A2
Pod Cannot Reach Another Pod on DIFFERENT Node

🔍 Symptom: anihpj-web pod on node-1 (IP 10.0.1.5) cannot ping anihpj-api pod on node-2 (IP 10.0.2.9). Same-node pod communication works fine.

  Node-1 (10.0.1.0/24)                 Node-2 (10.0.2.0/24)
  ┌─────────────────────┐              ┌─────────────────────┐
  │ anihpj-web 10.0.1.5 │———— ❌ ————→ │ anihpj-api 10.0.2.9 │
  │ ✅ local pods work   │              │ ✅ local pods work   │
  └─────────────────────┘              └─────────────────────┘
🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. VXLAN/Geneve tunnel not established: Run cilium status | grep Encapsulation. Check ip link show cilium_vxlan or ip link show cilium_geneve. Must be UP.
  2. Cross-node route missing: In native routing mode, each node needs routes to other pods. Run ip route | grep 10.0.2. Should show route via node-2 IP.
  3. Firewall blocking VXLAN/UDP 8472 or Geneve/UDP 6081: Check cloud security groups, host firewall. Run nc -u <node-2-ip> 8472 from node-1.
  4. MTU blackhole: VXLAN overhead (50 bytes) reduces effective MTU. Packets > 1450 may get silently dropped. Test: ping -M do -s 1400 10.0.2.9.
  5. Identity mismatch across nodes: Cilium uses numeric identities. If identity sync fails, packets may be dropped. Run cilium identity list on both nodes.
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. Cilium agent running different versions on nodes (identity format mismatch)
  2. BPF tunnel map (cilium_tunnel_map) corrupted
  3. Kernel module vxlan not loaded: lsmod | grep vxlan
  4. ARP cache stale on node-1 for node-2 after IP change
  5. Network fabric dropping fragmented UDP (VXLAN with large payloads)
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. Tunnel mode not configured: Check cilium config | grep tunnel. Default is "vxlan". Ensure all nodes agree.
  2. Node ports not open in firewall: 8472/UDP (VXLAN) or 6081/UDP (Geneve). Check iptables/security groups.
  3. Pod CIDR ranges not unique per node: Check cilium config | grep cluster-pool-ipv4-cidr and node CIDRs via kubectl get ciliumnodes.
  4. cilium-operator not running: Operator assigns per-node CIDRs. Check kubectl get pods -n kube-system -l name=cilium-operator.
  5. BPF Host Routing not enabled: On new kernels (5.10+), enable with cilium config set bpf-host-routing true for performance.
🔍 What to Look At / Take Note Of: ip link show cilium_vxlan -- interface MUST be UP. Cloud security groups MUST allow UDP 8472 (VXLAN) or 6081 (Geneve) between ALL worker nodes. MTU: if using VXLAN, packets > 1450 bytes get fragmented. cilium-health status is the fastest cross-node check.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. cilium connectivity test — identifies which cross-node scenarios fail
2. hubble observe --from-pod ns/pod-a --to-pod ns/pod-b — see where packets die
3. ip link show cilium_vxlan — tunnel must be UP on both nodes
4. For MTU: cilium config set mtu 1400 (or use auto-detection: cilium config set mtu auto)
5. For routing: ensure node-1 has ip route add 10.0.2.0/24 via <node-2-ip>

💡 Personal Advice: Cross-node failures are the #1 post-install issue. MTU is the silent killer — packets look like they should work but get dropped silently at the fabric level. For anihpj running in AWS: VXLAN + AWS VPC = effective MTU of ~8950 for jumbo frames, so if you are using standard 1500, you are fine. The issue is almost always the security group not allowing UDP between worker nodes.
A3
🟢 CATEGORY 1: ARCHITECTURE — Issue A3
Pod-to-Pod Latency Spikes (>100ms for Same-Region Nodes)

🔍 Symptom: anihpj-api responds with latency spikes — sometimes 2ms, sometimes 300ms. Intermittent. No packet loss visible with ping. Hubble shows forwarded packets.

🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. BPF conntrack table full: Each connection needs a conntrack entry. Full table = slow GC. Run cilium-dbg bpf ct list global | wc -l. If > 500k, investigate.
  2. eBPF map contention: Under high connection churn, BPF map locks cause tail latency. Check with cilium-dbg map events for map pressure metrics.
  3. Node CPU throttling: Cilium agent competes for CPU. Check kubectl top pod -n kube-system -l k8s-app=cilium. Agent should have guaranteed CPU.
  4. L7 proxy (Envoy) bottleneck: If L7 policies are applied, every new flow goes through Envoy. Check cilium status | grep "L7 Proxy". Envoy may need more resources.
  5. DNS resolution delays: Every new connection triggers DNS if toFQDNs policies are used. Check CoreDNS latency: kubectl logs -n kube-system -l k8s-app=kube-dns.
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. Kernel lock contention on vxlan/veth path under load
  2. Hubble exporting flows to slow backend causing buffer backpressure
  3. SELinux/AppArmor auditing every BPF syscall (check auditd logs)
  4. Transparent encryption (WireGuard/IPSec) key rotation happening mid-flow
  5. Network fabric ECMP rebalancing causing packet reordering
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. Cilium agent resource limits too low: Default requests may be too small for production. Increase CPU/memory limits in Helm values.
  2. Hubble enabled without relay: Hubble exports flows to nowhere, filling buffers. Disable if not needed: helm upgrade cilium cilium/cilium --set hubble.enabled=false.
  3. No resource limits on noisy neighbor pods: Other pods starving Cilium agent of CPU. Set QoS Guaranteed for Cilium.
  4. WireGuard encryption enabled on underpowered nodes: WireGuard adds ~5-10% CPU overhead. Ensure nodes have AES-NI support.
  5. iptables rules still active from kube-proxy: If KPR is partial, some traffic still hits iptables (slow path). Check cilium status | grep KubeProxyReplacement.
🔍 What to Look At / Take Note Of: cilium-dbg bpf ct list global | wc -l -- conntrack > 100k on a node is a red flag. Default GC interval is 5 min (too slow for high-churn). cilium-dbg monitor --type drop shows retransmissions. Envoy proxy at :9090/metrics for L7 latency.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. Profile with Hubble: hubble observe -o json | jq ".flow.latency" to find the hop adding latency
2. Check BPF maps: cilium-dbg bpf ct list global | tail -20 — look for CLOSING entries accumulating
3. Increase conntrack GC: cilium config set conntrack-gc-interval 30s
4. Scale Envoy if L7 proxy is the bottleneck: increase hubble.ui.replicas and proxy resources
5. Use cilium-dbg monitor --type drop to see if packets are being retransmitted due to drops

💡 Personal Advice: Tail latency in BPF datapath is almost always conntrack GC pressure. The default conntrack GC interval of 5 minutes is too long for high-churn workloads. Reduce it to 30s. For anihpj specifically: if you use toFQDNs policies referencing external APIs (like AWS RDS endpoints), every FQDN lookup adds DNS latency. Cache those IPs in CiliumCIDRGroup instead.
A4
🟢 CATEGORY 1: ARCHITECTURE — Issue A4
Packet Drops Between Pods in Different Namespaces

🔍 Symptom: anihpj-web (namespace: anihpj) cannot reach postgres (namespace: database). Same-namespace communication works. The packets are dropped with POLICY_DENIED verdict.

🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. NetworkPolicy with namespaceSelector mismatch: If a CNP selects pods via namespaceSelector, verify labels exist. Run kubectl get ns anihpj --show-labels.
  2. Default deny policy in either namespace: Check kubectl get cnp -A | grep "default-deny". A deny-all in either namespace blocks cross-namespace traffic.
  3. Ingress policy missing on destination: CiliumNetworkPolicy must have an ingress rule allowing the source. Ingress is always evaluated at the destination endpoint.
  4. Wrong entity type in policy: Using fromEndpoints instead of fromEntities for cluster-internal traffic. fromEntities: [cluster] may be needed.
  5. DNS policy blocking namespace discovery: If toFQDNs rule references a service name, DNS resolution may fail if DNS is denied for the source namespace.
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. CiliumNetworkPolicy and Kubernetes NetworkPolicy conflict (union behavior)
  2. Proxy redirection for L7 taking >5s timeout on first packet
  3. Cilium identity for namespace stuck in "deleting" state
  4. CNP applied to wrong cluster with CCNP (CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy)
  5. FQDN policy caching stale IP after pod restart in target namespace
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. No policies configured at all: In a new cluster with no CNPs, all traffic is allowed. If you see DENIED, a policy exists somewhere. Run cilium policy get.
  2. Namespace labels not yet applied: Namespace selector policies may fail if labels are missing. Example: kubectl label ns database app=db.
  3. Imported policies from another cluster (Cluster Mesh): If Cluster Mesh is active, policies from another cluster may apply. Check with cilium policy selectors.
  4. Policy audit mode enabled: Traffic is allowed but logged as DENIED for audit. Check hubble observe --verdict DROPPED — if audit mode, flow still succeeds.
  5. Wrong CNP API version: CiliumNetworkPolicy requires apiextensions.k8s.io/v1 CRDs. Run kubectl api-resources | grep cilium.
🔍 What to Look At / Take Note Of: CiliumNetworkPolicy + Kubernetes NetworkPolicy use UNION semantics -- a KNP ALLOW + CNP DENY = ALLOW. cilium policy trace ALWAYS shows the truth. Namespace labels are NOT set by default. Check BOTH namespaces for default-deny policies.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. cilium policy trace --src-k8s-pod anihpj:anihpj-web-xxx --dst-k8s-pod database:postgres-yyy --dport 5432 — shows which policy rule matches
2. hubble observe --from-pod anihpj/anihpj-web-xxx --to-pod database/postgres-yyy --verdict DROPPED — see the exact drop reason
3. Verify namespace labels: kubectl get ns anihpj database -o yaml | grep labels -A5
4. Check all CNPs: kubectl get cnp,ccnp -A — a CCNP may apply cluster-wide
5. Use audit mode to test: apply policy with --set policyAuditMode=true to log without blocking

💡 Personal Advice: Cross-namespace policy debugging is the #1 time sink in Cilium. Always use cilium policy trace before writing policies — it tells you exactly which rule matches. For anihpj: create a dedicated "allow-all-within-anihpj" CNP first, then add restrictive rules one by one. The union behavior of CiliumNetworkPolicy + Kubernetes NetworkPolicy catches everyone off guard — a KNP allow + CNP deny = ALLOW (union wins).
A5
🟢 CATEGORY 1: ARCHITECTURE — Issue A5
WireGuard/Transparent Encryption Breaking Pod Communication

🔍 Symptom: After enabling WireGuard encryption, some cross-node pod pairs cannot communicate. Hubble shows packets forwarded but never arrive. Encryption status shows "Enabled" but partial.

  Node-1                          Node-2
  ┌──────────┐                    ┌──────────┐
  │ WG Key A │—— secure tunnel ——→│ WG Key B │
  │ OK ✅    │                    │ ❌ FAIL   │
  └──────────┘                    └──────────┘
  Node-1 has key for Node-2, but Node-2 does NOT have key for Node-1
  Result: traffic flows one-way only
🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. Missing kernel module: WireGuard needs wireguard kernel module. Run lsmod | grep wireguard. If missing: modprobe wireguard.
  2. Node-to-node key exchange failed: Cilium uses kube-apiserver to exchange WireGuard public keys. Check cilium-dbg encrypt status — shows per-node key state.
  3. WireGuard port (51871/UDP) blocked: The default WireGuard port must be open between all nodes. Check firewall rules and security groups.
  4. MTU not adjusted for WireGuard overhead: WireGuard adds ~60 bytes overhead. With VXLAN + WireGuard, MTU should be 1400 or lower. Test: ping -M do -s 1380 <target>.
  5. Encryption enabled only on some nodes: Node restart may have missed the encryption config. Check cilium config | grep enable-wireguard on ALL nodes.
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. WireGuard key rotation in progress — new key not propagated to all nodes yet
  2. Node-2 agent restarted and lost in-memory peer table (requires full resync)
  3. WireGuard kernel bug with certain kernel versions (check compat matrix)
  4. Encryption key stored in k8s secret deleted accidentally
  5. Node IP changed but WireGuard peer still uses old IP
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. WireGuard not enabled in Helm: Must set encryption.enabled=true and encryption.type=wireguard or cilium install --set encryption.type=wireguard.
  2. Kernel too old: WireGuard requires kernel 5.6+. Check uname -r. On older kernels, install wireguard-dkms.
  3. Node-to-node connectivity via pod CIDR, not node IP: WireGuard tunnels use node IPs, not pod IPs. Verify nodes can reach each other on their primary IPs.
  4. cilium-operator not deployed: Operator manages key rotation. Must be running.
  5. Encryption strict mode causing ALL traffic to drop if any node lacks keys: Check with cilium config | grep encryption-strict-mode.
🔍 What to Look At / Take Note Of: WireGuard uses UDP port 51871 by default. Kernel module wireguard must be loaded on ALL nodes. MTU for VXLAN+WireGuard: 1400 (1500 - 50 VXLAN - 60 WG). cilium-dbg encrypt status shows per-node key state. One-way encryption = missing key on one side.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. cilium-dbg encrypt status — shows per-node encryption state and key count
2. kubectl get ciliumnodes -o yaml | grep -A10 "encryption" — verify keys are stored
3. ip link show cilium_wg0 — WireGuard interface must exist
4. wg show — shows WireGuard peer table directly
5. Restart agent: kubectl -n kube-system rollout restart daemonset cilium

💡 Personal Advice: WireGuard issues are almost always MTU-related or missing kernel modules. On AWS EKS Bottlerocket, WireGuard works out of the box. On Ubuntu, you may need linux-modules-extra-$(uname -r). For anihpj: if you use WireGuard, set MTU to 1400 in your Cilium config AND in your application. Nothing is more confusing than TCP connections that hang because the SYN reaches but the data does not.

🔌 A6 — A10: Service/ClusterIP Not Working

A6
🟢 CATEGORY 1: ARCHITECTURE — Issue A6
ClusterIP Service Not Routing to Backend Pods

🔍 Symptom: anihpj-api ClusterIP (10.96.100.50:8080) returns connection refused or timeout. Pods exist and are healthy. Direct pod IP access works fine.

  Client Pod                     ClusterIP Svc                  Backend Pods
  ┌──────────┐     ❌ TIME       10.96.100.50:8080             ┌──────────┐
  │curl svc  │────────────────→  ┌─────────────┐    ✅ OK     │anihpj-api│
  │          │                   │ No backends! │────────────→│ 10.0.1.9 │
  └──────────┘                   └─────────────┘              └──────────┘
  Direct pod IP access works — service selector/labels broken
🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. Service label selector mismatch: Run kubectl describe svc anihpj-api. Compare selector with pod labels: kubectl get pods -l app=anihpj-api. If labels don't match, service has no endpoints.
  2. Endpoints object empty: Check kubectl get endpoints anihpj-api. If no endpoints, the service cannot route. May be caused by pod not being Ready.
  3. Cilium service BPF map not updated: Run cilium-dbg service list | grep anihpj-api. If service is missing from BPF map, agent hasn't synced it. Restart agent.
  4. KPR (kube-proxy replacement) partial mode: In partial KPR, some services still use kube-proxy. Check cilium status | grep KubeProxyReplacement. If "Partial", some services may use iptables path.
  5. NetworkPolicy blocking service traffic: A CNP may block traffic to the service VIP. Run cilium policy trace --src-k8s-pod src:pod --dst-k8s-svc anihpj:anihpj-api --dport 8080.
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. BPF service map (cilium_services) full — max service entries reached
  2. Maglev backend selection table corruption (KPR strict mode)
  3. Service sessionAffinity causing sticky sessions to dead backends
  4. kube-apiserver watch connection broken — agent missed service create event
  5. Cilium operator overwriting service entries during reconciliation
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. Service type misconfigured: Check kubectl get svc anihpj-api -o yaml | grep type. Must be ClusterIP (or NodePort/LoadBalancer).
  2. Pods not yet Ready: New pods take time to pass readiness probes. Check kubectl get pods -l app=anihpj-api — all must be 1/1 Ready.
  3. Cilium agent not running on node where backend pods are: Agent must run on every node with pods. Check kubectl get pods -n kube-system -l k8s-app=cilium -o wide.
  4. kube-proxy still running: In KPR strict mode, kube-proxy must be removed. Check kubectl get pods -n kube-system | grep kube-proxy.
  5. DNS not resolving service name: Test with nslookup anihpj-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local. If DNS fails, check CoreDNS.
🔍 What to Look At / Take Note Of: kubectl get endpoints is the single most important command -- empty endpoints = selector mismatch. Service selector matches pod LABELS (from template), NOT deployment name. BPF service map: cilium-dbg service list. KPR mode matters: Partial may use iptables.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. kubectl get endpoints anihpj-api — must show at least one IP:port pair
2. kubectl describe svc anihpj-api — verify selector matches pod labels exactly
3. cilium-dbg service list | grep anihpj-api — service must appear in BPF map
4. If missing from BPF: cilium-dbg service list to see all services, then kubectl -n kube-system delete pod -l k8s-app=cilium to resync
5. Test with cilium connectivity test — includes service connectivity checks
