💡 Personal Advice: Service selector mismatch is the #1 cause. People copy YAML and forget to update labels. Always verify with kubectl get endpoints — if empty, your selector is wrong. For anihpj: the service selector must match the pod template labels in your Deployment, NOT the labels on the Deployment itself. This distinction catches many beginners.
A7
🟢 CATEGORY 1: ARCHITECTURE — Issue A7
DNS Resolution Fails for Kubernetes Services

🔍 Symptom: anihpj-web cannot resolve anihpj-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local. nslookup returns NXDOMAIN or timeout. Direct IP access works.

🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. CoreDNS pods not running: Check kubectl get pods -n kube-system -l k8s-app=kube-dns. CoreDNS must be Running.
  2. NetworkPolicy blocking DNS (UDP 53): Check hubble observe --to-port 53 --protocol UDP. If DROPPED, CNP is blocking DNS. Add toEndpoints: [{matchLabels: {"k8s-app": "kube-dns"}}] to your policy.
  3. CoreDNS ClusterIP not reachable from pod: Test with kubectl run -it --rm debug --image=busybox -- nslookup anihpj-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local. If this works but your pod doesn't, it's a policy issue.
  4. Pod DNS config wrong: Check kubectl exec anihpj-web -- cat /etc/resolv.conf. Should have nameserver 10.96.0.10 (CoreDNS ClusterIP) and search anihpj.svc.cluster.local.
  5. toFQDNs policy interfering: A restrictive toFQDNs policy may block DNS for non-matching domains. Check with cilium policy trace.
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. CoreDNS ConfigMap corrupted — upstream DNS unreachable
  2. DNS cache poisoning from a previous misconfiguration
  3. Hubble DNS visibility interfering (Hubble captures DNS but doesn't block)
  4. Service mesh DNS proxy (Istio/Consul) intercepting DNS requests
  5. NodeLocal DNSCache misconfigured and bypassing CoreDNS
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. CoreDNS not deployed: Some minimal cluster setups don't include CoreDNS. Check kubectl get svc -n kube-system kube-dns.
  2. CoreDNS ClusterIP changed: If the DNS service IP changes, all pods need restart to pick up the new /etc/resolv.conf.
  3. Pod in different namespace: Short name resolution (anihpj-api) only works within the same namespace. Use FQDN across namespaces.
  4. Cilium L7 DNS proxy enabled but not configured: Check cilium config | grep dns-proxy. If enabled, ensure CoreDNS traffic is allowed.
  5. CNI not setting up pod DNS correctly: Cilium should set /etc/resolv.conf. Check if kubelet --cluster-dns flag is set correctly.
🔍 What to Look At / Take Note Of: DNS is ALWAYS UDP port 53 first, TCP/53 only for large responses. Default-deny policy MUST include DNS allow rule FIRST. Pod /etc/resolv.conf should point to 10.96.0.10 (CoreDNS ClusterIP). Cross-namespace resolution REQUIRES FQDN.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. kubectl get pods -n kube-system -l k8s-app=kube-dns — verify CoreDNS is Running
2. kubectl exec anihpj-web -- nslookup anihpj-api.anihpj.svc.cluster.local 10.96.0.10 — test DNS directly
3. hubble observe --to-port 53 -n anihpj — check if DNS packets are forwarded or dropped
4. If dropped by policy: add DNS allow rule to CNP
5. If CoreDNS is down: kubectl -n kube-system rollout restart deployment coredns

💡 Personal Advice: DNS is the silent dependency everyone forgets. When you create a default-deny NetworkPolicy, the first thing that breaks is DNS — and then nothing else works because services can't be resolved. Always include a DNS allow rule FIRST in any restrictive policy. For anihpj: add toEndpoints: [{matchLabels: {"k8s-app": "kube-dns"}}] with toPorts: [{ports: [{port: "53", protocol: UDP}]}] as the very first egress rule.
A8
🟢 CATEGORY 1: ARCHITECTURE — Issue A8
NodePort Service Unreachable from Outside

🔍 Symptom: anihpj-api NodePort (30080) is not reachable from outside the cluster. curl <node-ip>:30080 times out. Pod is healthy, ClusterIP works internally.

🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. KPR not handling NodePort: In KPR mode, Cilium must handle NodePort via BPF. Check cilium status | grep KubeProxyReplacement. Must be "Strict" for full NodePort support.
  2. Host firewall blocking port: Check iptables on the node: iptables -L -n | grep 30080. Also check cloud security group/firewall rules.
  3. externalTrafficPolicy: Local with no local backends: If set to Local and the receiving node has no backend pod, traffic is dropped. Check with kubectl describe svc anihpj-api | grep externalTrafficPolicy.
  4. NodePort range mismatch: Default NodePort range is 30000-32767. Check kubectl get svc anihpj-api -o yaml | grep nodePort. Must be within range.
  5. BPF NodePort map not initialized: Run cilium-dbg bpf nodeport list. If empty, NodePort acceleration is not active. Check cilium config | grep enable-nodeport.
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. DSR (Direct Server Return) misconfigured causing asymmetric routing
  2. SNAT for NodePort not working (hairpin traffic from pod to own node's NodePort)
  3. NodePort BPF map limit reached (max entries configurable)
  4. Conntrack entry collision for NodePort flows from same source IP
  5. ARP responder for ExternalIPs not answering on node interface
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. Cilium NodePort not enabled: Set nodePort.enabled=true in Helm or cilium install --set nodePort.enabled=true.
  2. kube-proxy still running: Cilium KPR strict mode requires kube-proxy to be removed. Check kubectl get ds -n kube-system kube-proxy.
  3. Cloud load balancer health check failing: AWS NLB health check may hit wrong port. Check target group health status.
  4. Node firewall/security group not allowing inbound on NodePort range: Add rule for 30000-32767 TCP.
  5. kubelet --nodeport-addresses restricts NodePort listening: Check kubelet args for explicit IP binding.
🔍 What to Look At / Take Note Of: KPR MUST be "Strict" for full NodePort (not "Partial"). externalTrafficPolicy: Local drops traffic if receiving node has NO local backend. Cloud NLB health checks: use TCP on NodePort. NodePort range: 30000-32767 only.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. cilium status | grep KubeProxyReplacement — verify KPR mode
2. cilium-dbg bpf nodeport list — verify NodePort entries in BPF
3. ss -tlnp | grep 30080 — check if port is listening on node
4. curl -v <node-ip>:30080 from outside the cluster
5. If DSR issue: check cilium config | grep nodeport-mode — try switching from "dsr" to "snat"

💡 Personal Advice: KPR NodePort is one of Cilium's killer features but requires strict mode. The most common mistake is leaving kube-proxy running alongside Cilium KPR — they fight over NodePort handling. For anihpj on AWS: use an NLB with instance target type (not IP), and ensure the worker node security group allows inbound on your NodePort range.
A9
🟢 CATEGORY 1: ARCHITECTURE — Issue A9
Cilium Health Endpoints Reporting Unhealthy

🔍 Symptom: cilium status shows health endpoints as "unhealthy" on some nodes. Cluster appears to work but connectivity test fails on those nodes.

🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. Health endpoint IP unreachable between nodes: Cilium health checks involve cross-node pings. If cross-node communication is broken, health fails. Check with cilium-health status.
  2. Cilium health daemon not running: The cilium-health DaemonSet must run alongside the agent. Check kubectl get pods -n kube-system -l k8s-app=cilium-health.
  3. Node firewall blocking health check ICMP: Health checks use ICMP pings between nodes. Check if ICMP is allowed between all worker nodes.
  4. MTU issue on health endpoint: Health endpoints use the same tunnel/route as regular pods. If MTU is wrong, health pings fail. Check cilium-health status --verbose.
  5. Health endpoint IPAM exhaustion: Each node gets a health IP. If IP pool is exhausted, health endpoint can't be created. Check cilium status | grep "Health IP".
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. Node hostname changed causing health endpoint identity mismatch
  2. cilium-health pod stuck in CrashLoopBackOff due to RBAC issue
  3. Health endpoint BPF program detached after kernel update
  4. Concurrent node join causing health IP collision
  5. IPv6-only cluster with health check configured for IPv4
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. cilium-health DaemonSet not deployed: Some install methods skip it. Verify with kubectl get ds -n kube-system cilium-health.
  2. Cross-node connectivity not established yet: Health checks require functional cross-node pod communication. Fix cross-node issues first.
  3. Health endpoint CIDR conflicts with pod CIDR: Check cilium config | grep health-ipv4-cidr. Must be different from pod CIDR.
  4. Node initialization not complete: Wait for cilium status --wait to show all nodes as "OK".
  5. Cloud provider network not allowing direct pod-to-pod traffic: Some CNIs require cloud-specific routes.
🔍 What to Look At / Take Note Of: Health endpoint is a CANARY -- if unhealthy, cross-node pod traffic is also broken. cilium-health status runs ICMP pings between all nodes. Health IP CIDR must NOT overlap with pod CIDR. ICMP must be allowed between worker nodes.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. cilium-health status — diagnostic output showing per-node health
2. kubectl get pods -n kube-system -l k8s-app=cilium-health — all must be Running
3. cilium connectivity test — health checks are part of the test suite
4. If ICMP blocked: add ICMP allow rule in firewall/security group
5. Restart health daemon: kubectl -n kube-system rollout restart daemonset cilium-health

💡 Personal Advice: Health endpoint issues are usually a canary for deeper cross-node problems. If health endpoints are unhealthy, your cross-node pod communication is likely broken too — the health check is just the first thing to report it. Fix the underlying cross-node issue, and health will recover. For anihpj: don't ignore health endpoint warnings — they predict future pod communication failures.
A10
🟢 CATEGORY 1: ARCHITECTURE — Issue A10
Cilium ConfigMap Changes Not Taking Effect

🔍 Symptom: You update cilium-config ConfigMap (e.g., enable-hubble: "true") but cilium status still shows old values. Changes are ignored.

🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. Agent not restarted after ConfigMap change: Cilium agent reads ConfigMap at startup only. Changes require agent restart. Run kubectl -n kube-system rollout restart daemonset cilium.
  2. ConfigMap key has wrong format: Cilium ConfigMap uses specific key names. Check spelling: "enable-hubble" not "enableHubble", "enable-bpf-masquerade" not "enableBPFMasquerade".
  3. ConfigMap has duplicate keys: If the same key appears twice in the ConfigMap, the last value wins — but may be unexpected. Check kubectl get cm -n kube-system cilium-config -o yaml.
  4. Helm values overriding ConfigMap: If using Helm, values.yaml overrides the ConfigMap on each upgrade. Changes to ConfigMap directly get overwritten. Use helm upgrade --set key=value instead.
  5. Agent using CLI flags instead of ConfigMap: Some settings can be set via DaemonSet args, which take precedence over ConfigMap. Check kubectl get ds -n kube-system cilium -o yaml | grep -A100 "args:".
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. ConfigMap too large — kubelet cannot mount it into pod
  2. Cilium agent reading cached ConfigMap from disk, not the mounted volume
  3. Multiple cilium-config ConfigMaps in different namespaces
  4. ConfigMap update timestamp not detected by kubelet watch
  5. Immutable ConfigMap preventing edits (if --immutable flag was set)
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. ConfigMap not created yet: Cilium creates it on install. If missing, agent uses defaults. Check kubectl get cm -n kube-system cilium-config.
  2. Helm install with --set but ConfigMap shows different values: Helm template may have a bug or override. Check helm get values cilium -n kube-system.
  3. Using cilium CLI install with conflicting Helm release: Mixing CLI and Helm causes config conflicts. Choose one method.
  4. ConfigMap has values for a different Cilium version: Some keys are version-specific. Check compatibility with your Cilium version.
  5. CNI chaining config overriding Cilium settings: If chaining with AWS VPC CNI, some Cilium configs are ignored.
🔍 What to Look At / Take Note Of: Cilium agent does NOT hot-reload ConfigMap -- restart REQUIRED. Helm values override ConfigMap on every upgrade. ConfigMap keys use kebab-case: enable-hubble NOT enableHubble. Agent CLI args take PRECEDENCE over ConfigMap.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. Verify current config: kubectl get cm -n kube-system cilium-config -o yaml
2. After changing ConfigMap, restart agents: kubectl -n kube-system rollout restart daemonset cilium
3. Check per-node config: cilium config --node <node-name>
4. If using Helm: helm upgrade cilium cilium/cilium -n kube-system --reuse-values --set key=value
5. Verify agent picks up config: kubectl logs -n kube-system -l k8s-app=cilium | grep "config"

💡 Personal Advice: The #1 gotcha: Cilium agent does NOT hot-reload ConfigMap changes. You MUST restart the agent after any ConfigMap edit. If you are using Helm (and you should be), never edit the ConfigMap directly — always use helm upgrade. For anihpj: document your Cilium Helm values in your infrastructure repo. Direct ConfigMap edits are unreproducible and will be lost on the next upgrade.
💡 Personal Advice: The #1 gotcha: Cilium agent does NOT hot-reload ConfigMap changes. You MUST restart the agent after any ConfigMap edit. If you are using Helm (and you should be), never edit the ConfigMap directly — always use helm upgrade. For anihpj: document your Cilium Helm values in your infrastructure repo. Direct ConfigMap edits are unreproducible and will be lost on the next upgrade.

🪪 A11 — A15: Identity Allocation Issues

A11
🟢 CATEGORY 1: ARCHITECTURE — Issue A11
Cilium Identity Not Allocated to New Pod

🔍 Symptom: New anihpj pods stay in "ContainerCreating" or start but have no network connectivity. cilium endpoint list shows endpoint in "waiting-for-identity" state.

  Identity Allocation Flow:
  Pod Created → Cilium Agent → kube-apiserver (CRD) → Cilium Operator → Identity Number
                                                                              ↓
  Endpoint stuck at "waiting-for-identity" ← Operator not responding ← ❌ BROKEN
🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. Cilium Operator not running: Operator handles identity allocation. Check kubectl get pods -n kube-system -l name=cilium-operator. Must be Running.
  2. kube-apiserver unreachable from agent: Cilium agent uses CRDs for identity management. Check cilium status | grep "Kubernetes". Should show OK.
  3. CRD not installed: CiliumIdentity and CiliumEndpoint CRDs must exist. Run kubectl get crd | grep cilium. If missing, reinstall Cilium.
  4. Identity table full: Cilium supports ~64k identities. If all are used, new ones fail. Check cilium identity list | wc -l. If near 65535, investigate identity leak.
  5. RBAC preventing agent from creating CiliumIdentity: Check agent ServiceAccount permissions: kubectl auth can-i create ciliumidentities --as=system:serviceaccount:kube-system:cilium.
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. Cilium Operator leader election stalled (multiple operators fighting)
  2. Identity garbage collection deleting identities still in use
  3. etcd performance degradation causing slow CRD operations
  4. NetworkPolicy with identity-based rules causing circular identity dependency
  5. CiliumEndpointSlice (CES) not being created for large deployments
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. Cilium CRDs not installed: Run cilium install --dry-run to see what would be installed. CRDs install first.
  2. Operator Deployment missing: Some minimal installs skip operator. Install with cilium install --set operator.replicas=1.
  3. Agent or operator cannot reach kube-apiserver: Check network connectivity from agent pod to kube-apiserver Service.
  4. Wrong kube-apiserver address in Cilium config: Check cilium config | grep k8s-api-server.
  5. Namespace label missing for identity creation: Some identity modes depend on namespace labels for identity grouping.
🔍 What to Look At / Take Note Of: Cilium Operator is a SINGLE POINT OF FAILURE for identity allocation. Always run 2+ replicas. cilium identity list | wc -l -- max 65535 identities. CRDs must exist: kubectl get crd | grep cilium. RBAC: agent needs create/update on CiliumIdentity.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. kubectl get pods -n kube-system -l name=cilium-operator — operator must be Running
2. kubectl get crd | grep cilium — verify CiliumIdentity, CiliumEndpoint CRDs exist
3. cilium identity list — check identity count and look for orphaned identities
4. kubectl get ciliumidentities -A | wc -l — CRD count must match agent count
5. If operator is down: kubectl -n kube-system rollout restart deployment cilium-operator

💡 Personal Advice: "Waiting for identity" is almost always an operator problem. The operator is a single point of failure for identity allocation — if it's down, NO new pods get identities. Always run at least 2 operator replicas. For anihpj: monitor operator health with a simple kubectl check in your CI pipeline. An operator outage is silent but catastrophic.
A12
🟢 CATEGORY 1: ARCHITECTURE — Issue A12
Identity Mismatch — Pod Gets Wrong Security Identity

🔍 Symptom: anihpj-api pod gets identity 54321 (should be 12345 based on labels). CNP rules that should allow traffic are blocking it. cilium policy trace shows unexpected identity.

🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. Pod labels changed after identity allocation: Identity is derived from pod labels at creation time. If labels change, the identity doesn't update. Delete and recreate the pod.
  2. Multiple label sets with same identity: Cilium groups pods with identical security-relevant labels into one identity. If anihpj-web and anihpj-api share labels, they get the same identity. Add distinguishing labels.
  3. Identity garbage collection not running: Old identity may still be assigned. Check cilium identity list for duplicate label sets.
  4. Reserved identity collision: Identities 1-255 are reserved (e.g., 1=world, 2=cluster). If your pod gets a reserved identity, something is very wrong. Check reserved list: cilium identity list | grep "reserved".
  5. CES (CiliumEndpointSlice) stale: For large clusters, CES may cache old identities. Check kubectl get ces -A and look for stale entries.
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. CiliumEndpoint CRD manually edited causing identity drift
  2. Agent restart during identity allocation — half-allocated identity stuck
  3. Cross-cluster identity sync issue (Cluster Mesh) importing wrong identity
  4. Identity allocation race condition with >100 pods starting simultaneously
  5. Custom identity allocation plugin returning wrong identity
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. Pods created before Cilium agent fully initialized: Early pods may get incorrect identities. Restart them after agent is ready.
  2. Cilium identity allocation mode set to "crd" but CRD not ready: Check cilium config | grep identity-allocation-mode. Default is "crd".
  3. Namespace labels used for identity but not applied yet: Add labels with kubectl label ns anihpj app.kubernetes.io/part-of=anihpj.
  4. Pre-existing CiliumIdentity CRDs from previous install: Old identities may conflict. Clean up: kubectl delete ciliumidentities --all (only on fresh install!).
  5. Helm values for identity-relevant labels misconfigured: Check helm get values cilium -n kube-system | grep identity.
🔍 What to Look At / Take Note Of: Identity is derived from pod LABELS at creation time -- labels changed later do NOT update identity. Pods with identical security-relevant labels SHARE an identity. Reserved identities 1-255 are special (1=world, 2=cluster). Add component: api label.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. cilium identity list — find the identity assigned to your pod's labels
2. kubectl get ciliumendpoint -n anihpj anihpj-api-xxx -o yaml | grep -A5 "identity" — verify endpoint identity
3. cilium policy trace --src-k8s-pod src:pod --dst-k8s-pod anihpj:anihpj-api-xxx --dport 8080 — see which identity is used in policy evaluation
4. If identity is wrong: delete pod to force re-creation with correct identity
5. If labels are shared: add a unique label like cilium.io/ci: api to distinguish identities

💡 Personal Advice: Identity mismatch is subtle because traffic "should" work based on pod labels, but Cilium uses identities — which are derived from labels at a point in time. The golden rule: never change pod labels on a running pod expecting identity to update. Delete the pod. For anihpj: ensure your Deployment labels include a component-specific label (app=anihpj, component=api) so that api and web get different identities.
A13
🟢 CATEGORY 1: ARCHITECTURE — Issue A13
Identity Garbage Collection Deleting Active Identities

🔍 Symptom: Random pods lose connectivity. hubble observe shows DROPPED with "Identity not found". After a few minutes, connectivity returns. The pattern repeats.

🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. Identity GC interval too aggressive: Check cilium config | grep identity-gc-interval. Default is 15 minutes. If set too low, unused identities get cleaned before pods reconnect.
  2. Idle pods losing identity: If a pod has no active connections for > GC interval, its identity may be collected even though the pod is running. The endpoint stays but identity is gone.
  3. CiliumEndpoint GC mismatch: Endpoint GC and identity GC run on different timers. If endpoint GC is slower, you get endpoints with no identity.
  4. Operator identity heartbeat lost: Operator tracks identity usage via heartbeats. If heartbeat is lost (due to network issue), identity is collected prematurely.
  5. CiliumIdentity CRD deleted manually: Someone ran kubectl delete ciliumidentity <id>. The CRD is the source of truth — deleting it removes the identity.
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. Clock skew between agent and operator causing GC timing issues
  2. etcd compaction deleting old identity CRD revisions
  3. Agent running with --identity-gc-interval=0 (disabled) but operator still GCing
  4. Kubernetes finalizer on CiliumIdentity blocking recreation after GC
  5. BPF map for identities (cilium_ipcache) not synced with CRD state
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. Default GC values not appropriate for workload: Tune identity-gc-interval and identity-heartbeat-timeout for your connection patterns.
  2. Too many short-lived pods: If pods churn every 2 minutes with GC at 15 min, identities accumulate. But GC runs and may clean active ones too.
  3. Helm values for operator set incorrectly: Check operator.identityGCInterval and operator.identityHeartbeatTimeout in Helm values.
  4. Multiple Cilium operators in different namespaces: Only one operator should manage identities. Check for duplicate deployments.
  5. Cluster Mesh enabled without identity sync configured: Cross-cluster identities may have different GC policies.
🔍 What to Look At / Take Note Of: Default identity GC interval: 15 minutes. If pods have 15min+ idle periods, their identity may be GC'd. Operator heartbeat tracks identity usage -- network blips cause missed heartbeats. NEVER manually delete CiliumIdentity CRDs while pods are running.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. cilium config | grep identity — check GC interval and heartbeat timeout
2. kubectl get ciliumidentities -A | wc -l — verify identity count is stable
3. kubectl logs -n kube-system -l name=cilium-operator | grep "garbage" — see GC activity
4. Increase GC interval: cilium config set identity-gc-interval 30m
5. Fix heartbeat: cilium config set identity-heartbeat-timeout 30m

💡 Personal Advice: Identity GC killing active identities is a silent catastrophe — pods are running but traffic randomly drops. The default GC interval (15 min) is usually fine, but if your pods have long idle periods (e.g., batch jobs that connect once per hour), increase it. For anihpj: if your web and api pods maintain persistent connections (HTTP keep-alive), identity heartbeat should keep them alive. The issue usually affects pods that only make occasional outbound calls.
A14
🟢 CATEGORY 1: ARCHITECTURE — Issue A14
CiliumEndpoint CRD Not Created for Pod

🔍 Symptom: Pod is Running but kubectl get cep -n anihpj shows no CiliumEndpoint for it. cilium endpoint list doesn't list the pod. Network is not functional.

🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. CNI plugin not invoked: Kubelet calls CNI when creating a pod. If CNI config is missing or wrong, Cilium never sees the pod. Check /etc/cni/net.d/05-cilium.conflist on the node.
  2. Cilium agent disconnected from kube-apiserver: Agent needs API access to create CiliumEndpoint CRD. Check kubectl logs -n kube-system -l k8s-app=cilium | grep "unable to create endpoint".
  3. CNI request timeout: If CNI ADD takes >30s, kubelet times out and retries. Multiple retries may cause the endpoint to be abandoned. Check agent logs for timeout errors.
  4. Pod annotation missing: Cilium uses pod annotations (e.g., io.cilium.network.ipam) to configure IPAM. If annotations are stripped, endpoint creation fails.
  5. RBAC preventing CiliumEndpoint creation in pod namespace: Agent needs permissions in ALL namespaces. Check kubectl auth can-i create ciliumendpoints -n anihpj --as=system:serviceaccount:kube-system:cilium.
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. CiliumEndpoint CRD schema validation rejecting the endpoint spec
  2. kube-apiserver rate limiting causing 429 responses on CRD creation
  3. CNI conflist has wrong socket path for Cilium agent
  4. Pod security policy or PodSecurity admission blocking Cilium's privileged operations
  5. Network namespace creation failure in container runtime (runc/crun)
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. CNI conflist not deployed to node: Cilium DaemonSet should copy it. Check if the init container completed: kubectl describe pod -n kube-system -l k8s-app=cilium | grep "cni".
  2. Cilium CRDs not installed: Run kubectl get crd ciliumendpoints.cilium.io. Must exist before agent starts.
  3. Agent container not running as privileged: CNI operations require privileged mode. Check DaemonSet securityContext.
  4. Kubelet not configured to use Cilium CNI: Check kubelet flags: --network-plugin=cni --cni-conf-dir=/etc/cni/net.d.
  5. Node not labeled for Cilium: Some setups require node labels. Check kubectl get nodes -o yaml | grep cilium.
🔍 What to Look At / Take Note Of: No CEP = CNI plugin was never called. Check init container logs for CNI conflist copy errors. /etc/cni/net.d/05-cilium.conflist must exist. Agent needs RBAC to create CiliumEndpoint in ALL namespaces. CNI ADD timeout is 30s.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. kubectl get cep -n anihpj — check if any CiliumEndpoints exist in the namespace
2. kubectl logs -n kube-system -l k8s-app=cilium --tail=50 | grep -i "endpoint\|cni" — look for errors
3. kubectl describe pod -n anihpj anihpj-web-xxx | grep -i "network\|cni" — check pod events
4. On the node: ls -la /etc/cni/net.d/ — verify 05-cilium.conflist exists
5. If conflist missing: restart Cilium agent pod on that node

💡 Personal Advice: No CiliumEndpoint CRD usually means the CNI plugin was never called. This is a kubelet-to-CNI chain issue, not a Cilium bug. The most common cause on new clusters: the Cilium agent init container failed to copy the CNI conflist to /etc/cni/net.d/. Check init container logs specifically. For anihpj: verify that the node has the correct CNI conflist before creating any pods.
A15
🟢 CATEGORY 1: ARCHITECTURE — Issue A15
CiliumEndpointSlice (CES) Not Updating — Scalability Issues

🔍 Symptom: Large cluster (>1000 pods/node). New CiliumEndpoints are created but identity sync is slow. Hubble shows stale identity mappings. cilium status reports "CES sync: degraded".

🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. CES batching too slow: CES batches CiliumEndpoint updates to reduce kube-apiserver load. Check cilium config | grep ces. Increase ces-max-batch-size and decrease ces-batch-delay.
  2. kube-apiserver overloaded: CES uses CRDs. If kube-apiserver is slow, CES updates queue up. Check kubectl get --raw /metrics | grep apiserver_request_duration.
  3. etcd performance degradation: CRD operations write to etcd. Check etcd metrics: etcdctl endpoint status. High fsync duration = slow CES.
  4. CES max entries per slice too low: Default is 100 endpoints per slice. For very large clusters, increase: cilium config set ces-max-endpoints-per-slice 500.
  5. CES identity sync stuck on one slice: If one CES has a conflict (e.g., duplicate identity), all CES sync stalls. Check kubectl get ces -A for slices stuck in "updating".
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. CES CRD schema too large causing kube-apiserver memory pressure
  2. Network latency between Cilium agent and kube-apiserver
  3. Cilium Operator not handling CES reconciliation fast enough
  4. Kubernetes 1.29+ server-side apply conflicts on CES
  5. Multiple Cilium installations creating duplicate CES CRDs
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. CES feature not enabled: Check cilium config | grep enable-ces. Default is enabled on new installs, but may be off on upgrades.
  2. Kubernetes version too old for CES: CES requires Kubernetes 1.21+. Check kubectl version --short.
  3. etcd cluster undersized for CRD workload: CRD-heavy clusters need SSD-backed etcd with at least 2GB RAM per member.
  4. Cilium Operator not configured for HA: Single operator instance bottlenecks CES processing. Run at least 2 replicas.
  5. kube-apiserver priority and fairness misconfigured: CRD operations may be throttled. Check APF configuration.
🔍 What to Look At / Take Note Of: CES (CiliumEndpointSlice) batches CRD writes -- without it, every endpoint is a separate kube-apiserver call. Default: 100 endpoints per slice. CES requires Kubernetes 1.21+. If apiserver is slow, CES sync degrades.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. cilium config | grep ces — review all CES-related settings
2. kubectl get ces -A | wc -l — verify slice count is reasonable
3. kubectl top nodes and kubectl top pods -n kube-system — check resource pressure
4. Tune CES batch: cilium config set ces-max-batch-size 1000 --restart
5. If etcd is the bottleneck, consider using etcd defragmentation and increasing etcd storage speed

💡 Personal Advice: CES is Cilium's answer to kube-apiserver overload at scale. Without CES, every CiliumEndpoint CRD write is an individual kube-apiserver call — at 1000 pods/node × 100 nodes, that's 100k CRD writes. CES batches them. If CES is slow, the entire identity system degrades. For anihpj: unless you have >500 pods, CES issues are unlikely. But for CCA exam purposes, know that CES batching is the scalability solution.
💡 Personal Advice: CES is Cilium's answer to kube-apiserver overload at scale. Without CES, every CiliumEndpoint CRD write is an individual kube-apiserver call — at 1000 pods/node × 100 nodes, that's 100k CRD writes. CES batches them. If CES is slow, the entire identity system degrades. For anihpj: unless you have >500 pods, CES issues are unlikely. But for CCA exam purposes, know that CES batching is the scalability solution.

💥 A16 — A18: Cilium Agent Crash/Restart Problems

A16
🟢 CATEGORY 1: ARCHITECTURE — Issue A16
Cilium Agent CrashLoopBackOff

🔍 Symptom: Cilium agent pod on one node constantly crashes and restarts. kubectl get pods -n kube-system -l k8s-app=cilium shows CrashLoopBackOff or Error.

🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. Kernel too old or missing features: Cilium requires kernel 5.10+ with specific BPF features. Check uname -r. Run cilium kernel-check (if available) to list required kernel configs.
  2. Conflicting CNI or BPF programs: Previous CNI (Calico, Flannel) left BPF programs or iptables rules. Check bpftool prog list for non-Cilium programs. Clean up old CNI before installing Cilium.
  3. Insufficient host resources: Cilium agent needs memory for BPF maps. Check node memory: kubectl describe node <node> | grep -A5 "Allocated". Increase agent memory limits.
  4. ConfigMap has invalid value: A typo in cilium-config (e.g., "ture" instead of "true") can crash the agent on startup. Check kubectl get cm -n kube-system cilium-config -o yaml for obvious errors.
  5. BPF filesystem not mounted: Cilium mounts /sys/fs/bpf. If the mount fails, agent crashes. Check mount | grep bpf. Should show bpffs on /sys/fs/bpf.
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. SELinux/AppArmor profile blocking BPF system calls (bpf() syscall denied)
  2. Transparent hugepages causing BPF map allocation failure
  3. Cilium agent binary corrupted on disk (rare — re-pull image)
  4. Kernel lockdep triggering false positive and panicking on BPF operations
  5. libbpf version mismatch between agent and host kernel
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. Kernel version not supported: Verify kernel >= 5.10 with uname -r. Older kernels lack required BPF features.
  2. Previous CNI not fully removed: Run iptables -L -n -t nat | wc -l — if thousands of rules, old CNI rules remain. Flush: iptables -F -t nat.
  3. Cilium DaemonSet securityContext wrong: Agent needs privileged: true and hostPID: true. Check DaemonSet spec.
  4. Node has incompatible OS: Windows nodes are not supported by Cilium. Check kubectl get nodes -o wide for OS.
  5. Required kernel modules not loaded: lsmod | grep -E "vxlan|geneve|ip6_tunnel". Load missing modules.
🔍 What to Look At / Take Note Of: ALWAYS check kubectl logs --previous first -- crash reason in last 20 lines. Kernel 5.10+ REQUIRED. bpftool prog list shows leftover BPF programs. mount | grep bpf -- bpffs must be mounted at /sys/fs/bpf. iptables leftovers are #1 crash cause.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. kubectl logs -n kube-system <cilium-pod> --previous — check the crash logs
2. cilium status --node <node> — if agent partially started, this shows what failed
3. kubectl describe pod -n kube-system <cilium-pod> — check for OOMKilled, liveness probe failures
4. On the node: dmesg | grep -i "bpf\|cilium" — kernel-level errors
5. Increase agent resources in Helm: --set resources.limits.memory=1Gi --set resources.requests.cpu=500m

💡 Personal Advice: CrashLoopBackOff in Cilium is 90% a kernel compatibility issue or a leftover CNI conflict. Always check kubectl logs --previous first — the crash reason is almost always in the last 20 lines. For anihpj: if you are migrating from Calico/Flannel to Cilium, wipe the node clean first. Old iptables rules and BPF programs cause the most mysterious crashes.
A17
🟢 CATEGORY 1: ARCHITECTURE — Issue A17
Cilium Agent OOMKilled — Memory Exhaustion

🔍 Symptom: Cilium agent pod is repeatedly killed by OOM killer. kubectl describe pod shows "OOMKilled" with exit code 137. Memory usage grows over time until crash.

🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. BPF conntrack table too large: Each connection needs a conntrack entry (~200 bytes in BPF map + kernel memory). High connection churn = memory growth. Check cilium-dbg bpf ct list global | wc -l.
  2. Hubble flow buffer full: Hubble buffers flows in memory before exporting. If the exporter is slow or disabled, buffer grows unbounded. Check cilium config | grep hubble-buffer.
  3. BPF policy map too large: Complex CNPs with many rules create large BPF policy maps. Check cilium-dbg bpf policy get --all for policy map size.
  4. Too many endpoints on node: Each endpoint consumes agent memory. 1000+ pods/node requires significant memory. Check cilium endpoint list | wc -l.
  5. Memory leak in Cilium version: Some Cilium versions have known memory leaks. Check GitHub issues for your version. Upgrade to latest patch release.
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. Go runtime memory fragmentation (golang GC not releasing to OS)
  2. Kernel memory leak in BPF subsystem (check /proc/meminfo for BPF entries)
  3. Hubble exporting flows to dead relay endpoint (buffers fill up)
  4. Cilium monitor (cilium-dbg monitor) left running consuming memory
  5. TLS certificates for Hubble in memory (large cert chains)
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. Default memory limit too low: Cilium defaults to 512Mi. For production with Hubble, set at least 1Gi. Use Helm: --set resources.limits.memory=1Gi.
  2. Hubble enabled with default buffer size on large cluster: Reduce buffer: --set hubble.eventBufferCapacity=4095 or disable Hubble if not needed.
  3. Prometheus metrics enabled on large cluster: Metric cardinality can consume significant memory. Limit metrics with --set prometheus.metrics="" to disable.
  4. Many policies applied cluster-wide: CCNPs with broad selectors create large policy maps on every node.
  5. Node has many short-lived connections: BPF conntrack entries for TIME_WAIT connections consume memory.
🔍 What to Look At / Take Note Of: OOMKilled = exit code 137. Conntrack table is the #1 memory consumer. Default 512Mi limit is too low for production with Hubble. Each BPF conntrack entry ~200 bytes in kernel + mirror. Connection-per-request apps explode conntrack.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. kubectl describe pod -n kube-system <cilium-pod> | grep OOMKilled — confirm it's OOM
2. Check BPF map sizes: cilium-dbg bpf ct list global | wc -l and cilium-dbg bpf policy get --all | wc -l
3. Increase memory limit: update Helm values or DaemonSet directly
4. Reduce conntrack: cilium config set conntrack-gc-interval 30s for faster cleanup
5. Disable Hubble if not needed: cilium config set enable-hubble false

💡 Personal Advice: OOM in Cilium agent is almost always conntrack bloat. The agent keeps a BPF conntrack table AND a userspace mirror. For anihpj: if you have HTTP microservices with keep-alive connections, that's fine — those are long-lived. But if you have connection-per-request patterns (like database calls without connection pooling), your conntrack table explodes. Fix the application pattern, not just the memory limit.
A18
🟢 CATEGORY 1: ARCHITECTURE — Issue A18
Cilium Agent Fails to Connect to kube-apiserver

🔍 Symptom: Cilium agent logs show "unable to connect to kube-apiserver" or "Unauthorized". Agent starts but cannot sync state. cilium status shows Kubernetes connection as "Disabled" or "Error".

🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. ServiceAccount token expired or missing: Check kubectl get sa -n kube-system cilium. Verify token is mounted: kubectl exec -n kube-system <cilium-pod> -- ls /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/.
  2. RBAC ClusterRole missing permissions: Cilium needs extensive RBAC. Check kubectl describe clusterrole cilium. Verify all required API groups are listed.
  3. kube-apiserver unreachable from node network: Agent uses host network. Test from node: curl -k https://<api-server-ip>:6443/healthz. Check firewall/network policy.
  4. Wrong kube-apiserver address configured: Check cilium config | grep k8s-api-server. If using a non-standard API server address, it must be explicitly set.
  5. TLS certificate verification failure: Agent needs the kube-apiserver CA cert. Check cilium config | grep k8s-client-ca. If using custom CA, it must be in the ConfigMap.
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. kube-apiserver rate limiting agent requests (too many watches)
  2. NetworkPolicy blocking agent's access to kube-apiserver on port 443
  3. Cilium agent using wrong ServiceAccount (multiple SAs in namespace)
  4. kube-apiserver behind a load balancer with session affinity issues
  5. MutatingWebhook modifying Cilium agent pod spec (removing SA token mount)
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. Cilium ServiceAccount not created: Helm/CLI install creates it. Verify: kubectl get sa -n kube-system cilium.
  2. RBAC ClusterRole not installed: Check kubectl get clusterrole cilium. If missing, reinstall Cilium.
  3. ClusterRoleBinding not linking SA to ClusterRole: Check kubectl get clusterrolebinding cilium.
  4. kube-apiserver endpoint not reachable from node: Test connectivity from the node itself, not from within a pod.
  5. Node authorization mode restricting kubelet API access: Check kube-apiserver flags for --authorization-mode.
🔍 What to Look At / Take Note Of: Cilium agent needs EXTENSIVE RBAC -- ClusterRole covers 20+ API groups. Agent uses hostNetwork: true -- check NODE-level connectivity. Private EKS/GKE API endpoints need worker node subnet access. SA token must be mounted at /var/run/secrets.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. kubectl logs -n kube-system <cilium-pod> | grep -i "apiserver\|unauthorized\|connection refused" — find the exact error
2. kubectl auth can-i --list --as=system:serviceaccount:kube-system:cilium — verify RBAC permissions
3. kubectl get clusterrolebinding cilium -o yaml — verify binding exists and references correct SA
4. From the node: curl -k https://<api-server>:6443/healthz — test raw connectivity
5. If all else fails: reinstall Cilium with cilium install to restore RBAC

💡 Personal Advice: kube-apiserver connectivity issues in the agent are almost always RBAC. Cilium needs extensive permissions (CRD management, endpoint creation, identity allocation). Never hand-edit Cilium RBAC — reinstall to get the correct set. For anihpj: if you use a private kube-apiserver endpoint (EKS private endpoint), ensure the worker node subnets can reach it.
💡 Personal Advice: kube-apiserver connectivity issues in the agent are almost always RBAC. Cilium needs extensive permissions (CRD management, endpoint creation, identity allocation). Never hand-edit Cilium RBAC — reinstall to get the correct set. For anihpj: if you use a private kube-apiserver endpoint (EKS private endpoint), ensure the worker node subnets can reach it.

🔧 A19 — A20: Endpoint Creation Failures

A19
🟢 CATEGORY 1: ARCHITECTURE — Issue A19
Endpoint Stuck in "restoring" or "creating" State

🔍 Symptom: cilium endpoint list shows endpoint in "restoring" state for minutes or "creating" indefinitely. Pod is Running but has no network.

  Endpoint States:
  creating → waiting-for-identity → ready (normal: ~2-5 seconds)
  restoring → ready (normal: after agent restart, ~30s for all endpoints)
  
  ❌ STUCK: creating (identity not allocated — A11)
  ❌ STUCK: restoring (agent cannot rebuild BPF state)
  ❌ STUCK: waiting-for-identity (operator down — A11)
🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. Agent restarting while endpoints are restoring: If agent restarts during restore, it starts over. Check kubectl get pods -n kube-system -l k8s-app=cilium for recent restarts.
  2. BPF map corruption during restore: After node reboot, BPF maps may be corrupted if /sys/fs/bpf was not cleanly unmounted. Check dmesg | grep bpf for errors.
  3. CiliumEndpoint CRD has stale state: If the CRD shows an endpoint as "ready" but BPF programs are detached, agent gets confused. Delete the stale CiliumEndpoint CRD and let the pod recreate.
  4. Veth pair already exists from previous endpoint: If an old veth wasn't cleaned up, new endpoint creation fails. Check ip link show | grep lxc for orphaned interfaces.
  5. CNI DEL failed for previous pod: If pod deletion didn't clean up properly (CNI DEL timeout), new pod on same IP fails. Check agent logs for CNI DEL errors.
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. Kernel IP forwarding disabled on node (net.ipv4.ip_forward=0)
  2. Container runtime (containerd) namespace stuck in deleting state
  3. BPF program size exceeding kernel limit (1M instructions max)
  4. cgroup filesystem not mounted — BPF cgroup hooks fail
  5. Node disk full — cannot write BPF map state to /sys/fs/bpf
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. First pod on node takes longer: Agent initializes BPF maps on first endpoint. Expect 30-60s for the first pod.
  2. Cilium agent liveness probe too aggressive: If probe kills agent during first endpoint creation, it restarts and starts over. Increase initialDelaySeconds in DaemonSet.
  3. BPF filesystem permissions wrong: /sys/fs/bpf must be writable by the agent. Check ls -la /sys/fs/bpf on the node.
  4. Kernel lockdown mode preventing BPF writes: Some secure boot configurations restrict BPF. Check dmesg | grep lockdown.
  5. Cilium CNI conflist has wrong chaining mode: If chaining with another CNI, endpoint creation may depend on the other CNI's response.
🔍 What to Look At / Take Note Of: Endpoint restore after agent restart: normal to take 2-3 min for 100+ pods. Stuck restore = state inconsistency between CRD and BPF maps. ip link show | grep lxc -- orphaned veth pairs. First pod on new node takes 30-60s.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. cilium endpoint list — identify stuck endpoints and their state
2. kubectl get cep -n <ns> <pod-name> -o yaml — check CRD state vs. agent state
3. kubectl logs -n kube-system <cilium-pod> | grep "endpoint\|restor" — find restore errors
4. Delete stuck CiliumEndpoint CRD and restart pod
5. If restore is stuck: restart Cilium agent on that node with kubectl -n kube-system delete pod -l k8s-app=cilium --field-selector spec.nodeName=<node>

💡 Personal Advice: Endpoint restore stuck is a recovery race condition. The agent tried to restore state from a previous run but hit an inconsistency. The fix is usually a clean restart. For anihpj: if you have many pods (>100) on a node, endpoint restore after agent restart can take 2-3 minutes. That's normal — the agent is rebuilding BPF programs for every endpoint. Don't panic, just wait.
A20
🟢 CATEGORY 1: ARCHITECTURE — Issue A20
IPAM Exhaustion — No IPs Available for New Pods

🔍 Symptom: New pods stuck in "ContainerCreating" with event: "failed to allocate IP: no more IPs available". cilium status shows "IPAM: 256/256 allocated".

  IPAM Modes:
  ┌──────────────────────┬────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ cluster-pool (default)│ Pre-allocated CIDR per node: /24 (256)│
  │ kubernetes           │ Uses k8s podCIDR range from node spec │
  │ eni (AWS)            │ AWS ENI secondary IPs per instance    │
  │ azure                │ Azure VNet IPs per VM                 │
  │ multi-pool           │ Multiple CIDR pools for segmentation  │
  └──────────────────────┴────────────────────────────────────────┘
🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. Per-node CIDR exhausted: Default cluster-pool allocates /24 per node (256 IPs). If you have >256 pods on a node, IPs run out. Check cilium status | grep "IPAM".
  2. Old CiliumEndpoints not cleaned up: Deleted pods may leave stale CiliumEndpoint CRDs holding IPs. Check kubectl get cep -A | grep -v "ready" for orphaned endpoints.
  3. IPAM mode mismatch with cloud provider: On AWS, use ENI IPAM mode. On Azure, use Azure IPAM. cluster-pool mode may exhaust IPs if not tuned. Check cilium config | grep ipam.
  4. CIDR range too small: Default cluster-pool-ipv4-cidr is 10.0.0.0/8, but if overridden to a smaller range, IPs run out. Check cilium config | grep cluster-pool-ipv4-cidr.
  5. Node CIDR fragmentation: If nodes join/leave frequently, CIDR blocks get fragmented. The IP allocator may have free IPs but they're in wrong blocks. Check kubectl get ciliumnodes -o yaml | grep -A2 "ipam".
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. Cilium Operator not allocating new CIDRs when node pool grows
  2. Cloud API rate limiting preventing ENI/IP allocation (AWS/Azure)
  3. Custom IPAM plugin returning errors for specific pods
  4. CiliumNode CRD not updated with new IP allocations
  5. Duplicate IP detection causing IP to be marked as in-use
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. Pod CIDR not configured: Set --set ipam.mode=cluster-pool --set ipam.operator.clusterPoolIPv4PodCIDRList=10.0.0.0/16 in Helm.
  2. Per-node CIDR mask too restrictive: Default is /24 per node. For high-density nodes, increase: --set ipam.operator.clusterPoolIPv4MaskSize=22 (1024 IPs per node).
  3. Cloud IPAM mode not set on cloud provider: AWS needs --set ipam.mode=eni. Azure needs --set ipam.mode=azure.
  4. Multiple IP pools not configured for multi-tenant: Set up multi-pool IPAM for namespace-level IP allocation.
  5. Cilium Operator not running: Operator allocates per-node CIDRs. Must be Running with at least 1 replica.
🔍 What to Look At / Take Note Of: Default cluster-pool: /24 per node = 256 IPs. Sidecar containers consume IPs too. Stale CEP CRDs hold IPs after pod deletion. AWS ENI mode: limited by ENI attachments per instance. Azure: limited by NIC IPs per VM. cilium status | grep IPAM shows % used.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. cilium status | grep IPAM — check allocation percentage per node
2. kubectl get cep -A --no-headers | wc -l — count actual endpoints vs. allocated IPs
3. kubectl get ciliumnodes -o yaml | grep -A20 "ipam" — see per-node IP pools
4. Clean up stale CEPs: kubectl get cep -A | grep -v "ready" | awk '{print $1,$2}' | xargs -I{} sh -c 'kubectl delete cep -n {}'
5. Increase per-node CIDR: helm upgrade cilium cilium/cilium --set ipam.operator.clusterPoolIPv4MaskSize=22

💡 Personal Advice: IPAM exhaustion is the silent killer of pod scaling. You won't notice until the 257th pod on a node fails to start. For anihpj: the default /24 per node (256 IPs) is usually plenty for most workloads. But if you run sidecar-heavy pods (2 containers per pod = 2 IPs) or use Istio with sidecars, plan accordingly. Always run cilium status after a scale-up event to check IPAM headroom.
💡 Personal Advice: IPAM exhaustion is the silent killer of pod scaling. You won't notice until the 257th pod on a node fails to start. For anihpj: the default /24 per node (256 IPs) is usually plenty for most workloads. But if you run sidecar-heavy pods (2 containers per pod = 2 IPs) or use Istio with sidecars, plan accordingly. Always run cilium status after a scale-up event to check IPAM headroom.

🛡️ Category 2: Network PolicyP1-P18

18 troubleshooting issues covering CiliumNetworkPolicy enforcement, L7 HTTP/DNS/Kafka/gRPC rules, policy audit mode, host firewall CCNP, and CiliumCIDRGroup management.

📦 P1 — P5: CNP Not Enforcing

🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. endpointSelector mismatch: Policy selects pods by labels. Run kubectl get pods -l <selector>. If no pods match, policy does nothing.
  2. Policy in wrong namespace: CNP is namespaced. A CNP in 'default' does NOT affect pods in 'anihpj'. Verify with kubectl get cnp -n anihpj.
  3. Policy status not enforced: Check cilium-dbg bpf policy get <id>. If 'Disabled', agent hasn't processed it. Restart agent.
  4. CiliumClusterwideNetworkPolicy needed: For cluster-wide rules or host-level rules, use CCNP not CNP.
  5. CRD schema validation failed: Check kubectl describe cnp <name> for events. Use cilium policy validate file.yaml.
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. Policy version conflict (two policies with same name in different API versions)
  2. CiliumEndpoint CRD not updated with new policy revision
  3. Agent watch on CNP CRD broken — missed create event
  4. Policy with empty ingress AND egress (implicit deny but no explicit allow)
  5. Kubernetes NetworkPolicy with same selector overriding Cilium behavior
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. CNP CRD not installed: Check kubectl get crd ciliumnetworkpolicies.cilium.io. If missing, Cilium wasn't installed correctly.
  2. No default-deny: New cluster has no policies — all traffic allowed. Apply default-deny first.
  3. Agent not watching namespace: Check cilium config | grep policy. Must be 'default' or 'always'.
  4. Wrong API version: CNP v1 vs v2 have different schemas. Check Cilium version compatibility.
  5. Helm disables policy: Check cilium config | grep enable-policy. Must be 'default' or 'always'.
🔍 What to Look At: cilium policy get shows Enabled vs Disabled. kubectl describe cnp has Status field. Selector matching 0 pods = silently inactive.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. cilium policy get — verify policy enforced
2. kubectl get pods -l <selector> -A — verify selector matches
3. cilium-dbg bpf policy get <id> — check BPF policy map
4. hubble observe --verdict DROPPED — verify drops
5. If not in BPF: restart agent pod on affected node

💡 Personal Advice: The #1 reason CNPs 'don't work' is selector mismatch. Run kubectl get pods -l with the exact selector BEFORE applying. For anihpj: add policy-group: api label to pods for trivial selectors.
P2
🛡️ CATEGORY 2: NETWORK POLICY — Issue P2
CNP Blocks Traffic But Should Allow

🔍 Symptom: CNP applied but legitimate anihpj-web→anihpj-api traffic blocked. Hubble shows DROPPED. Policy has allow rule but it's not matching.

🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. Rule ordering: Rules evaluated top-to-bottom. Deny before allow = blocked. Check YAML order.
  2. fromEndpoints label mismatch: Source pod labels changed. Verify labels on both source and destination.
  3. namespaceSelector missing: fromEndpoints without namespaceSelector only matches SAME namespace. Add namespaceSelector: {} for all.
  4. Protocol/port mismatch: Rule says port 8080 but app listens on 8443. Check kubectl get svc --show-labels.
  5. Policy union confusion: Cilium uses UNION for CNP+KNP. KNP allow + CNP deny = ALLOW (union wins).
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. L7 rule redirecting to proxy but proxy not running
  2. toFQDNs with DNS not resolved (fails first, works later)
  3. fromCIDR but pod IP not in that CIDR
  4. CCNP and CNP conflicting on same endpoint
  5. Policy applied to wrong direction (ingress on source instead of dest)
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. No allow rules: New cluster all-allowed. First CNP with ANY deny blocks everything. Create allows first.
  2. Default-deny before allows: Apply allow rules FIRST, then default-deny. Order matters.
  3. Labels not standardized: Pods need consistent labels (app=anihpj, component=api).
  4. Wrong namespace in trace: cilium policy trace needs correct source/dest namespaces.
  5. Cluster Mesh policies: If Cluster Mesh active, imported policies from other clusters apply.
🔍 What to Look At: Trace shows FINAL verdict + matching rule. Union: KNP+CNP = ALLOW if either allows. namespaceSelector REQUIRED for cross-ns. Rule order matters in CNP.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. cilium policy trace --src-k8s-pod ns:src --dst-k8s-pod ns:dst --dport X
2. Review YAML rule order
3. hubble observe --verdict DROPPED --from-pod ns/src
4. Verify labels: kubectl get pods --show-labels
5. Test with audit mode first

💡 Personal Advice: Use cilium policy trace BEFORE deploying — it shows exact matching rule. For anihpj: keep a debug namespace with no policies for connectivity testing.
P3
🛡️ CATEGORY 2: NETWORK POLICY — Issue P3
Default-Deny Blocks All Traffic Including DNS

🔍 Symptom: Default-deny CNP applied. All pods lose connectivity — even DNS stops. Nothing can reach anything.

🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. DNS rule missing: Default-deny blocks EVERYTHING including DNS (UDP 53). FIRST egress rule MUST allow DNS to kube-dns.
  2. No allow rules at all: Policy with empty ingress:[] means deny all. Need explicit allows.
  3. Health probes blocked: Kubelet liveness/readiness probes blocked. Add toEntities: [host].
  4. kube-apiserver blocked: Pods need kube-apiserver access. Add toEntities: [kube-apiserver].
  5. CoreDNS cross-namespace: CoreDNS in kube-system. CNP in anihpj must have egress to kube-system labels.
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. CNP with no egress blocks outbound but ingress from others still reaches
  2. Agent health endpoint blocked causing unhealthy agent
  3. Prometheus scraping blocked
  4. Sidecar mesh communication blocked on different ports
  5. CNI meta-plugin communication blocked
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. No baseline: Start permissive, lock down gradually. Don't default-deny on day 0.
  2. No DNS template: Always deploy standard 'allow-dns' CNP before restrictive policies.
  3. Missing fromEntities: [cluster]: Allow all cluster-internal traffic as baseline.
  4. CCNP too broad: CCNP default-deny affects ALL namespaces. Use namespaced CNP.
  5. No audit testing: Apply with policyAudit: true first to see what would break.
🔍 What to Look At: Hubble shows DNS drops as UDP/53 to 10.96.0.10. Trace with --dport 53 shows DENIED. Always include DNS allow as rule #1 in any restrictive policy.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. Add DNS allow FIRST: egress to kube-dns UDP 53
2. Add cluster: fromEntities: [cluster] for ingress
3. Add egress: toEntities: [cluster] for outbound
4. Add kube-apiserver: toEntities: [kube-apiserver]
5. cilium policy trace before applying

💡 Personal Advice: The DNS lesson is painful. Default-deny = everything blocked, including DNS. For anihpj: create dns-allow.yaml and ALWAYS deploy it before any restrictive CNP.
P4
🛡️ CATEGORY 2: NETWORK POLICY — Issue P4
L3/L4 Works But L7 Rules Ignored

🔍 Symptom: CNP with L3/L4 and L7 rules. Port blocking works but HTTP method/path filtering ignored — all methods pass through.

🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. L7 proxy not enabled: L7 requires Envoy. Check cilium status | grep Proxy. Enable with --set proxy.enabled=true.
  2. L7 rule syntax error: Rules under toPorts: [{ports: [...], rules: {http: [...]}}]. Wrong nesting silently fails.
  3. Proxy redirection not active: cilium-dbg bpf proxy list empty. Restart agent after enabling proxy.
  4. Port mismatch L3 vs L7: L7 rules only apply to port they're nested under. Check correct port.
  5. HTTP/2 or gRPC traffic: L7 HTTP rules only inspect HTTP/1.1. HTTP/2/gRPC need dedicated rules.
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. Envoy proxy crash or resource exhaustion
  2. Too many L7 policies causing proxy config timeout
  3. TLS traffic — L7 inspection needs TLS termination
  4. Kafka L7 needs specific Kafka proxy config
  5. Proxy listener port conflict with service port
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. Proxy not in default install: Add --set proxy.enabled=true on fresh install.
  2. Envoy image unavailable: Check kubectl get pods -n kube-system -l app=envoy.
  3. Kernel too old for socket redirect: L7 proxy needs kernel 5.7+ for optimal redirect.
  4. Mixed L3/L7 syntax: New users confuse CNP and CCNP L7 formats. Check examples.
  5. No test traffic: L7 activates only when traffic hits the protected port. Generate traffic.
🔍 What to Look At: Proxy must be 'Running'. Hubble shows HTTP details only through proxy. gRPC needs separate rules. L7 rules nested under correct toPorts entry.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. cilium status | grep Proxy
2. cilium-dbg bpf proxy list
3. hubble observe --protocol HTTP
4. Check CNP YAML nesting
5. Test: curl -X GET svc/api vs curl -X POST

💡 Personal Advice: L7 adds ~1-2ms latency per request through Envoy. Don't use L7 when L3/L4 suffices. For anihpj: L3/L4 for service-to-service, L7 only for method/path control like blocking POST /admin.
P5
🛡️ CATEGORY 2: NETWORK POLICY — Issue P5
NetworkPolicy Rules Work Intermittently

🔍 Symptom: CNP sometimes blocks, sometimes allows. Same curl works 3/5 times. Hubble shows mixed FORWARDED/DROPPED.

🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. toFQDNs DNS TTL: FQDN resolves to IP. When TTL expires, new IP may differ. Old IP still allowed until re-resolution.
  2. Policy order race: Multiple policies competing. BPF map evaluation order determines behavior.
  3. Pod restart identity change: New identity on restart. Policy may block until new identity syncs.
  4. CES identity sync lag: CiliumEndpointSlice batch delay. Stale identities cause intermittent blocks.
  5. DNS cache inconsistency: Between re-resolution cycles, cached IPs differ from actual. First=allowed, second=blocked.
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. BPF conntrack timeout between requests causing re-evaluation
  2. LB sending to different backends with different identities
  3. Hubble sampling making it appear intermittent
  4. fromCIDR with changing pod IPs (CIDR static, IPs change)
  5. NodeLocal DNSCache bypassing Cilium DNS proxy
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. toFQDNs not resolved: First connections to FQDN may fail until DNS resolved. Retry.
  2. Agent version mismatch: Different agent versions causing policy sync issues.
  3. Incomplete rollout: Not all agents received updated policy. cilium policy wait <rev>.
  4. EndpointSlice not updated: Service endpoints changed but not propagated.
  5. Multiple CNPs same selector: Overlapping rules cause evaluation ordering issues.
🔍 What to Look At: Run trace multiple times. Same result = stable. Different = race. DNS cache: cilium-dbg fqdn cache list. Identity: check after pod restart.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. hubble observe --verdict DROPPED -o json — patterns
2. cilium policy trace multiple times
3. cilium-dbg bpf policy get <id> — revision check
4. toFQDNs: cilium-dbg fqdn cache list
5. kubectl get cep -A | grep <pod>

💡 Personal Advice: Intermittent = DNS (toFQDNs) or identity sync lag. For anihpj: avoid toFQDNs for internal services — use ClusterIP or CIDRGroup instead.

📋 P6 — P10: L7 Policy Issues

P6
📋 CATEGORY 2: NETWORK POLICY — Issue P6
HTTP L7 Rule Not Matching Specific Path

🔍 Symptom: HTTP L7 rule with path '/api/.*' matching all paths including /admin. Regex not working as expected.

🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. RE2 regex, not PCRE: Cilium uses RE2. No backreferences, no lookaheads. /api/.* matches /api AND /api/admin. Use /api/[^/]+$ for exact.
  2. Missing anchors: Without ^ and $, pattern matches anywhere. Use ^/api$ for exact match.
  3. Headers ANDed: Multiple header conditions are ANDed. Both must match.
  4. Method without path: method: GET without path matches ALL GET requests. Add path restriction.
  5. Wrong toPorts entry: Each toPorts with L7 creates separate proxy listener. Rules in one don't apply to another.
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. HTTP/2 not inspected (binary to HTTP parser)
  2. Encoded URLs (%2F) not matching regex
  3. Query params in path (strip ? and after)
  4. Case sensitivity in header values
  5. Request body exceeding proxy buffer
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. RE2 unfamiliar: Practice RE2. No negative lookaheads. Use [^/]+ instead.
  2. Testing without anchors: Always test regex with ^...$ anchors.
  3. HTTP/2 lowercases headers: Use lowercase in policy rules for HTTP/2.
  4. Multiple L7 policies merged: All L7 rules for a port merge. Check for conflicts.
  5. No Hubble verification: Use hubble observe --http-path /test to verify.
🔍 What to Look At: RE2 ≠ PCRE. No lookaheads. Hubble shows ACTUAL matched path. Headers must match EXACTLY. Test regex with ^...$ anchors.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. Test regex externally: echo '/api/users' | grep -E '^/api/[^/]+$'
2. hubble observe --protocol HTTP --http-path /api
3. Check merged: cilium-dbg bpf policy get <id>
4. Start simple: method-only, then add path
5. cilium policy trace with simulated request

💡 Personal Advice: Path regex is #1 L7 headache. Test externally first. For anihpj: use TWO rules — allow GET /api/.*, deny POST /admin/.*. Deny-allow ordering critical.
P7
📋 CATEGORY 2: NETWORK POLICY — Issue P7
DNS Policy Not Resolving Specific Domains

🔍 Symptom: toFQDNs policy allows api.github.com but DNS fails. Hubble shows DNS queries dropped or NXDOMAIN.

🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. matchName vs matchPattern: matchName: 'api.github.com' = EXACT. matchPattern: '*.github.com' = wildcard. Wrong type silently fails.
  2. DNS proxy not intercepting: Check cilium status | grep DNS. DNS proxy must be enabled.
  3. CoreDNS bypassed: NodeLocal DNSCache or custom resolv.conf bypasses Cilium DNS proxy.
  4. TCP/53 blocked: Large DNS responses use TCP/53. Allow both UDP and TCP/53.
  5. toFQDNs under wrong section: toFQDNs is egress only. Putting under ingress silently fails.
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. DNS cache TTL expired and re-resolution blocked
  2. CNAME chain too long (each hop needs policy match)
  3. DNS IP change but old IP cached
  4. L7 DNS conflicting with L3 DNS (L3 allows all, L7 restricts)
  5. DNS proxy rate limiting
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. DNS proxy not enabled: toFQDNs requires DNS proxy. --set dnsProxy.enabled=true.
  2. No CoreDNS allow: Pod needs egress to CoreDNS UDP/TCP 53 even with toFQDNs.
  3. matchPattern wrong: Pattern is glob: *.example.com (not regex). Only * supported.
  4. FQDN in wrong namespace: Cross-namespace toFQDNs needs CCNP.
  5. DNS timeout before proxy ready: First lookup may timeout. Retry.
🔍 What to Look At: matchName = exact. matchPattern = glob (*.example.com). DNS proxy must be OK. Hubble shows DNS QTypes. DNS cache shows resolved IPs.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. hubble observe --protocol DNS --from-pod ns/pod
2. cilium-dbg fqdn cache list
3. kubectl exec pod -- nslookup api.github.com
4. Verify matchPattern uses * wildcard
5. cilium status | grep DNS

💡 Personal Advice: toFQDNs is powerful but tricky. DNS proxy MUST intercept. For critical APIs, use CiliumCIDRGroup instead — more reliable than DNS-dependent policies.
P8
📋 CATEGORY 2: NETWORK POLICY — Issue P8
Kafka L7 Policy Not Filtering Messages

🔍 Symptom: CNP with Kafka L7 rules (topic filtering) applied but all messages pass regardless of topic.

🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. Kafka proxy not configured: Kafka L7 needs dedicated proxy. Enable: --set kafka.enabled=true.
  2. Topic name case-sensitive: 'Orders' ≠ 'orders'. Verify exact Kafka topic name.
  3. API version mismatch: Cilium Kafka inspection works with API versions 0-2. Newer may not parse.
  4. Non-standard port: Kafka default 9092. If using different port, add explicit port in rules.
  5. SASL/SSL encrypted: Proxy must terminate TLS to inspect. Configure TLS certs in Cilium.
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. Message batching causing incomplete data to proxy
  2. Multiple brokers — proxy only intercepts targeted broker
  3. ZooKeeper separate from Kafka (not inspected)
  4. Client library version incompatible with proxy
  5. Proxy buffer overflow on large messages
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. Kafka not default: Install with --set kafka.enabled=true.
  2. Proxy pod not running: Check kubectl get pods -n kube-system | grep kafka.
  3. CNP syntax: rules: {kafka: [{role: 'consume', topic: 'orders'}]}. Role: consume/produce.
  4. No real traffic: Kafka proxy activates on Kafka protocol. Generate real traffic.
  5. No Kafka in test env: Set up test Kafka pod if needed.
🔍 What to Look At: Kafka proxy separately enabled. Topic case-sensitive exact match. Role: consume/produce. Hubble shows Kafka protocol. SASL/SSL needs TLS termination.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. kubectl logs -n kube-system -l app=cilium-envoy | grep kafka
2. hubble observe --protocol KAFKA
3. Verify topic: kubectl exec kafka-pod -- kafka-topics --list
4. cilium-dbg bpf proxy list | grep kafka
5. Test with kafka-console-producer/consumer

💡 Personal Advice: Kafka L7 is niche. For CCA: know it exists with topic/role filtering. For anihpj: unless jobpost uses Kafka, stick with HTTP L7.
P9
📋 CATEGORY 2: NETWORK POLICY — Issue P9
gRPC L7 Policy Not Working

🔍 Symptom: CNP with gRPC L7 rules applied but all gRPC methods pass through unfiltered.

🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. gRPC under http section: gRPC rules must use rules: {grpc: [...]}, NOT rules: {http: [...]}.
  2. Method path format: gRPC method is /package.Service/Method. Must match exactly.
  3. HTTP/2 required: gRPC uses HTTP/2. Proxy must support HTTP/2.
  4. TLS termination needed: Encrypted gRPC needs TLS termination at proxy to inspect.
  5. Streaming not supported: Some gRPC streaming types may not be supported. Check Cilium version.
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. Proto file unavailable for method parsing
  2. gRPC-web different protocol (needs separate handling)
  3. Reflection calls blocked (different methods)
  4. LB stripping HTTP/2 headers
  5. gRPC deadline exceeded before policy evaluation
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. gRPC support version: gRPC L7 added in Cilium 1.12+. Check cilium version.
  2. HTTP/2 proxy: gRPC uses same Envoy but needs HTTP/2 support.
  3. Method discovery: grpcurl svc:50051 list to see actual methods.
  4. Service connection: gRPC client must connect through service, not pod IP.
  5. mTLS disabling inspection: mTLS requires both client and server certs at proxy.
🔍 What to Look At: grpc key (not http). Method: /Package.Service/Method. HTTP/2 required. Hubble shows gRPC protocol. TLS termination needed.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. grpcurl svc:50051 describe
2. hubble observe --protocol gRPC
3. CNP: use grpc: key, not http:
4. Enable HTTP/2 in proxy
5. Test without TLS first

💡 Personal Advice: gRPC L7 is more niche than Kafka. For CCA: know grpc vs http syntax. For anihpj: use L3/L4 based on service accounts — simpler.
P10
📋 CATEGORY 2: NETWORK POLICY — Issue P10
L7 Policy Performance Degradation

🔍 Symptom: After L7 policies enabled, request latency +10-20ms. Pod CPU higher. Some requests timeout under load.

🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. Envoy resource limits: Too many L7 listeners consume CPU/memory. kubectl top pods -n kube-system | grep envoy.
  2. Connection pool exhaustion: Envoy pools to backends. Full pool = queuing. Increase pool size.
  3. Too many L7 rules: 100+ rules = significant regex overhead. Consolidate.
  4. TLS termination CPU: Crypto per connection adds 1-5ms.
  5. Proxy buffer too small: Large headers/bodies overflow buffer. Increase proxy buffer.
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. BPF redirect to proxy adds ~0.5ms
  2. Hubble export competing for CPU
  3. BPF policy check before redirect adds latency
  4. Multiple proxies (ingress + sidecar) doubling
  5. Proxy health check failures causing resets
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. Start L3/L4 only: New cluster — use L3/L4 until L7 needed. Each L7 rule adds latency.
  2. Proxy resources low: Default 128Mi/0.1 CPU may be too low. Increase: --set proxy.resources.limits.cpu=1.
  3. Specific vs broad regex: /api/v1/users faster than /api/.*.
  4. Disable Hubble if unused: Hubble capture adds overhead.
  5. Proxy metrics: Enable: --set proxy.prometheus.enabled=true.
🔍 What to Look At: Envoy CPU/memory. Listener count: cilium-dbg bpf proxy list | wc -l. Hubble latency shows proxy hop. Consolidate patterns.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. kubectl top pods -n kube-system -l app=envoy
2. cilium status | grep Proxy
3. Reduce rules: consolidate regex
4. Increase proxy resources
5. Consider Ingress/Gateway API instead of per-pod proxy

💡 Personal Advice: L7 proxy latency is the price of deep inspection. For anihpj: keep L7 rules minimal. 10ms may be acceptable for security but not real-time. Measure BEFORE and AFTER.

🔊 P11 — P13: Policy Audit Mode

P11
🔊 CATEGORY 2: NETWORK POLICY — Issue P11
Policy Audit Mode Shows No Logs

🔍 Symptom: CNP with policyAudit:true but Hubble shows no DROPPED flows. Audit mode seems not working.

🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. Hubble filtering hides drops: Use hubble observe --verdict DROPPED with NO other filters.
  2. Policy not denying: If policy would allow anyway, nothing to audit. Audit only logs WOULD-BE denies.
  3. Hubble relay down: cilium status | grep Relay. Relay must be connected.
  4. Audit on wrong policy: policyAudit: true must be on the specific policy, not a different one.
  5. Event buffer full: High traffic fills buffer. Increase hubble-event-queue-size.
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. Audit only works with CNP, not Kubernetes NetworkPolicy
  2. Hubble TLS expired preventing connection
  3. Multiple Hubble instances (UI ≠ CLI)
  4. Events to different peer
  5. Kernel dropping BPF events under load
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. Hubble not enabled: Audit requires Hubble. cilium config | grep enable-hubble = true.
  2. Syntax: policyAudit: true at top level of CNP spec, not under rules.
  3. No denies on new cluster: If nothing denies, audit shows nothing. Create test deny.
  4. Hubble observe flags: Default shows FORWARDED only. Add --verdict DROPPED.
  5. Relay not deployed: cilium hubble enable --relay.
🔍 What to Look At: Hubble shows 'DROPPED (Audit)' vs 'DROPPED (Policy)'. Use -o json for reason. Audit only logs DENIED flows, not ALLOWED.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. hubble observe --verdict DROPPED -n anihpj
2. kubectl get cnp -o yaml | grep policyAudit
3. Test: apply CNP denying port 9999
4. curl svc:9999 — should appear DROPPED
5. cilium status | grep Hubble

💡 Personal Advice: Audit mode is your safety net. Deploy in audit 24-48h before enforcing. For anihpj: audit lets you see what WOULD break without actually breaking production.
P12
🔊 CATEGORY 2: NETWORK POLICY — Issue P12
Switching Audit to Enforce Breaks Traffic

🔍 Symptom: Policy in audit for days showing no drops. Switched to enforce — immediately broke production. False confidence from audit.

🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. Audit only shows THAT policy: Multiple policies. Audit on one doesn't show what OTHER policies deny. Union matters.
  2. Hubble filters hid drops: Your observe command may have filtered out some drops. Use unfiltered.
  3. Race on switch: Removing audit + reapply = brief gap where old policy removed, new not active.
  4. DNS behavior differs: Audit allows DNS so FQDNs resolve. Enforce blocks DNS so FQDNs can't resolve.
  5. Policy edited between: Different revision between audit and enforce. Rules changed.
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. Sidecar/init container traffic not in Hubble (short-lived)
  2. Health probes blocked but missed in audit window
  3. Metrics scraping blocked (periodic, may miss)
  4. CRD lag: some nodes still running old policy
  5. Hubble sampling hiding some flows
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. No test traffic: New cluster with no traffic — audit shows nothing. Generate test traffic.
  2. Audit too short: 24-48h minimum. Some traffic is daily/weekly (cronjobs, batches).
  3. Only checking ingress: Policy has both directions. Audit applies to both.
  4. UI vs CLI: UI has time limits. CLI with --since 72h captures more.
  5. Multiple ns not checked: Policy in ns-A may audit fine, but ns-B policy also affects.
🔍 What to Look At: Unfiltered Hubble: NO flags on observe. Check ALL policies (cnp+ccnp). DNS behavior differs audit vs enforce. Switch one policy at a time.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. Before switch: hubble observe --verdict DROPPED --since 72h NO filters
2. kubectl get cnp,ccnp -A — ALL policies
3. Switch one at a time, wait 30min
4. Have rollback ready
5. Monitor: watch hubble observe --verdict DROPPED

💡 Personal Advice: Audit is probability, not guarantee. Longer audit = more confidence but never 100%. For anihpj: switch during maintenance window with rollback plan.
P13
🔊 CATEGORY 2: NETWORK POLICY — Issue P13
Audit Mode Performance Impact

🔍 Symptom: Audit mode on many policies causes agent CPU spike and Hubble flow loss. 'event queue full, dropping events' in logs.

🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. Event generation rate: Every WOULD-BE denied packet = Hubble event. High traffic = millions/sec. Overwhelms buffer.
  2. Buffer too small: Default 4095 events. Under audit with high traffic, fills in ms. Increase to 65535.
  3. Multiple audit policies: 5 policies = 5x events. Audit one at a time.
  4. Relay bottleneck: Relay aggregates from all agents. Undersized relay = backpressure.
  5. perf ring buffer overflow: Kernel-side BPF events overflow before reaching agent.
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. TLS encryption overhead on event transmission
  2. Agent Go GC from event allocation
  3. Disk I/O for Hubble Timescape
  4. Network bandwidth agent→relay
  5. Prometheus cardinality from per-flow metrics
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. Audit on default-deny: NEVER audit default-deny on busy cluster — generates events for ALL traffic.
  2. No rate limiting: Hubble doesn't rate-limit. Traffic spike = event storm.
  3. All policies in audit: Audit one policy at a time, not all.
  4. Relay not scaled: Single relay for large cluster. Increase replicas/resources.
  5. No retention: Events accumulate. Set up consumer or Timescape.
🔍 What to Look At: Event queue depth. Agent CPU during audit. Audit scope: specific pods/ports. Relay CPU/memory. Sampling for high-traffic envs.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. Limit scope: audit specific ports/protocols
2. Increase buffer: hubble-event-queue-size: 65535
3. Scale relay: more replicas/resources
4. cilium-dbg bpf events list | wc -l
5. Flow sampling: capture 1/N flows

💡 Personal Advice: Audit is for VALIDATION, not constant monitoring. Turn on, observe 24-48h, enforce. For anihpj: audit during low traffic with specific scope.

🔥 P14 — P16: Host Firewall Problems

P14
🔥 CATEGORY 2: NETWORK POLICY — Issue P14
Host Firewall CCNP Blocks SSH to Nodes

🔍 Symptom: CCNP for host firewall applied. SSH to worker nodes blocked. Administrators locked out.

🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. nodeSelector matches all: nodeSelector: {} = ALL nodes. Use specific labels.
  2. No SSH allow: Must include: fromEntities: [world] toPorts: [{ports: [{port: '22'}]}].
  3. fromEntities not set: Host uses fromEntities (world, cluster, host, remote-node, kube-apiserver).
  4. Control-plane affected: CCNP on master nodes blocks kube-apiserver. Exclude control-plane.
  5. Kubelet port 10250 blocked: Kube-apiserver needs kubelet access. Blocking breaks node management.
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. Node IP change causing CCNP to match different nodes
  2. Cilium health ICMP blocked
  3. CNI agent communication blocked
  4. NodePort traffic blocked
  5. Cloud metadata (169.254.169.254) blocked
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. hostFirewall not enabled: CCNP nodeSelector needs hostFirewall.enabled=true in Helm.
  2. CCNP syntax: nodeSelector not endpointSelector. fromEntities not fromEndpoints.
  3. No baseline allows: Start with SSH, kubelet, Cilium allows before denies.
  4. Testing from wrong source: Test from world (external), not cluster (internal). Different entities.
  5. Node labels missing: kubectl label node worker-1 host-firewall=enabled.
🔍 What to Look At: nodeSelector (not endpointSelector). fromEntities: world, cluster, host, remote-node, kube-apiserver, ingress. SSH=22, kubelet=10250.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. kubectl get ccnp -o yaml — verify nodeSelector
2. cilium-dbg bpf policy get --host
3. Add SSH: fromEntities world port 22
4. Add kubelet: port 10250 from kube-apiserver
5. hubble observe --from-entity world --to-port 22 --verdict DROPPED

💡 Personal Advice: Host firewall is powerful but dangerous. One wrong CCNP = lockout from ALL nodes. Test on single non-critical node first. For anihpj: skip host firewall unless you truly need host-level protection.
P15
🔥 CATEGORY 2: NETWORK POLICY — Issue P15
Host Firewall Not Enforcing on Specific Nodes

🔍 Symptom: CCNP with nodeSelector applied but only enforces on some nodes. Others matching same selector show no rules.

🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. Labels missing on nodes: kubectl get nodes --show-labels. Must match EXACTLY.
  2. Agent version mismatch: Host firewall needs Cilium 1.12+. Older agents ignore CCNP.
  3. hostFirewall not enabled: Must be enabled cluster-wide AND node has labels. Check ConfigMap.
  4. CCNP not synced: cilium policy wait <rev>. Some agents lag.
  5. matchExpressions: Complex selectors may not evaluate correctly. Use simple matchLabels.
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. Agent restart cleared in-memory policy
  2. CRD watch broken for specific agent
  3. Node renamed — selector misses it
  4. Tainted nodes (doesn't affect CCNP but may affect agent)
  5. Custom agent config overriding host firewall
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
  1. Not in initial install: Check helm get values cilium | grep hostFirewall.
  2. Labels AFTER CCNP: Add labels before CCNP. If CCNP first, selects nothing.
  3. CCNP in namespace: CCNP is cluster-scoped. Don't use -n flag.
  4. Testing wrong node: SSH to specific node: cilium-dbg bpf policy get --host.
  5. Masters excluded: Some setups skip Cilium on masters. Check agent presence.
🔍 What to Look At: EXACT label match per node. Per-node BPF check. Agent >= v1.12. CCNP cluster-scoped. hostFirewall.enabled=true in ConfigMap.
🔧 How to Solve:

1. kubectl get nodes --show-labels | grep <label>
2. Per-node: cilium-dbg bpf policy get --host
3. kubectl describe ccnp <name>
4. Check agent versions per node
5. Restart agent on non-enforcing nodes

💡 Personal Advice: Label issues = #1 host firewall inconsistency. For anihpj: use single label cilium-host-fw=true on ALL workers for simple nodeSelector.
P16
🔥 CATEGORY 2: NETWORK POLICY — Issue P16
Host Firewall Blocks Inter-Node Cilium Communication

🔍 Symptom: After host firewall CCNP, Cilium agents can't communicate. Health status fails. Cross-node broken.

🔴 5 Most Likely Causes
  1. Tunnel port blocked: UDP 8472 (VXLAN) or 6081 (Geneve) must be open between ALL nodes.
  2. ICMP blocked: Health checks use ICMP. Must allow from remote-node entity.
  3. WireGuard port blocked: UDP 51871 for encryption. Open between all nodes.
  4. Hubble peer blocked: Port 4244 for Hubble relay. Blocking breaks flow aggregation.
  5. etcd blocked: TCP 2379 to external etcd if used.
🟡 5 Less Likely Causes
  1. NodePort inter-node forwarding blocked
  2. Agent API port 9090 blocked
  3. SNAT/masquerade traffic blocked
  4. DNS for node hostnames blocked
  5. ClusterIP traffic blocked at host level
🟣 5 New Cluster Causes
